# Business Analytics and AI Application (Course 6141)

## Course Overview

Students in this course will develop a comprehensive understanding of business analytics and artificial intelligence fundamentals, covering topics such as business requirement analysis, data collection, and machine learning. Designed specifically for undergraduate and Master of Business Administration (MBA) students with zero prior programming background, this course emphasizes intuitive, evidence-based reasoning over abstract theoretical complexity.

Through hands-on exercises, students gain operational proficiency in Python programming, progressing from core syntax to intermediate techniques essential for enterprise data projects. Students acquire data manipulation skills using foundational libraries including NumPy and Pandas for data cleaning, aggregation, and financial visualization. They also master automated web data extraction techniques using HyperText Markup Language (HTML), Cascading Style Sheets (CSS), and Beautiful Soup to collect public competitor intelligence. 

With an introduction to modern machine learning tools through Scikit-Learn, participants learn to implement, interpret, and evaluate predictive models. Ethical considerations in artificial intelligence projects, algorithmic risk management, and governance frameworks are examined to ensure the responsible application of these analytical technologies.

## Development Environment and AI Error Protocol

All sessions use Google Antigravity IDE with four workspace panels: `[1] File Explorer`, `[2] Code Editor`, `[3] AI Agent Chat`, and `[4] Integrated Terminal`. When an error occurs, students identify the error type in Panel `[4]`, capture the traceback with `Windows Key + Shift + S` or `Command + Shift + 4`, paste it into Panel `[3]`, review the explanation, apply the verified fix in Panel `[2]`, and confirm an error-free execution with return code 0. Session 1.1 provides the complete orientation; every later lab applies the same workflow.

## Module 1: Foundations of Business Analytics and Python Programming

Module 1 establishes the computational foundation for business students. Students learn to translate executive business questions into structured quantitative workflows, set up a standardized Python execution environment, master variables and arithmetic for commercial metrics, control program flow, and inspect tabular corporate filings using Pandas.

<a id="session-01-01"></a>
### Session 1.1: Business Analytics Architecture, Google Antigravity IDE Initialization, and Core Arithmetic

![Session 1.1 Summary Slide](assets/session_01_01_slide.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_01_01_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_01_01_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 1 (115/09/13 - 115/09/19) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **Session Purpose:**
  * Set up your Python workspace
  * Replace spreadsheet cell formulas with named variables
  * Calculate real business metrics in code
* **Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. Run a script in Google Antigravity IDE
  2. Compute operating expense and margin with variables
  3. Fix a NameError or SyntaxError with AI Chat

* **Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `Python Interpreter`: The computational engine that reads Python source code and executes instructions sequentially line-by-line in real time without requiring compilation.
  * `Variable Assignment`: Storing data in computer memory using an assignment operator (`=`) and an intuitive descriptive label (such as `net_sales = 574785.0`), replacing fragile cell coordinates like `C13`.
  * `Primitive Data Types`: The foundational categories of information processed by Python: whole numbers (`int`), decimal numbers (`float`), and text strings (`str`).
  * `Traceback & Exception`: The automatic diagnostic navigation report generated by Python when an instruction violates a syntax or runtime rule, pinpointing the exact file and line number.
  * `Integrated Development Environment (IDE)`: A unified software workstation (Google Antigravity IDE) providing an editor, execution terminal, file explorer, and integrated AI assistant in a single interface.

* **Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * Sequential Execution & Program State: Python executes code strictly line-by-line from top to bottom. Unlike spreadsheets where formulas across sheets can trigger circular reference loops, Python code maintains a clear, linear flow where each statement computes or alters memory state sequentially.
  * Cell Coordinate Formulas vs. Descriptive Variables: Transitioning from Excel spatial grid thinking (such as `=C13-E13`) to self-documenting computational recipes (`operating_expense = net_sales - operating_income`). Code variables retain clear business meaning and eliminate silent formula drift.
  * Interpreter Diagnostics & Traceback Mechanics: Python acts as an interactive assistant. When a syntax or naming rule is violated, Python pauses and outputs a Traceback pointing to the exact line number and cause, turning errors into immediate learning signals rather than system failures.

* **Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Variable Arithmetic):* Defining commercial numbers (`net_sales = 574785.0`, `operating_income = 36852.0`) and calculating Operating Expense and Operating Margin Percentage with standard math operators (`-`, `/`, `*`).
  * *Micro-Example 2 (Dynamic Parameter Reusability):* Changing a single baseline assumption (such as revising sales tax or discounting prices by 5%) and observing how Python recalculates all downstream figures instantaneously without manual cell dragging.
  * *Micro-Example 3 (Traceback Troubleshooting & AI Fix):* Creating an intentional variable typo (`netsales` instead of `net_sales`), observing the `NameError` in the terminal, capturing it with `Windows Key + Shift + S`, and pasting into the AI chat to verify how the co-pilot identifies the typo and supplies the correction.

* **Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * File Link: [data/session_01_01_dataset.csv](data/session_01_01_dataset.csv)
  * Simple 4-row tabular financial dataset used to verify calculations:
    * `Segment_Name`: North America, International, AWS, Consolidated Total.
    * `Net_Sales_USD_M`: Revenue in millions of U.S. Dollars.
    * `Operating_Income_USD_M`: Operating income in millions of U.S. Dollars.

* **Official Python Documentation & References:**
  * [Python 3.12 Official Tutorial: Using Python as an Interactive Calculator](https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/introduction.html#using-python-as-a-calculator).
  * [PEP 8: Style Guide for Python Code (Readability and Variable Naming Standards)](https://peps.python.org/pep-0008/).
  * [Google Antigravity IDE Quickstart: Workspace Panels and AI Pair Programming](https://antigravity.google/docs).

* **Integrated Development Environment Architecture (Google Antigravity IDE):**
  * Students use Google Antigravity IDE, a modern AI-powered development environment designed for business analytics, financial modeling, and collaborative data science.
  
  ![Google Antigravity IDE Interface](assets/antigravity_ide_interface.png)

  * **The 4 Workspace Panels:**
    * `[1] File Explorer (Left Panel):` Workspace file hierarchy. Used to organize corporate datasets (`data/`), slide decks (`slides/`), Python scripts (`.py`), and curriculum documentation (`.md`).
    * `[2] Code Editor & Notebook Canvas (Center Top):` Primary workspace for writing Python code, defining financial variables, and executing arithmetic business formulas.
    * `[3] AI Agent Chat Canvas (Right Panel):` An interactive AI pair-programming assistant powered by Gemini. Students paste code snippets or error screenshots directly into this window to receive plain-English root cause explanations and verified code corrections.
    * `[4] Integrated Terminal & Console (Center Bottom):` Command line interface running the Python execution kernel. Displays script output results and detailed system error tracebacks.

  * **AI Error Diagnosis Protocol (Capture and Resolve in Seconds):**
    * *Zero Fear Principle:* In Python, an error is not a system failure or a test penalty. It is simply a diagnostic navigation clue (Traceback) showing the exact line number and rule violation.
    * *Step A (Identify):* When code execution stops, look at the last line in Panel `[4]` (Terminal), which names the error type (e.g., `NameError`, `SyntaxError`, `TypeError`).
    * *Step B (Capture):* Take a fast screen capture of the error message using `Windows Key + Shift + S` (Windows) or `Command + Shift + 4` (macOS).
    * *Step C (Paste & Prompt):* Click inside Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat), press `Ctrl + V` to attach the captured image, and ask: *"Why did this error happen, and what is the exact corrected code?"*
    * *Step D (Apply):* Review the AI explanation, understand the typo or missing parenthesis, and copy the verified fix back into Panel `[2]`.

* **Step-by-Step Lab Protocol (Exercise 1: Foundational Practice):**
  * *Step 1: Open Environment & Dataset:* Launch Google Antigravity IDE. Place `data/session_01_01_dataset.csv` in your working directory and open the raw file via Panel `[1]` (File Explorer) to observe comma-separated tabular records.
  * *Step 2: Inspect Primary Sources & Financial Terminology:* Confirm against Note 15 of Amazon's Form 10-K that Consolidated Net Sales for FY2023 equaled `$574,785` million and Operating Income equaled `$36,852` million.
  * *Step 3: Define Variables and Execute First Python Calculations:*
    In your first Python notebook cell, type the following arithmetic statements and run the cell:
    ```python
    # Amazon Consolidated Financial Metrics for FY2023 (in USD Millions)
    net_sales = 574785.0
    operating_income = 36852.0

    # Calculate Operating Expenses
    operating_expense = net_sales - operating_income

    # Calculate Operating Margin Percentage
    operating_margin_pct = (operating_income / net_sales) * 100.0

    print("Operating Expense (USD M):", operating_expense)
    print("Operating Margin (%):", round(operating_margin_pct, 2))
    ```
  * *Step 4: Bridge Excel Spreadsheet Mechanics to Python Code:*
    * In Microsoft Excel, if `Net_Sales` is in cell `C13` (`574785.00`) and `Operating_Income` is in cell `E13` (`36852.00`), the expense formula in cell `D13` is `=C13-E13`, and the margin formula in cell `F13` is `=(E13/C13)*100`.
    * Notice that Python variable assignment (`operating_income / net_sales`) performs the identical mathematical operation as cell references, but uses memorable descriptive English words instead of abstract grid coordinates like `E13`.
  * *Step 5: Compare Segment Profitability:*
    Execute a comparative calculation for Amazon Web Services (AWS) in FY2023:
    ```python
    aws_sales = 90757.0
    aws_income = 24631.0
    aws_margin_pct = (aws_income / aws_sales) * 100.0
    print("AWS Operating Margin (%):", round(aws_margin_pct, 2))
    ```
    Confirm that the AWS operating margin (`27.14%`) is over four times higher than the consolidated corporate operating margin (`6.41%`), demonstrating how cloud infrastructure drives corporate operating cash flow.

* **Step-by-Step Applied Coding Practice (Exercise 2+: Hands-On Programming & Debugging):**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `session_1_1_practice.py` to calculate financial metrics across multiple business units and practice error debugging.
  * *Task 1 (Segment Margin Calculation):* Define variables for Amazon Web Services (AWS) using FY2023 figures (`aws_sales = 90757.0`, `aws_income = 24631.0`). Compute `aws_margin_pct` and print the formatted result with `round(..., 2)`.
  * *Task 2 (Intentional Syntax Error & AI Diagnosis):* Deliberately omit the closing parenthesis in your `print()` statement (e.g., `print("AWS Margin:", round(aws_margin_pct, 2)`). Run the script in Panel `[4]` (Terminal), observe the `SyntaxError`, capture the terminal message using `Windows Key + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and verify the AI's explanation and fix.
  * *Task 3 (Multi-Unit Comparison Output):* Combine consolidated and cloud margin calculations into a single clean script that prints both metrics formatted with labels. Verify that the script executes with return code 0.

<a id="session-01-02"></a>
### Session 1.2: Essential Python Syntax Completion: Control Flow, Collections, and Business Automation

![Session 1.2 Summary Slide](assets/session_01_02_slide.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_01_02_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_01_02_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 2 (115/09/20 - 115/09/26) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **Session Purpose:**
  * Turn written business rules into Python logic
  * Let code decide and repeat automatically
  * Stop copying formulas row by row
* **Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. Apply tiered rules with if / elif / else
  2. Process client records with lists, dictionaries, and loops
  3. Fix an IndentationError in a full script

* **Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `Boolean Expression`: A logical comparison that evaluates strictly to `True` or `False`, used by computers to test commercial conditions (such as `annual_spend >= 50000.0`).
  * `Conditional Branching (else)`: Directing code execution down distinct logical pathways depending on operational thresholds, eliminating complex spreadsheet nested logic.
  * `List Collection`: An ordered, mutable sequence of business records enclosed in square brackets (e.g., `daily_orders = [120, 85, 94, 142]`), indexed sequentially starting from zero.
  * `Dictionary Collection`: A key-value associative data structure enclosed in curly braces (e.g., `{"client_id": "CUST-101", "annual_spend_USD": 145000.0}`), enabling instant data retrieval by descriptive business keys rather than numeric indices.
  * `Definite Iteration (for loop)`: A programmatic control structure that automatically iterates over every record in a collection, executing identical business rules across thousands of entries in milliseconds.

* **Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * Eliminating Fragile Nested Spreadsheets: In Excel, multi-tier decision policies require nested formulas such as `=IF(C2>100000, "Platinum", IF(C2>50000, "Gold", IF(C2>20000, "Silver", "Standard")))`. These formulas quickly become unreadable, impossible to audit, and vulnerable to formula drift. In Python, conditional branching (`if-elif-else`) structures business rules into clean vertical indentation blocks that mirror managerial decision trees.
  * Deterministic Repetition over Human Fatigue: When processing transactional ledgers with thousands of rows, human analysts experience fatigue, eye strain, and copy-paste errors. A Python `for` loop executes repetitive calculations across 10 records or 1,000,000 records with identical mathematical precision and zero cognitive degradation.
  * Real-World Entity Modeling with Dictionaries: Modeling complex commercial entities as structured computational objects. A single customer account is captured as a dictionary of labeled attributes (name, industry, spend, risk score), while an enterprise client portfolio is structured as an iterable list of dictionaries.
  * Indentation as Syntax Structure (The Whitespace Discipline): Unlike older programming languages that use curly brackets `{}` or `BEGIN/END` tokens, Python enforces clean code formatting through whitespace indentation (4 spaces). An `IndentationError` occurs when code blocks are misaligned, serving as an automated guardrail for code readability.

* **Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Conditional Customer Tiering):*
    Evaluating client annual spend to determine customer loyalty tiers and discount rates:
    ```python
    annual_spend = 82000.0

    if annual_spend >= 100000.0:
        tier = "Platinum"
        discount_rate = 0.15
    elif annual_spend >= 50000.0:
        tier = "Gold"
        discount_rate = 0.10
    elif annual_spend >= 20000.0:
        tier = "Silver"
        discount_rate = 0.05
    else:
        tier = "Standard"
        discount_rate = 0.0

    print(f"Customer Tier: {tier} | Approved Discount: {discount_rate * 100:.0f}%")
    ```
  * *Micro-Example 2 (Iterative Revenue Accumulation):*
    Looping through a list of daily store sales to calculate cumulative revenue and average daily performance:
    ```python
    daily_sales = [4200.0, 5100.0, 3900.0, 6200.0, 7800.0, 8400.0, 4900.0]
    total_revenue = 0.0

    for sale in daily_sales:
        total_revenue += sale

    avg_daily_sales = total_revenue / len(daily_sales)
    print("Total Weekly Revenue (USD):", round(total_revenue, 2))
    print("Average Daily Sales (USD):", round(avg_daily_sales, 2))
    ```
  * *Micro-Example 3 (Dictionary Client Profile & Safe Retrieval):*
    Storing commercial account attributes in a dictionary and computing available credit limits:
    ```python
    client = {
        "client_id": "CUST-104",
        "company_name": "Apex Logistics",
        "annual_spend_USD": 215000.0,
        "credit_limit_USD": 75000.0,
        "outstanding_balance_USD": 23400.0
    }

    available_credit = client["credit_limit_USD"] - client["outstanding_balance_USD"]
    print(f"{client['company_name']} Available Credit: ${available_credit:,.2f}")
    ```

* **Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * File Link: [data/session_01_02_dataset.csv](data/session_01_02_dataset.csv)
  * Standard 10-row commercial client dataset used for batch automation and loop exercises:
    * `Customer_ID`: Unique client identification code (e.g., `CUST-101`).
    * `Industry_Segment`: Commercial vertical (Retail, Technology, Healthcare, Finance, Manufacturing).
    * `Annual_Spend_USD`: Total historical purchase volume in U.S. Dollars.
    * `Order_Count`: Total orders completed in the fiscal year.
    * `Avg_Order_Value_USD`: Average transaction size in U.S. Dollars.
    * `Payment_Terms_Days_Count`: Invoiced credit settlement period (30, 60, 90 days).
    * `Risk_Score_Count`: Internal risk assessment index (1 to 100).

* **Official Python Documentation & References:**
  * [Python 3.12 Official Tutorial: More Control Flow Tools (if Statements and for Loops)](https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/controlflow.html).
  * [Python 3.12 Official Tutorial: Data Structures (Lists, Dictionaries, and Tuples)](https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/datastructures.html).
  * [PEP 8: Style Guide for Python Code (Indentation and Whitespace Standards)](https://peps.python.org/pep-0008/#indentation).

* **Step-by-Step Lab Protocol (Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Client Portfolio Automation):**
  * *Step 1: Create Practice File:* Launch Google Antigravity IDE and create a new script named `session_1_2_automation.py` in your working directory.
  * *Step 2: Build a Client Portfolio List:*
    Define an iterable list of customer dictionaries containing audited client records from `data/session_01_02_dataset.csv`:
    ```python
    clients = [
        {"id": "CUST-101", "segment": "Retail", "spend": 145000.0, "risk": 15},
        {"id": "CUST-102", "segment": "Technology", "spend": 82000.0, "risk": 22},
        {"id": "CUST-103", "segment": "Healthcare", "spend": 31000.0, "risk": 38},
        {"id": "CUST-104", "segment": "Finance", "spend": 215000.0, "risk": 12},
        {"id": "CUST-105", "segment": "Manufacturing", "spend": 64000.0, "risk": 45}
    ]
    ```
  * *Step 3: Automate Tier Assignment and Credit Multiplier via for Loop:*
    Write a `for` loop that iterates over each client, applies conditional branching based on `spend`, and computes an approved credit limit:
    ```python
    for c in clients:
        # Tiering logic based on annual purchase volume
        if c["spend"] >= 100000.0 and c["risk"] < 30:
            c["tier"] = "Platinum"
            c["credit_limit"] = c["spend"] * 0.40
        elif c["spend"] >= 50000.0 and c["risk"] < 50:
            c["tier"] = "Gold"
            c["credit_limit"] = c["spend"] * 0.25
        else:
            c["tier"] = "Standard"
            c["credit_limit"] = c["spend"] * 0.10

        print(f"Client {c['id']} ({c['segment']}): Tier={c['tier']} | Credit Limit=${c['credit_limit']:,.2f}")
    ```
  * *Step 4: Execute and Verify Output in Terminal:*
    Run the script in Panel `[4]` (Terminal) using `$ python session_1_2_automation.py`. Confirm that all 5 client accounts are evaluated and printed with proper credit limits.

* **Step-by-Step Applied Coding Practice (Exercise 2+: Hands-On Programming & AI Debugging):**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `session_1_2_practice.py` to automate a multi-tier commercial commission payout pipeline.
  * *Task 1 (Tiered Commission Functionality):*
    A sales team records quarterly sales volumes: `sales_reps = [{"name": "Alice", "revenue": 142000.0}, {"name": "Bob", "revenue": 68000.0}, {"name": "Charlie", "revenue": 35000.0}]`. Write a loop calculating commission payouts: `12%` for revenue over `$100,000`, `8%` for revenue between `$50,000` and `$100,000`, and `5%` for revenue below `$50,000`.
  * *Task 2 (Deliberate IndentationError & AI Diagnosis):*
    Intentionally misalign one line inside the `if` block (e.g., adding 2 extra spaces or removing indentation). Run the script in Panel `[4]` (Terminal), observe the red `IndentationError: unexpected indent`, capture the terminal traceback with `Windows Key + Shift + S`, paste the image into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and review how the AI explains whitespace alignment.
  * *Task 3 (Dictionary Summary Output):*
    Correct the indentation, append the calculated `commission_payout` back into each rep's dictionary, and print a formatted compensation summary with zero syntax errors. Verify that the script terminates with return code 0.


<a id="session-01-03"></a>
### Session 1.3: Tabular Data Wrangling Part 1: NumPy Arrays, Pandas DataFrames, and CSV Ingestion

![Session 1.3 Map](assets/session_01_03_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_01_03_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_01_03_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 3 (115/09/27 - 115/10/03) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **1. Session Purpose:**
  * Business systems (sales registers, accounting, customer databases, Excel) share tables as Comma-Separated Values (CSV) files
  * Our case: a retail chain exports monthly results for its 12 stores as a CSV file
  * Each row holds one store's sales, costs, visitors, and customer rating
  * Your manager asks: which stores are most profitable, and which meet the target? Today, Python answers these questions for every store at once

* **2. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "Did the store data load correctly?": read and inspect a `DataFrame` (a table in Python, like one Excel sheet)
  2. "What is each store's profit margin?": create new columns with vectorized math (one formula runs on every row at once)
  3. "Which stores meet our target?": filter rows with `.loc[]` (keep rows that match a condition) and fix a `KeyError` (the column name does not exist)

* **3. Business Context: Why CSV?**
  * Where business data comes from: sales registers, the accounting system, the customer database, and Excel reports. Each system can export its tables as one shared file.
  * `.xlsx` (Excel file) vs `.csv` (CSV file):

    | | `.xlsx` Excel file | `.csv` CSV file |
    |---|---|---|
    | Format | Packed format | Plain text |
    | What you need to open it | A spreadsheet program such as Excel | Almost anything: Notepad, Excel, Google Sheets, Python `pandas`, AI assistants |
    | Opened in Notepad | Unreadable symbols (starts with `PK`) | Readable: `Store_ID,Region_Name,Monthly_Sales_USD` |

  * Trade-off: CSV keeps only values, with no formulas and no formatting.

* **4. Data Basics: Rows and Columns:**
  * **Row** (read across): one record. Here, all the facts about one store, such as row `1` = everything about `STR-1001`.
  * **Column** (read down): one type of information for every store, such as `Monthly_Sales_USD`. In pandas, one column is a `Series`.
  * **Cell:** one value where a row and a column meet, such as `185000.0`.
  * Size of a table: `df.shape` returns `(12, 6)` = 12 rows, 6 columns. Row labels (the `index`) start at `0`, not 1.

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * File Link: [data/session_01_03_dataset.csv](data/session_01_03_dataset.csv)
  * Audited 12-row commercial store dataset used for vector arithmetic and filtering:
    * `Store_ID`: Unique retail location code (e.g., `STR-1001`).
    * `Region_Name`: Geographic division (East, West, North, South).
    * `Monthly_Sales_USD`: Gross store revenue in U.S. Dollars.
    * `Operating_Cost_USD`: Monthly operational store overhead in U.S. Dollars.
    * `Customer_Footfall_Count`: Total monthly foot traffic count.
    * `Customer_Satisfaction_Score`: Customer review rating (1.0 to 5.0 scale).

* **6. Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `pandas` and `NumPy`:
    * What: free Python libraries for tables (`pandas`) and fast number arrays (`NumPy`)
    * Why: they sort, filter, and calculate tables of any size
  * `CSV file` and `pd.read_csv()`:
    * What: a plain-text table where commas separate values; `pd.read_csv()` loads it
    * Why: most business systems export data as CSV
  * `DataFrame`:
    * What: a whole table in Python, with row labels and column names
    * Why: it is the main object you clean, calculate, and filter
  * `Series`:
    * What: one column of a DataFrame, such as `df["Monthly_Sales_USD"]`
    * Why: most calculations work column by column
  * `Vectorized Computation`:
    * What: one formula applied to every row at once, with no loop
    * Why: fast, and nothing to drag down row by row
  * `.loc[]` vs `.iloc[]`:
    * What: `.loc[]` selects by label or condition; `.iloc[]` selects by position number
    * Why: pull out exactly the rows a question needs
    * Example: `df.loc[df["Region_Name"] == "West"]` keeps the West rows; `df.iloc[0:2]` keeps rows 0 and 1 (`0:2` starts at 0 and stops **before** 2)

* **7. Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * **7.1 Columns, not cells: one formula for every row**
    * *The idea:* In Excel, a formula lives in one cell. To calculate the margin of 12 stores, you type the formula once and drag it down, which creates 12 separate copies, one per row. In pandas, a calculation is written once for a whole column, and pandas applies it to every row at the same time. This is called vectorized computation: the formula works on the whole column (a "vector" of values) instead of cell by cell.
    * *See it on real data:* operating margin for the first 3 stores.

      | | Excel | pandas |
      |---|---|---|
      | What you write | `=(C2-D2)/C2*100` in `E2`, then drag down to `E13` | `df["Operating_Margin_Pct"] = (df["Monthly_Sales_USD"] - df["Operating_Cost_USD"]) / df["Monthly_Sales_USD"] * 100` |
      | How many formulas exist | 12, one per row | 1, for the whole column |
      | `STR-1000` | `30.99` | `30.985915` |
      | `STR-1001` | `34.05` | `34.054054` |
      | `STR-1002` | `27.55` | `27.551020` |

    * *Common mistake (in Excel):* someone types a number over `E5`, for example to "fix" a value for a meeting. That row's formula is gone, the cell still looks normal, and next month's report is wrong without any warning sign that catches the eye. In pandas there is no per-row copy to overwrite: the next run recalculates every row from the data.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `df["Monthly_Sales_USD"] - df["Operating_Cost_USD"]` | `44000.0` for `STR-1000` | operating profit for every store |
      | `df["Monthly_Sales_USD"] / df["Customer_Footfall_Count"]` | `11.45` for `STR-1000` | sales per visitor: what an average visitor spends |
      | `df["Operating_Cost_USD"] / df["Monthly_Sales_USD"] * 100` | `69.01` for `STR-1000` | cost as a share of sales, in percent |
      | `df["Monthly_Sales_USD"] * 1.05` | `149100.0` for `STR-1000` | a "what if sales grew 5%?" scenario |
      | `df["Monthly_Sales_USD"] / 1000` | `142.0` for `STR-1000` | sales in thousands, easier to read in a report |

    * *Remember:* write the rule once for the column; pandas applies it to every row.
  * **7.2 Inside a DataFrame: index, columns, and values**
    * *The idea:* A DataFrame has three parts, like a labeled spreadsheet:
      * the **index**: one label per row, shown at the left. By default the labels are `0, 1, 2, ...`, counting from `0`, not `1`;
      * the **columns**: one name per column, shown at the top, such as `Store_ID`;
      * the **values**: the data itself, stored as a NumPy array (a fast block of numbers that pandas uses underneath).
    * *See it on real data:* the first 3 rows as pandas prints them.

      ```text
         Store_ID Region_Name  Monthly_Sales_USD  ...
      0  STR-1000        East           142000.0  ...
      1  STR-1001        West           185000.0  ...
      2  STR-1002       North            98000.0  ...
      ```

      The `0`, `1`, `2` on the left are the index, not data. `STR-1001` is in the row labeled `1`, which is the **second** row.
    * *One column vs a smaller table:* `df["Store_ID"]` (single brackets) returns one column, a `Series`. `df[["Store_ID", "Region_Name"]]` (double brackets: a list inside the brackets) returns a smaller `DataFrame` with the listed columns.
    * *Common mistake:* counting rows from 1. In Python, the first row has position `0`, so "the third row" is position `2`.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `type(df["Store_ID"])` | `Series` | single brackets give one column |
      | `df[["Store_ID", "Region_Name"]].shape` | `(12, 2)` | double brackets give a smaller table: 12 rows, 2 columns |
      | `df.columns.tolist()` | `['Store_ID', 'Region_Name', ...]` | the exact column names, to copy instead of typing |
      | `df.index.tolist()` | `[0, 1, 2, ..., 11]` | the row labels start at `0` |
      | `len(df)` | `12` | the number of rows |
      | `df.loc[0, "Store_ID"]` | `'STR-1000'` | one value: row label `0`, column `Store_ID` |

    * *Remember:* index on the left, column names on top, values in between; counting starts at `0`.
  * **7.3 Data types: every column has exactly one type**
    * *The idea:* pandas gives each column one data type (`dtype`), and every value in that column follows it. The type decides what you can do with the column: you can add numbers, but not text.

      | `dtype` | Meaning | Columns in our data |
      |---|---|---|
      | `float64` | decimal numbers | `Monthly_Sales_USD`, `Operating_Cost_USD`, `Customer_Satisfaction_Score` |
      | `int64` | whole numbers | `Customer_Footfall_Count` |
      | `str` | text (older pandas versions show `object`) | `Store_ID`, `Region_Name` |

    * *Why it matters:* if a number is typed with a comma, such as `"1,200"`, pandas cannot read it as a number, so the whole column becomes text. Adding text joins the pieces instead of summing them: a column with `"1,200"` and `"800"` "sums" to `"1,200800"`, not `2000`. In Excel the same value would sit in its cell as text and be quietly skipped by `SUM`.
    * *How to catch it:* run `df.dtypes` right after loading. A sales column showing `str` instead of `float64` means some values were stored as text.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `df["Monthly_Sales_USD"].sum()` | `1829000.0` | a number column adds up as numbers |
      | `df["Region_Name"].head(3).sum()` | `'EastWestNorth'` | a text column "adds up" by joining the words |
      | `pd.Series(["1,200", "800"]).sum()` | `'1,200800'` | numbers stored as text are joined, not summed |
      | `pd.Series(["1,200", "800"]).str.replace(",", "").astype(float).sum()` | `2000.0` | remove the comma, convert to `float`, and the sum is right |
      | `df["Customer_Footfall_Count"].mean()` | `13075.0` | an `int64` column works for averages too |

    * *Remember:* check the types first; a number column must show `float64` or `int64`.
  * **7.4 Check before you calculate: four inspection tools**
    * *The idea:* A professional analyst never calculates on data they have not looked at. Four commands answer the first questions about any new file:

      | Command | Question it answers | Result for our file |
      |---|---|---|
      | `df.shape` | How big is the table? | `(12, 6)`: 12 rows, 6 columns |
      | `df.head(3)` | What do the first rows look like? | `STR-1000`, `STR-1001`, `STR-1002` |
      | `df.info()` | What are the types, and are values missing? | `12 non-null` in every column: no gaps |
      | `df.describe()` | What are the typical, smallest, and largest values? | sales: mean `152416.67`, min `89000.0`, max `225000.0` |

    * *Common mistake:* `df.shape` has no brackets, because it is a stored value (an attribute). `df.head()` and `df.info()` do have brackets, because they are actions (methods). Writing `df.shape()` raises `TypeError: 'tuple' object is not callable`.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `df["Region_Name"].value_counts()` | `East 3`, `West 3`, `North 3`, `South 3` | how many stores each region has |
      | `df.isnull().sum().sum()` | `0` | the number of empty cells in the whole table |
      | `df["Customer_Satisfaction_Score"].min()` | `3.8` | the lowest rating |
      | `df["Customer_Satisfaction_Score"].max()` | `4.9` | the highest rating |
      | `df["Customer_Satisfaction_Score"].mean()` | `4.35` (rounded) | the average rating |

    * *Remember:* shape, head, info, describe: look first, calculate second.
  * **7.5 Picking rows: by label with `.loc[]`, by position with `.iloc[]`**
    * *The idea:* There are two ways to point at a row. `.loc[]` uses the **label** in the index, or a condition. `.iloc[]` uses the **position**, counting from `0`. On a freshly loaded file the labels and positions are the same, so the two look alike. They differ as soon as the rows are reordered.
    * *See it on real data:* sort the stores by sales, from lowest to highest. The row order changes, but every row keeps its label.

      | Request | Meaning | Result |
      |---|---|---|
      | `sorted_df.loc[1, "Store_ID"]` | the row whose label is `1` | `STR-1001`, wherever it now sits |
      | `sorted_df.iloc[0]["Store_ID"]` | whatever row is now first | `STR-1006`, the lowest sales |

    * *Filtering with `.loc[]`:* `.loc[]` also accepts a condition, such as `df.loc[df["Region_Name"] == "West"]`, and keeps only the rows where the condition is `True`. This is the form you will use most.
    * *Common mistake:* misspelling a column name. `df["Monthly_Sale_USD"]` (missing `s`) raises `KeyError: 'Monthly_Sale_USD'`: pandas looked for a column with that exact name and found none. Copy column names from `df.columns` instead of typing them.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `df.loc[df["Region_Name"] == "East", "Store_ID"]` | `STR-1000`, `STR-1004`, `STR-1008` | the East stores: a condition plus one column |
      | `df.loc[df["Customer_Satisfaction_Score"] >= 4.5, "Store_ID"]` | `STR-1001`, `STR-1004`, `STR-1005`, `STR-1008`, `STR-1011` | the 5 best-rated stores |
      | `df.iloc[0]["Store_ID"]` | `'STR-1000'` | the first row |
      | `df.iloc[-1]["Store_ID"]` | `'STR-1011'` | `-1` means the last row |
      | `df.iloc[0:3]["Store_ID"]` | `STR-1000`, `STR-1001`, `STR-1002` | rows at positions 0, 1, 2 (stops before 3) |
      | `df.loc[2, "Region_Name"]` | `'North'` | row label `2`, column by name |
      | `df.iloc[2, 1]` | `'North'` | the same cell by position: row 2, column 1 |

    * *Remember:* `.loc[]` = label or condition; `.iloc[]` = position from `0`.

* **8. Development Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE:**

  ![Google Antigravity IDE Interface](assets/antigravity_ide_interface.png)

  * `[1]` File Explorer: create your script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S13_demo_1.py`), and check the dataset is in `data/`.
  * `[2]` Code Editor: write a few lines, run, repeat. Small steps keep errors small.
  * `[3]` AI Agent Chat: on an error, capture the traceback (Python's error report) with `Win + Shift + S` (macOS: `Cmd + Shift + 4`), paste it with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why it failed.
  * `[4]` Terminal: run `python` followed by your script name. Return code `0` means the script finished with no errors.

* **9. Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * Every example has three parts: **1. Input** (load the data), **2. Process** (calculate or select), and **3. Output** (print the result).
  * Name each file with your own name and the session code, for example `Reacher_S13_demo_1.py` (pattern: `YourName_S13_demo_1.py`).
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Load a CSV File and Inspect It):* `YourName_S13_demo_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the tool and the data
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_01_03_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: measure the table and take a preview
    dimensions = df.shape
    column_types = df.dtypes
    preview = df.head(3)

    # 3. Output: show the results
    print("Dataset Dimensions (Rows, Columns):", dimensions)
    print("Column Data Types:\n", column_types)
    print(preview)
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `import pandas as pd` loads the pandas library and gives it the short name `pd`, so every pandas tool can be written as `pd.something`.
      * `pd.read_csv(...)` opens the CSV file, reads the first line as column names, and stores the table in a variable named `df` (short for DataFrame). The path `data/...` starts from the project folder.
      * `df.shape`, `df.dtypes`, and `df.head(3)` measure the table, list each column's type, and copy the first 3 rows. Each result is stored in its own variable, so part 3 can print it.
      * `print()` shows a result in the Terminal. A script that calculates without printing shows nothing at all.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Dataset Dimensions (Rows, Columns): (12, 6)
      Column Data Types:
       Store_ID                           str
      Region_Name                        str
      Monthly_Sales_USD              float64
      ...
         Store_ID Region_Name  ...  Customer_Footfall_Count  Customer_Satisfaction_Score
      0  STR-1000        East  ...                    12400                          4.2
      ```

      * `(12, 6)` means 12 rows (stores) and 6 columns, as expected for this file.
      * The money columns are `float64` (numbers), so you can calculate with them.
      * The `...` in the preview is not missing data: pandas hides middle columns when the table is wider than the Terminal.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`.tail()`):*
    * New tool: `.tail(n)` shows the last `n` rows. Why: new records are often added at the bottom.
    * Task: in part **2. Process**, change `preview = df.head(3)` to `preview = df.____(3)` so it shows the last 3 rows.
    * Check: the preview shows index `9`, `10`, `11` (`STR-1009`, `STR-1010`, `STR-1011`).
  * *Micro-Example 2 (Calculate New Columns Without a Loop):* `YourName_S13_demo_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the store data
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_01_03_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: profit and margin for every store at once
    df["Operating_Profit_USD"] = (
        df["Monthly_Sales_USD"] - df["Operating_Cost_USD"]
    )
    df["Operating_Margin_Pct"] = (
        df["Operating_Profit_USD"] / df["Monthly_Sales_USD"] * 100.0
    )

    # 3. Output: show the first three stores
    print(df[["Store_ID", "Monthly_Sales_USD",
              "Operating_Margin_Pct"]].head(3))
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `df["Monthly_Sales_USD"] - df["Operating_Cost_USD"]` subtracts two columns. pandas lines them up row by row, so each store's cost is subtracted from that same store's sales (section 7.1).
      * `df["Operating_Profit_USD"] = ...` stores the result as a new column. If the name does not exist yet, pandas adds it at the right end of the table.
      * The second calculation uses the new profit column straight away: profit / sales x 100.
      * The round brackets around each calculation only let the formula continue on the next line; they do not change the result.
      * `df[["Store_ID", "Monthly_Sales_USD", "Operating_Margin_Pct"]]` (double brackets) keeps just those 3 columns, and `.head(3)` keeps the first 3 rows.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
         Store_ID  Monthly_Sales_USD  Operating_Margin_Pct
      0  STR-1000           142000.0             30.985915
      1  STR-1001           185000.0             34.054054
      2  STR-1002            98000.0             27.551020
      ```

      * `STR-1000`: `(142000 - 98000) / 142000 x 100 = 30.99`%. Of every $100 of sales, about $31 is left as operating profit.
      * `STR-1002` sells the least and also keeps the smallest share as profit (`27.55`%).
      * The long decimals are correct but hard to read; Quick Check 2 rounds them.
  * *Quick Check 2 (`.round()`):*
    * New tool: `.round(n)` rounds every number in a column to `n` decimal places. Why: long decimals such as `30.985915` are hard to read in a report.
    * Task: add one line at the end of part **2. Process**: `df["Operating_Margin_Pct"] = df["Operating_Margin_Pct"].____(1)`
    * Check: the margins print as `31.0`, `34.1`, and `27.6`.
  * *Micro-Example 3 (Filter Rows and Fill Missing Values):* `YourName_S13_demo_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the store data
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_01_03_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: keep West stores with sales of $150,000+
    west_high_sales = df.loc[
        (df["Region_Name"] == "West")
        & (df["Monthly_Sales_USD"] >= 150000.0)
    ]
    #    then fill missing satisfaction scores with the median
    median_score = df["Customer_Satisfaction_Score"].median()
    df["Customer_Satisfaction_Score"] = (
        df["Customer_Satisfaction_Score"].fillna(median_score)
    )

    # 3. Output: show the filtered stores and the median
    print(west_high_sales[["Store_ID", "Monthly_Sales_USD"]])
    print("Median Satisfaction Score Imputed:", median_score)
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `(df["Region_Name"] == "West")` checks every row and gives `True` for West stores and `False` for the others. Note the double `==`: it compares, while a single `=` stores a value.
      * `(df["Monthly_Sales_USD"] >= 150000.0)` gives `True` for stores selling $150,000 or more.
      * `&` combines the two answers row by row: a row is `True` only if both are `True`. Each condition sits in its own brackets so that Python compares first and combines second.
      * `df.loc[...]` keeps the `True` rows and stores them in `west_high_sales`; the original `df` is not changed.
      * `.median()` finds the middle value of the satisfaction scores: half the stores score higher, half lower. `.fillna(median_score)` puts that value into any empty cell (`NaN`, "Not a Number").
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
         Store_ID  Monthly_Sales_USD
      1  STR-1001           185000.0
      5  STR-1005           175000.0
      9  STR-1009           160000.0
      Median Satisfaction Score Imputed: 4.35
      ```

      * All 3 West stores sell $150,000 or more, so all 3 pass. With a higher limit, such as $170,000, only 2 would. The index labels `1`, `5`, `9` show where each store sat in the original table: filtering keeps the labels.
      * The median of the 12 scores is `4.35`. This dataset has no empty scores, so `.fillna()` changes nothing today, but the same line protects the script when next month's file has gaps.
  * *Quick Check 3 (`len()`):*
    * New tool: `len()` counts items; for a DataFrame, it returns the number of rows. Why: it answers "how many?" questions in one step. Example: `len(df)` returns `12`.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print("Stores found:", ___(west_high_sales))`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `Stores found: 3`.

* **10. Official Python Documentation & References:**
  * [Pandas Official Documentation: 10 Minutes to Pandas](https://pandas.pydata.org/docs/user_guide/10min.html).
  * [Pandas Official Guide: Indexing and Selecting Data (.loc and .iloc)](https://pandas.pydata.org/docs/user_guide/indexing.html).
  * [NumPy Official Documentation: NumPy Quickstart Tutorial](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/user/quickstart.html).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Retail Performance Wrangling:**
  * *Step 1: Open Workspace:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S13_wrangling.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S13_wrangling.py`). Verify that `data/session_01_03_dataset.csv` is present in Panel `[1]`.
  * *Step 2: Load, Calculate, and Summarize:*
    Type the following Python statements:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the store data
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_01_03_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: add profit and margin columns, then summarize
    df["Operating_Profit_USD"] = (
        df["Monthly_Sales_USD"] - df["Operating_Cost_USD"]
    )
    df["Operating_Margin_Pct"] = (
        df["Operating_Profit_USD"] / df["Monthly_Sales_USD"] * 100.0
    )
    summary = df.describe()

    # 3. Output: show summary statistics for sales and margin
    print("Descriptive Financial Statistics:\n",
          summary[["Monthly_Sales_USD", "Operating_Margin_Pct"]])
    ```
    * `.describe()` returns summary statistics: count, mean, standard deviation, min, max, and quartiles.
  * *Step 3: Compare Excel Mechanics with Pandas:*
    * In Excel, you type `=(C2-D2)/C2*100` in cell `E2` and drag it down, so every row gets its own copy of the formula. If someone types a number over `E5`, that row is wrong, and the mistake is easy to miss.
    * In Pandas, one line of `df["Operating_Margin_Pct"]` code covers every row, so there is no copy to overwrite.
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:*
    Run the script via Panel `[4]` (Terminal) using `python YourName_S13_wrangling.py`. Confirm that the summary statistics are printed.

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Find the Top Stores:**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In `YourName_S13_practice.py`, find the top stores: `Operating_Margin_Pct >= 30.0` and `Customer_Satisfaction_Score >= 4.5`.
  * *Task 1 (Targeted Filtering):* Use `.loc[]` to select the qualified stores and output only `Store_ID`, `Region_Name`, and `Operating_Margin_Pct`.
  * *Task 2 (Deliberate KeyError & AI Diagnosis):* Deliberately misspell a column name (e.g., `df["Monthly_Sale_USD"]` instead of `"Monthly_Sales_USD"`). Read the `KeyError` (the column name does not exist), capture the traceback with `Win + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and review the AI's explanation.
  * *Task 3 (Clean CSV Export):* Correct the column name, then sort with `.sort_values()`; add `ascending=False` for the highest margin first. Save the table with `.to_csv()` to `data/top_performing_stores.csv`.
  * *Success Check:* the result has 5 stores, led by `STR-1011` (South, 36.89%), and the script ends with return code `0`.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - Store Scorecard:**
  * *Goal:* Build a one-page scorecard that sorts all 12 stores into three tiers, so the regional manager can see at a glance which stores to reward and which to watch.
  * *What you need:*
    * File: `data/session_01_03_dataset.csv` (12 rows, described in **5. Lab Practice Dataset**).
    * Script name: `YourName_S13_challenge.py`.
    * Tools you already know: `pd.read_csv()`, column math, `.round()`, `&`, `.loc[]`, `.sort_values()`, `len()`, and `.to_csv()`.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. Add `Operating_Profit_USD` = `Monthly_Sales_USD` - `Operating_Cost_USD`.
    2. Add `Operating_Margin_Pct` = `Operating_Profit_USD` / `Monthly_Sales_USD` x 100, rounded to 1 decimal place.
    3. Add `Sales_per_Visitor_USD` = `Monthly_Sales_USD` / `Customer_Footfall_Count`, rounded to 2 decimal places. It shows how much an average visitor spends.
    4. Add a `Tier` column using these rules, in this order:
       * Every store starts as `"Standard"`.
       * `"Gold"`: `Operating_Margin_Pct >= 33.0` **and** `Customer_Satisfaction_Score >= 4.5`.
       * `"Watch"`: `Operating_Margin_Pct < 30.0`.
    5. Print how many stores are in each tier, one line per tier, for example `Gold stores: 4`.
    6. Print all 12 stores sorted by `Operating_Margin_Pct`, highest first, showing only `Store_ID`, `Region_Name`, `Operating_Margin_Pct`, `Sales_per_Visitor_USD`, `Customer_Satisfaction_Score`, and `Tier`.
    7. Save the same sorted table to `data/store_scorecard.csv` without row labels.
    8. Answer in a comment at the end of your script: which tier holds the store with the highest `Sales_per_Visitor_USD`, and what does that tell the manager?
  * *New tool for this exercise: set a value for selected rows only:*
    * `df["Tier"] = "Standard"` writes one value into every row of a new column.
    * `df.loc[mask, "Tier"] = "Gold"` changes only the rows where `mask` is `True`. `mask` is a condition like the one in Micro-Example 3, with each part wrapped in `( )` and joined with `&`.
  * *Hints:*
    * To count a tier, filter first, then use `len()`: `len(df.loc[df["Tier"] == "Gold"])`.
    * Pass a list of column names to `df[[ ... ]]` to keep only the columns you need.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * `Gold stores: 4` (`STR-1011`, `STR-1004`, `STR-1008`, `STR-1001`), `Watch stores: 5`, `Standard stores: 3`.
    * The first row of the sorted table is `STR-1011` (South, `36.9`), and the last row is `STR-1006` (North, `23.6`).
    * The highest `Sales_per_Visitor_USD` is `STR-1002` (`11.95`), a **Watch** store with a margin of `27.6`. High spending per visitor does not guarantee a high profit margin.

<a id="session-01-04"></a>
### Session 1.4: Tabular Data Wrangling Part 2: Advanced Filtering, Multi-Segment GroupBy, and Financial Auditing

![Session 1.4 Map](assets/session_01_04_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_01_04_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_01_04_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 4 (115/10/04 - 115/10/10) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **1. Session Purpose:**
  * Each division gets a spending budget every quarter, and finance compares it with what was actually spent
  * Our case: 4 divisions x 4 quarters = 16 rows in one CSV file
  * The finance director asks: who overspent, by how much, and who is responsible? Today, Python answers this for the whole year at once

* **2. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "Which quarters went over budget?": filter rows with a Boolean mask (a `True`/`False` value for every row) and `&`
  2. "How much did each division overspend this year?": summarize groups with `groupby()` (split rows into groups, like an Excel pivot table, which is a summary table with one row per group) and `.agg()`
  3. "Who is responsible?": join tables with `pd.merge()` (join two tables on a shared column, like Excel's `VLOOKUP` formula, which looks up a value in another table) and fix a `KeyError`

* **3. Business Context: Budget vs Actual:**
  * Four terms, one example (Consumer division, Q1):

    | Term | Meaning | Example |
    |---|---|---|
    | Budget | planned spending | `780,000` |
    | Actual | real spending | `810,000` |
    | Variance | Actual - Budget | `+30,000` |
    | Variance % | Variance / Budget x 100 | `+3.85%` |

  * Positive variance = overspent; negative variance = under budget.
  * Cost center: the accounting code a division's spending is charged to, such as `CC-202` for Consumer. Each cost center has a responsible VP (Vice President).
  * Company rule in our data: a quarter is flagged (`Audit_Status` = `Variance_Flag`) when the variance is more than 2%.
  * Consumer division by quarter: Q1 `+3.85%` (flagged), Q2 `+1.92%` (over, within the rule), Q3 `+5.77%` (flagged), Q4 `-0.64%` (under budget).

* **4. Data Basics: From Excel Tools You Know to pandas:**
  * Three Excel tools, in one line each:
    * **Filter:** hides the rows that do not match a rule, such as showing only the Consumer division.
    * **Pivot Table:** summarizes a large table by group. For example, put `Division_Name` in Rows and `Actual_Expense_USD` in Values to get total spending per division: 4 rows instead of 16.
    * **`VLOOKUP`:** a formula that looks up a value, such as a cost center code, in another table and returns a value from the same row, such as the VP's name. You tell it which column to return by number (for example, the 2nd column).
  * The same three jobs in pandas:
    * Filter -> Boolean mask `df[mask]`: the rule is written in code, so it repeats exactly next quarter.
    * Pivot Table -> `df.groupby().agg()`: one line; run it again when new data arrives.
    * `VLOOKUP` -> `pd.merge()`: `VLOOKUP` returns a column by number; `pd.merge()` matches rows by column name, such as `Cost_Center_Code`.

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * File Link: [data/session_01_04_dataset.csv](data/session_01_04_dataset.csv)
  * Audited 16-row quarterly budget dataset (4 divisions x 4 quarters; each row is one division's spending in one quarter):
    * `Transaction_ID`: Unique record identifier (e.g., `TXN-5001`).
    * `Division_Name`: Operating business unit (Cloud, Consumer, Enterprise, Logistics).
    * `Cost_Center_Code`: Accounting cost center (CC-101, CC-202, CC-303, CC-404).
    * `Fiscal_Quarter`: Financial reporting quarter (Q1, Q2, Q3, Q4).
    * `Budget_Expense_USD`: Authorized spending budget in U.S. Dollars.
    * `Actual_Expense_USD`: Realized expenditure in U.S. Dollars.
    * `Audit_Status`: Internal audit classification (`Verified`, or `Variance_Flag` when spending is more than 2% over budget).

* **6. Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `Boolean Mask`:
    * What: one `True` or `False` per row
    * Why: picks exactly the rows that meet a rule
  * `&` `|` `~`:
    * What: "and", "or", "not" for masks
    * Why: Python's `and` / `or` fail on whole columns
  * `Split-Apply-Combine`:
    * What: split rows into groups, calculate each group, combine the results
    * Why: the idea behind every pivot table
  * `groupby()` + `.agg()`:
    * What: `groupby()` splits; `.agg()` sums, counts, or averages each group
    * Why: yearly totals per division in one step
  * `pd.merge()`:
    * What: joins two tables row by row
    * Why: adds facts from another table, such as the VP's name, with no copy-paste
  * `Key Column`:
    * What: a column both tables share, such as `Cost_Center_Code`
    * Why: tells pandas which rows belong together

* **7. Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * **7.1 Combining rules: `&`, not `and`**
    * *The idea:* A rule on a column, such as `df["Division_Name"] == "Consumer"`, does not give one answer. It gives one `True` or `False` **for every row**: a whole column of answers, called a Boolean mask. To combine two rules, Python must compare the two columns row by row: row 1 with row 1, row 2 with row 2, and so on. pandas uses `&` ("and"), `|` ("or"), and `~` ("not") for this row-by-row work.
    * *See it on real data:* the first 4 rows of our dataset.

      | Row | Division | `is_consumer` | `is_over` | Both true (`&`) | Either true (or) |
      |---|---|---|---|---|---|
      | `TXN-5000` | Cloud | `False` | `False` | `False` | `False` |
      | `TXN-5001` | Consumer | `True` | `True` | `True` | `True` |
      | `TXN-5002` | Enterprise | `False` | `False` | `False` | `False` |
      | `TXN-5003` | Logistics | `False` | `True` | `False` | `True` |

      Here `is_consumer = df["Division_Name"] == "Consumer"` and `is_over = df["Actual_Expense_USD"] > df["Budget_Expense_USD"]`. With `is_consumer & is_over`, a row is kept only when **both** columns say `True` (`TXN-5001`). With `is_consumer | is_over`, one `True` is enough (`TXN-5001` and `TXN-5003`).
    * *Common mistake 1, using `and`:* Python's own `and` can only judge one `True` or `False` at a time. Given a whole column, it does not know whether you mean "all rows", "any row", or "row by row", so it stops with `ValueError: The truth value of a Series is ambiguous`. "Ambiguous" means "it could mean several things".
    * *Common mistake 2, missing brackets:* Python works out `&` **before** `==` and `>`. So in `df["Division_Name"] == "Consumer" & df["Actual_Expense_USD"] > df["Budget_Expense_USD"]`, it first tries `"Consumer" & df["Actual_Expense_USD"]`, which makes no sense, and raises a `TypeError`. Brackets force the right order: `(df["Division_Name"] == "Consumer") & (df["Actual_Expense_USD"] > df["Budget_Expense_USD"])`.
    * In these examples, `is_over = df["Actual_Expense_USD"] > df["Budget_Expense_USD"]`.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `(df["Division_Name"] == "Logistics") & is_over` | `Q1`, `Q3` | the Logistics quarters that overspent |
      | `(df["Fiscal_Quarter"] == "Q4") & is_over` | `Enterprise` | only one division overspent in Q4 |
      | `(df["Audit_Status"] == "Variance_Flag") & (df["Fiscal_Quarter"] == "Q3")` | `TXN-5008`, `TXN-5009`, `TXN-5011` | the flagged quarters in Q3 |
      | `(~is_over).sum()` | `8` | `~` flips the rule: 8 quarters stayed within budget |
      | `df["Division_Name"].isin(["Cloud", "Enterprise"]).sum()` | `8` | `.isin()` is a short way to write "Cloud or Enterprise" |

    * *Remember:* one rule gives one `True` / `False` per row; combine rules with `&`, `|`, or `~`, and put each rule in its own brackets.
  * **7.2 Split-apply-combine: how `groupby()` builds a summary**
    * *The idea:* Most management questions are about groups: "How much did **each division** spend?" Answering them always takes the same three steps, whether in an Excel pivot table or in pandas:
      1. **Split** the rows into groups, one per division.
      2. **Apply** a calculation inside each group, such as a sum.
      3. **Combine** the answers into a new, smaller table with one row per group.
    * *See it on real data:* the Consumer division's 4 quarterly rows go into one group, and `"sum"` adds them up:

      | Step | Consumer division |
      |---|---|
      | Split | Q1 `810000.0`, Q2 `795000.0`, Q3 `825000.0`, Q4 `775000.0` |
      | Apply (`"sum"`) | `810000.0 + 795000.0 + 825000.0 + 775000.0 = 3205000.0` |
      | Combine | one row: `Consumer` `3205000.0` |

      The same steps run for Cloud, Enterprise, and Logistics at the same time, so the 16 rows become 4: Cloud `1785000.0`, Consumer `3205000.0`, Enterprise `1280000.0`, Logistics `2468000.0`.
    * *What you choose:* the column to split by (`groupby("Division_Name")`), the column to calculate on, and the calculation: `"sum"` for totals, `"mean"` for averages, `"count"` for how many rows, `"max"` for the largest value.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `df.groupby("Fiscal_Quarter")["Actual_Expense_USD"].sum()` | Q1 `2185000.0`, Q2 `2171000.0`, Q3 `2235000.0`, Q4 `2147000.0` | total spending per quarter: Q3 was the most expensive |
      | `df.groupby("Division_Name")["Transaction_ID"].count()` | `4` for each division | a quick check that no quarter is missing |
      | `df.groupby("Cost_Center_Code")["Actual_Expense_USD"].max()` | `CC-202` `825000.0` (largest) | the most expensive quarter of each cost center |
      | `df.groupby("Audit_Status")["Transaction_ID"].count()` | `Variance_Flag` `6`, `Verified` `10` | how many quarters were flagged |

    * *Remember:* `groupby()` does the split, `.agg()` does the apply, and pandas does the combine for you.
  * **7.3 Joining tables: match by name, not by position**
    * *The idea:* Information often sits in two tables: spending in one, the responsible VP in another. Joining means bringing the matching row from the second table next to each row of the first, using a column that both share: the key column, here `Cost_Center_Code`.
    * *Why Excel's `VLOOKUP` is fragile:* `VLOOKUP` is told to return "the 2nd column" of the lookup table. If someone later inserts a new column, such as `Region`, the 2nd column is now `Region`, and every formula quietly returns a region instead of a VP name. No error appears, so the mistake is easy to miss.
    * *Why `pd.merge()` is safer:* `pd.merge(spending, vp_info, on="Cost_Center_Code")` matches rows by the **name** of the key column and brings over every other column by name. Moving or inserting columns changes nothing.
    * In these examples, `ledger = pd.merge(df, vp_info, on="Cost_Center_Code", how="left")`, as in Micro-Example 3, and `vp_renamed` is `vp_info` with its key column renamed to `CostCenter`.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `ledger.shape` | `(16, 8)` | still 16 rows; one new column, `VP_Lead`, added (7 + 1) |
      | `ledger.loc[ledger["Division_Name"] == "Logistics", "VP_Lead"]` | `David Kim` in all 4 rows | one VP row is matched to every quarter of that cost center |
      | `pd.merge(df, vp_renamed, on="Cost_Center_Code")` | `KeyError: 'Cost_Center_Code'` | `vp_renamed` calls the key `CostCenter`: the names must match exactly |

    * *Remember:* name the key column with `on=`; the key must have exactly the same name and the same values in both tables.
  * **7.4 What happens to rows with no match: `how="left"` vs `how="inner"`**
    * *The idea:* Sometimes a row in the main table has no partner in the other table, for example a new cost center that has not been given a VP yet. The `how=` setting decides what happens to that row.
    * *See it on an example:* suppose our spending table also had a new cost center, `CC-505`, which is not in `vp_info`:

      | `Cost_Center_Code` | `Variance_USD` | `VP_Lead` with `how="left"` | with `how="inner"` |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | `CC-202` | `85000.0` | Marcus Vance | Marcus Vance |
      | `CC-404` | `28000.0` | David Kim | David Kim |
      | `CC-505` | `5000.0` | `NaN` (empty) | row removed |

      `NaN` stands for "Not a Number", pandas' mark for a missing value.
    * *Why it matters:* with `how="inner"`, the `CC-505` overspend silently disappears from the report, and nobody is asked to explain it. With `how="left"`, the row stays and the empty `VP_Lead` tells you that someone must be assigned.
    * In these examples, `vp_3_rows` is `vp_info` without the `CC-404` row (as if Logistics had no VP yet), and `merged` is the result of the left merge.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `pd.merge(df, vp_3_rows, on="Cost_Center_Code", how="left")` | `16` rows, `4` with `NaN` in `VP_Lead` | `vp_3_rows` lacks `CC-404`: its 4 quarters stay, with no VP |
      | `pd.merge(df, vp_3_rows, on="Cost_Center_Code", how="inner")` | `12` rows | the 4 Logistics quarters silently disappear |
      | `merged["VP_Lead"].isnull().sum()` | `4` | count the gaps after a left merge |

    * *Remember:* use `how="left"` when every row of your main table must stay in the report, then look for `NaN` to find the gaps.

* **8. Development Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE:**
  * `[1]` File Explorer: create your script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S14_demo_1.py`), and check the dataset is in `data/`.
  * `[2]` Code Editor: write a few lines, run, repeat. Small steps keep errors small.
  * `[3]` AI Agent Chat: on an error, capture the traceback (Python's error report) with `Win + Shift + S` (macOS: `Cmd + Shift + 4`), paste it with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why it failed.
  * `[4]` Terminal: run `python` followed by your script name. Return code `0` means the script finished with no errors.

* **9. Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * Every example has three parts: **1. Input**, **2. Process**, and **3. Output**. Name each file with your own name and the session code, for example `Reacher_S14_demo_1.py` (pattern: `YourName_S14_demo_1.py`).
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Find Overspending with a Boolean Mask):* `YourName_S14_demo_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the quarterly budget data
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_01_04_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: Consumer quarters that spent more than budget
    mask = (
        (df["Division_Name"] == "Consumer")
        & (df["Actual_Expense_USD"] > df["Budget_Expense_USD"])
    )
    overruns = df[mask]

    # 3. Output: count and list them
    print("Consumer overrun quarters:", len(overruns))
    print(overruns[["Fiscal_Quarter", "Budget_Expense_USD",
                    "Actual_Expense_USD"]])
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `(df["Division_Name"] == "Consumer")` gives one `True` / `False` per row: `True` for the 4 Consumer rows.
      * `(df["Actual_Expense_USD"] > df["Budget_Expense_USD"])` compares the two money columns row by row: `True` where a quarter spent more than planned.
      * `&` keeps `True` only where both are `True` (section 7.1). The result, `mask`, is a column of 16 `True` / `False` values. Naming it makes the rule easy to read and reuse.
      * `df[mask]` keeps the rows where `mask` is `True` and stores them in `overruns`.
      * `len(overruns)` counts those rows.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Consumer overrun quarters: 3
        Fiscal_Quarter  Budget_Expense_USD  Actual_Expense_USD
      1             Q1            780000.0            810000.0
      5             Q2            780000.0            795000.0
      9             Q3            780000.0            825000.0
      ```

      * The Consumer division overspent in 3 of its 4 quarters; only Q4 (`775000.0`) stayed within the `780000.0` budget.
      * Q3 has the largest overrun: `825000 - 780000 = 45000`, or `5.77`% over budget.
      * The labels `1`, `5`, `9` are the original row positions in the 16-row file.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`~`):*
    * New tool: `~` (not) turns every `True` into `False`, and every `False` into `True`. Why: find the opposite group, such as quarters that stayed within budget.
    * Task: in part **2. Process**, change `& (df["Actual_Expense_USD"] > df["Budget_Expense_USD"])` to `& _(df["Actual_Expense_USD"] > df["Budget_Expense_USD"])`.
    * Check: the script prints `Consumer overrun quarters: 1`, and the table shows `Q4` (budget `780000.0`, actual `775000.0`). The label still says "overrun", since only the rule changed.
  * *Micro-Example 2 (Total Each Division with `groupby()`):* `YourName_S14_demo_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the quarterly budget data
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_01_04_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: yearly totals for each division
    div_summary = df.groupby("Division_Name").agg(
        Total_Budget_USD=("Budget_Expense_USD", "sum"),
        Total_Actual_USD=("Actual_Expense_USD", "sum"),
        Quarters=("Transaction_ID", "count"),
    )
    div_summary["Variance_USD"] = (
        div_summary["Total_Actual_USD"]
        - div_summary["Total_Budget_USD"]
    )

    # 3. Output: one row per division
    print(div_summary[["Total_Actual_USD", "Variance_USD"]])
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `df.groupby("Division_Name")` splits the 16 rows into 4 groups, one per division (section 7.2).
      * Inside `.agg(...)`, each line has the form `New_Name=("Source_Column", "calculation")`. For example, `Total_Budget_USD=("Budget_Expense_USD", "sum")` adds up the budget of each division and calls the result `Total_Budget_USD`.
      * `Quarters=("Transaction_ID", "count")` counts the rows in each group; it is a quick check that every division has 4 quarters.
      * The next line subtracts two of the new columns to get each division's yearly variance.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
                     Total_Actual_USD  Variance_USD
      Division_Name
      Cloud                 1785000.0      -15000.0
      Consumer              3205000.0       85000.0
      Enterprise            1280000.0           0.0
      Logistics             2468000.0       28000.0
      ```

      * The division names are now the row labels: `groupby()` turns the group names into the index.
      * Consumer overspent by `85000.0` for the year and Logistics by `28000.0`; Enterprise spent exactly its budget; Cloud stayed `15000.0` under.
      * Consumer had one quarter under budget, yet it is still the largest yearly overspender: quarterly flags and yearly totals answer different questions.
  * *Quick Check 2 (`.sort_values()`):*
    * New tool: `.sort_values()` reorders rows by one column; `ascending=False` puts the largest first. Why: the director reads the biggest overspender first.
    * Task: add one line at the end of part **2. Process**: `div_summary = div_summary.___________("Variance_USD", ascending=False)`
    * Check: the first row is `Consumer` (`85000.0`), then `Logistics` (`28000.0`).
  * *Micro-Example 3 (Add VP Names with `pd.merge()`):* `YourName_S14_demo_3.py` (VP names are fictional)
    ```python
    # 1. Input: budget data and a VP list (fictional names)
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_01_04_dataset.csv")
    vp_info = pd.DataFrame({
        "Cost_Center_Code": ["CC-101", "CC-202", "CC-303", "CC-404"],
        "VP_Lead": ["Sarah Chen", "Marcus Vance",
                    "Elena Rostova", "David Kim"],
    })

    # 2. Process: add VP names, then flag spending over budget
    ledger = pd.merge(df, vp_info, on="Cost_Center_Code", how="left")
    ledger["Over_Budget"] = (
        ledger["Actual_Expense_USD"] > ledger["Budget_Expense_USD"]
    )

    # 3. Output: show the first three rows
    print(ledger[["Transaction_ID", "VP_Lead", "Over_Budget"]].head(3))
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `pd.DataFrame({...})` builds a small table directly in code. Inside the curly brackets, each column name is followed by its list of values, in row order. The two lists must have the same length (here 4).
      * `pd.merge(df, vp_info, on="Cost_Center_Code", how="left")` takes each of the 16 rows of `df`, looks up its cost center in `vp_info`, and adds the matching `VP_Lead` (section 7.3). `how="left"` keeps every row of `df`, even one with no match (section 7.4).
      * `ledger["Actual_Expense_USD"] > ledger["Budget_Expense_USD"]` compares row by row and stores `True` / `False` in a new `Over_Budget` column.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
        Transaction_ID        VP_Lead  Over_Budget
      0       TXN-5000     Sarah Chen        False
      1       TXN-5001   Marcus Vance         True
      2       TXN-5002  Elena Rostova        False
      ```

      * Every row now carries the name of the person responsible for that spending; no VP name was copied by hand.
      * `TXN-5001` (Consumer, Q1) is over budget, and Marcus Vance is accountable for it.
      * The table still has 16 rows; `.head(3)` only shows the first 3.
  * *Quick Check 3 (`.value_counts()`):*
    * New tool: `.value_counts()` counts how often each value appears in a column. Why: answers "how many quarters were over budget?" in one line.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(ledger["Over_Budget"].____________())`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `False 8` and `True 8`: half of the 16 quarters went over budget.

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Quarterly Division Audit:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S14_audit.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S14_audit.py`).
  * *Step 2: Group by Division and Quarter:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the quarterly budget data
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_01_04_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: variance per row, then group by two columns
    df["Variance_USD"] = df["Actual_Expense_USD"] - df["Budget_Expense_USD"]
    df["Variance_Pct"] = df["Variance_USD"] / df["Budget_Expense_USD"] * 100.0
    quarterly_audit = df.groupby(["Division_Name", "Fiscal_Quarter"]).agg(
        Total_Actual=("Actual_Expense_USD", "sum"),
        Total_Variance=("Variance_USD", "sum"),
        Mean_Variance_Pct=("Variance_Pct", "mean"),
    )

    # 3. Output: one row per division and quarter
    print("Quarterly Division Audit Summary:\n", quarterly_audit)
    ```
    * Grouping by a list of two columns gives one row per division and quarter: 16 rows, labeled by both values.
  * *Step 3: Verify Output Integrity:*
    Confirm that quarters with overruns show positive `Total_Variance` values. 8 of the 16 rows should be positive.
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:*
    Run the script via Panel `[4]` (Terminal) using `python YourName_S14_audit.py` and confirm return code `0`.

  * *Task 2 (Deliberate KeyError & AI Diagnosis):* Merge the result with `vp_info` (from Micro-Example 3), but type `on="Cost_Center"` on purpose. Read the `KeyError` (the column name does not exist), capture the traceback with `Win + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and review the AI's explanation of join keys.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - VP Exception Report:**
  * *Goal:* Build an exception report that tells the finance director which VPs must explain their spending. A VP is escalated when the cost center has repeated flagged quarters **and** overspent for the whole year.
  * *What you need:*
    * File: `data/session_01_04_dataset.csv` (16 rows, described in **5. Lab Practice Dataset**).
    * The `vp_info` table from Micro-Example 3 (VP names are fictional).
    * Script name: `YourName_S14_challenge.py`.
    * Tools you already know: Boolean masks, `groupby()` + `.agg()`, `pd.merge()`, `.sort_values()`, and `.to_csv()`.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. Add `Variance_USD` = `Actual_Expense_USD` - `Budget_Expense_USD` and `Variance_Pct` = `Variance_USD` / `Budget_Expense_USD` x 100, rounded to 2 decimal places.
    2. Add `My_Flag` = `True` when `Variance_Pct > 2.0`. Check your rule: count the rows where `My_Flag` disagrees with `Audit_Status == "Variance_Flag"`. The count must be `0`.
    3. **Flagged summary:** keep only the flagged rows, then group by `Cost_Center_Code` and compute `Flagged_Quarters` (count of rows), `Flagged_Overrun_USD` (sum of `Variance_USD`), and `Worst_Variance_Pct` (largest `Variance_Pct`).
    4. **Yearly summary:** using all 16 rows, group by `Cost_Center_Code`, sum budget and actual spending, and compute `Year_Variance_Pct` = (actual - budget) / budget x 100, rounded to 2 decimal places.
    5. Start from `vp_info` and merge in the yearly summary, then the flagged summary, both with `on="Cost_Center_Code"` and `how="left"`. If a cost center had no flagged quarter, its empty values must become `0`.
    6. Add `Escalate` = `True` when `Flagged_Quarters >= 2` **and** `Year_Variance_Pct > 1.0`.
    7. Sort by `Flagged_Overrun_USD`, then by `Worst_Variance_Pct`, both largest first. Print the table and save it to `data/vp_exception_report.csv` without row labels.
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * `.reset_index()`: after `groupby()`, the group names (here, the cost center codes) become the row labels. `.reset_index()` turns them back into a normal column, so you can merge on `Cost_Center_Code`.
    * `.fillna(0)`: replaces empty values (`NaN`) with `0`, for example `report["Flagged_Quarters"] = report["Flagged_Quarters"].fillna(0)`.
    * Sorting by two columns: `.sort_values(["Flagged_Overrun_USD", "Worst_Variance_Pct"], ascending=False)`. The second column only decides ties in the first.
    * `"max"` inside `.agg()` returns the largest value in each group, just as `"sum"` returns the total.
  * *Hints:*
    * Build the two summaries as two separate tables first, print each one, and only then merge.
    * `(df["My_Flag"] != (df["Audit_Status"] == "Variance_Flag")).sum()` counts disagreements, because each `True` counts as 1.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Rule check: `0` disagreements; 6 of the 16 quarters are flagged.
    * Table order: `CC-202` Marcus Vance (2 flagged, `75000.0`, worst `5.77`), `CC-404` David Kim (2, `45000.0`, `4.92`), `CC-303` Elena Rostova (1, `10000.0`, `3.12`), `CC-101` Sarah Chen (1, `10000.0`, `2.22`).
    * `Escalate` is `True` only for Marcus Vance (`Year_Variance_Pct` `2.72`) and David Kim (`1.15`). Elena Rostova and Sarah Chen each had one flagged quarter but finished the year at or under budget.
