## Module 2: Web Data Extraction, Visual Dashboards, and Midterm Milestone

Module 2 shifts focus from internal corporate records to external market intelligence and visual storytelling. Students learn to extract public competitor price data using web scrapers, construct publication-grade business charts, and present audited exploratory findings during the midterm milestone.

<a id="session-02-01"></a>
### Session 2.1: Web Data Extraction: Scraping Financial and Competitor Intelligence with Beautiful Soup

![Session 2.1 Map](assets/session_02_01_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_02_01_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_02_01_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 5 (115/10/11 - 115/10/17) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **1. Session Purpose:**
  * Our pricing team watches 3 competitor websites, where prices sit on web pages, not in CSV files
  * Copying hundreds of prices by hand is slow and error-prone
  * The pricing manager asks: what does each competitor charge, and who discounts most? Today, Python reads a web page and turns it into a table

* **2. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "How does a web page reach my computer?": explain an HTTP (HyperText Transfer Protocol) GET request and read HTML (HyperText Markup Language: the text with tags that describes a web page)
  2. "What is BetaMart charging?": extract prices with Beautiful Soup (a Python library that reads HTML) and CSS (Cascading Style Sheets) selectors (an address for tags, such as `.price`)
  3. "What if a product has no stock tag?": handle a missing tag (the search returns `None`, meaning nothing found) and fix an `AttributeError`

* **3. Business Context: Why Scrape?**
  * By hand: open each competitor page, copy each price into Excel, and do it all again tomorrow. 3 sites x 100 products = 300 copy-pastes a day, with typos.
  * With a Python script: read every product on the page in seconds, get the same result every run, and run it again every morning. The output is a clean table, ready for pandas.
  * Rules of the road (scrape responsibly):
    * Public pages only, never behind a login
    * Check the site's `robots.txt` and terms of use. `robots.txt` is a short public file on a website (for example, `betamart.example/robots.txt`) that lists which pages automated scripts may and may not read.
    * Pause between requests, for example `time.sleep(2)` (wait 2 seconds), so the site is not overloaded
    * Name your script in the request. The User-Agent is a short label sent with every request that tells the site who is asking.

* **4. Data Basics: From Web Request to HTML:**
  * 1. Your browser or Python script sends an HTTP GET request: "send me this page".
  * 2. The web server answers with the page as HTML text.
  * The browser draws that text as a page for people; Python reads the text itself. In the example below, `data-sku` stores the product's SKU (Stock Keeping Unit), the store's product code:

    ```html
    <div class="product" data-sku="SKU-801">
      <h3 class="name">Office Desk Chair</h3>
      <span class="price">$49.50</span>
      <span class="stock">120 units</span>
    </div>
    ```

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * Saved web page: [data/session_02_01_catalog.html](data/session_02_01_catalog.html), a fictional BetaMart catalog page with 4 product cards (SKU-807 has no stock tag).
  * File Link: [data/session_02_01_dataset.csv](data/session_02_01_dataset.csv)
  * Audited 12-row competitor pricing dataset compiled from automated extraction (competitor names are fictional):
    * `Product_SKU`: Stock Keeping Unit, the product code (e.g., `SKU-801`).
    * `Competitor_Name`: Competitor (AlphaRetail, BetaMart, GammaGlobal).
    * `Category_Name`: Merchandise category (Electronics, Office Supplies, Hardware, Appliances).
    * `List_Price_USD`: Extracted competitor catalog price in U.S. Dollars.
    * `Promotion_Discount_Pct`: Advertised promotional markdown percentage.
    * `Stock_Availability_Units`: Available warehouse stock units.

* **6. Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `HTTP GET`:
    * What: a request, using the HyperText Transfer Protocol (HTTP), that asks a web server for a page
    * Why: every page your browser or script reads starts with one
  * `HTML Tag`:
    * What: a label around content, such as `<span class="price">`
    * Why: tags tell you where each price or name sits
  * `DOM Tree`:
    * What: Document Object Model: tags nested inside tags, like folders
    * Why: one product card holds its name, price, and stock
  * `Beautiful Soup (bs4)`:
    * What: a Python library that reads HTML into a searchable tree
    * Why: find tags by name or class in one line
  * `CSS Selector`:
    * What: a short address for tags: `.price` = class "price"
    * Why: `.select()` / `.select_one()` use it to pick tags
  * `Ethical Scraping`:
    * What: follow `robots.txt`, pause between requests, stay on public pages
    * Why: protects the website and your company

* **7. Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * **7.1 A web page is text with tags, not a table**
    * *The idea:* When you open a web page, your browser receives a long piece of text written in HTML and draws it as a page. The prices you see are inside that text, wrapped in tags such as `<span class="price">$49.50</span>`. A tag has three parts: an opening tag (`<span ...>`), the content (`$49.50`), and a closing tag (`</span>`). The `class="price"` part is a label that the website's designers put on the tag, and it is what we use to find it.
    * *See it on real data:* one product card from our saved BetaMart page.

      ```text
      <div class="product" data-sku="SKU-801">
        <h3 class="name">Office Desk Chair</h3>
        <span class="price">$49.50</span>
        <span class="stock">120 units</span>
      </div>
      ```

      | What we want | Where it is in the HTML |
      |---|---|
      | Product code | the `data-sku` setting of the `div` tag: `SKU-801` |
      | Name | inside the `h3` tag with class `name`: `Office Desk Chair` |
      | Price | inside the `span` tag with class `price`: `$49.50` |
      | Stock | inside the `span` tag with class `stock`: `120 units` |

    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `<h3 class="name">Office Desk Chair</h3>` | tag `h3`, class `name`, content `Office Desk Chair` | the product name |
      | `<span class="stock">120 units</span>` | tag `span`, class `stock`, content `120 units` | the stock level, as text |
      | `<div class="product" data-sku="SKU-801">` | tag `div`, class `product`, setting `data-sku` | the card that holds one product; its code is a setting, not content |
      | `<h1>BetaMart Weekly Prices</h1>` | tag `h1`, no class, content `BetaMart Weekly Prices` | the page heading |

    * *Remember:* scraping means finding the right tags and taking the text inside them.
  * **7.2 Tags inside tags: the DOM tree**
    * *The idea:* Tags are nested: the `div` card contains the name, price, and stock tags, just as a folder contains files. This nesting is called the DOM tree (Document Object Model). Beautiful Soup reads the HTML text and builds this tree, so you can ask for "the price tag inside this card" instead of searching through raw text.
    * *Why nesting matters:* our page has 4 cards, and each one has its own price. If you search the whole page for `.price`, you get all 4 prices. If you first take one card and then search inside it, you get exactly that card's price, already paired with the right name.
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Search | Result |
      |---|---|
      | `soup.select(".price")` (whole page) | 4 tags: `$49.50`, `$320.00`, `$429.00`, `$195.00` |
      | `card.select_one(".price")` (one card) | 1 tag: `$49.50`, for `SKU-801` only |

    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `soup.select(".stock")` | 3 tags: `120 units`, `38 units`, `15 units` | whole page: only 3, because one card has no stock tag |
      | `soup.select(".product")[1].find("h3").text` | `Wireless Router` | the name inside the second card (position `1`) |
      | `soup.select(".product")[3]["data-sku"]` | `SKU-810` | the code of the fourth card |
      | `soup.select_one(".product .stock").text` | `120 units` | a space in a selector means "inside": a stock tag inside a product card |

    * *Remember:* loop over the cards, then search inside each card; that keeps every value with its own product.
  * **7.3 Four ways to find tags**

    | Method | Finds | Returns | Example |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | `.find("h3")` | the first tag with that name | one tag | `Office Desk Chair` |
    | `.find_all("h3")` | every tag with that name | a list of tags | 4 product names |
    | `.select_one(".price")` | the first tag matching a CSS selector | one tag | `$49.50` |
    | `.select(".price")` | every tag matching a CSS selector | a list of tags | 4 prices |

    * *The idea:* `find` methods search by tag name; `select` methods use CSS selectors (Cascading Style Sheets), the same short addresses web designers use: `.price` means "class `price`", and `div.product` means "a `div` tag with class `product`".
    * *Getting the content:* a tag is not yet text. `.text` takes the words inside it (`"$49.50"`), and `tag["data-sku"]` reads a setting written inside the opening tag (`"SKU-801"`). `.strip()` removes spaces and line breaks around the text.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `soup.find("span").text` | `$49.50` | the first `span` on the page happens to be a price |
      | `len(soup.find_all("span"))` | `7` | 4 price tags + 3 stock tags |
      | `len(soup.select("div.product"))` | `4` | the number of product cards |
      | `soup.select_one(".discount")` | `None` | no tag on the page has the class `discount` |
      | `soup.h1.text` | `BetaMart Weekly Prices` | a short way to get the first tag of a kind |

    * *Remember:* one tag: `find` / `select_one`; all tags: `find_all` / `select`; then `.text` for the words.
  * **7.4 Text is not a number yet**
    * *The idea:* Everything scraped is text, even prices. `"$49.50"` cannot be added or averaged. Two steps turn it into a number: remove the dollar sign with `.replace("$", "")`, which gives `"49.50"`, then convert it with `float()`, which gives `49.5`.
    * *Common mistake:* forgetting the conversion. Text "added" together is joined, not summed, and `float("$49.50")` with the `$` still there raises `ValueError: could not convert string to float`.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `float("320.00")` | `320.0` | clean text converts directly |
      | `float("$1,299.99".replace("$", "").replace(",", ""))` | `1299.99` | remove every symbol first: the `$` and the thousands comma |
      | `int("120 units".split()[0])` | `120` | `.split()` cuts the text at spaces; `[0]` takes the first piece, `"120"` |
      | `"49.5" + "10"` | `'49.510'` | text is joined, not added |
      | `float("49.5") + 10` | `59.5` | after conversion, the math works |

    * *Remember:* clean the text first, then `float()`.
  * **7.5 Select by class name, not by position**
    * *The idea:* You could tell a script "take the 2nd tag in each card". That works until the website adds something, such as a red "SALE" badge before the price; then the 2nd tag is the badge and the script quietly collects the word "SALE". Selecting by class name (`.price`) follows the label, not the position, so it keeps working when the layout changes.
    * *More examples:* the same card before and after the website adds a "SALE" badge in front of the price.

      | Code | Before the badge | After the badge | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | `card.find_all("span")[0].text` | `$49.50` | `SALE` | "the first `span`" now points at the badge |
      | `card.select_one(".price").text` | `$49.50` | `$44.50` | the class name still finds the price |

    * *Remember:* use class names; still recheck the script whenever the site is redesigned, because class names can change too.
  * **7.6 When a tag is missing: `None`**
    * *The idea:* Real pages are irregular. On our page, the card for `SKU-807` has no stock tag. When `.select_one(".stock")` finds nothing, it returns `None`, Python's word for "nothing". Asking for the text of nothing, `None.text`, stops the script with `AttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'text'`.
    * *The fix:* store the result first, check it, then read it:

      ```text
      stock_tag = card.select_one(".stock")
      if stock_tag is not None:
          stock = stock_tag.text.strip()
      else:
          stock = "not shown"
      ```

    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `soup.select(".product")[2].select_one(".stock")` | `None` | the third card (`SKU-807`) has no stock tag |
      | `[c["data-sku"] for c in soup.select(".product") if c.select_one(".stock") is None]` | `['SKU-807']` | list every card with a missing stock tag |
      | `soup.select(".product")[0].select_one(".stock").text` | `120 units` | the first card has one, so `.text` works |

    * *Remember:* any tag might be missing; check for `None` before you read `.text`.

* **8. Development Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE:**
  * `[1]` File Explorer: create your script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S21_demo_1.py`), and check the files are in `data/`.
  * `[2]` Code Editor: write a few lines, run, repeat. Small steps keep errors small.
  * `[3]` AI Agent Chat: on an error, capture the traceback (Python's error report) with `Win + Shift + S` (macOS: `Cmd + Shift + 4`), paste it with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why it failed.
  * `[4]` Terminal: run `python` followed by your script name. Return code `0` means the script finished with no errors. First time only: `pip install beautifulsoup4`.

* **9. Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * Every example has three parts: **1. Input**, **2. Process**, and **3. Output**. Name each file with your own name and the session code, for example `Reacher_S21_demo_1.py` (pattern: `YourName_S21_demo_1.py`).
  * First time only: install Beautiful Soup in the Terminal with `pip install beautifulsoup4`.
  * The examples read a saved copy of a fictional competitor page, `data/session_02_01_catalog.html`, so they work the same in class without internet access. On a live site, `requests.get(url).text` returns the same kind of HTML text.
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Read a Web Page with Beautiful Soup):* `YourName_S21_demo_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: read the saved web page (HTML text)
    from bs4 import BeautifulSoup
    html_text = open("data/session_02_01_catalog.html").read()

    # 2. Process: build the tree, then find the first product name
    soup = BeautifulSoup(html_text, "html.parser")
    first_name = soup.find("h3").text

    # 3. Output: show what we found
    print("Page title:", soup.title.text)
    print("First product:", first_name)
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `from bs4 import BeautifulSoup` loads Beautiful Soup. The library is installed as `beautifulsoup4` but imported as `bs4`.
      * `open("data/...html").read()` opens the saved page and reads the whole file as one long piece of text, stored in `html_text`. On a live website, `requests.get(url).text` would give you the same kind of text.
      * `BeautifulSoup(html_text, "html.parser")` reads that text and builds the DOM tree (section 7.2), stored in `soup`. `"html.parser"` is the HTML reader that comes with Python.
      * `soup.find("h3")` returns the first `h3` tag on the page, and `.text` takes the words inside it.
      * `soup.title.text` reads the page's title, the text shown on the browser tab.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Page title: BetaMart Online Catalog (fictional)
      First product: Office Desk Chair
      ```

      * The title confirms that the script read the right page.
      * Only one name is printed, because `.find()` stops at the first match; Quick Check 1 counts all of them.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`.find_all()`):*
    * New tool: `.find_all()` returns every matching tag as a list; `.find()` returns only the first. Why: count how many products the page shows.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print("Products on page:", len(soup.________("h3")))`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `Products on page: 4`.
  * *Micro-Example 2 (Turn Product Cards into Rows):* `YourName_S21_demo_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: read and parse the saved web page
    from bs4 import BeautifulSoup
    html_text = open("data/session_02_01_catalog.html").read()
    soup = BeautifulSoup(html_text, "html.parser")

    # 2. Process: one dictionary per product card
    products = []
    for card in soup.select(".product"):
        price_text = card.select_one(".price").text
        products.append({
            "sku": card["data-sku"],
            "name": card.select_one(".name").text.strip(),
            "price_usd": float(price_text.replace("$", "")),
        })

    # 3. Output: one line per product
    for item in products:
        print(item)
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `products = []` starts an empty list that will collect one dictionary per product.
      * `for card in soup.select(".product"):` repeats the indented lines once for each product card; on each pass, `card` is one card (section 7.2).
      * `card.select_one(".price").text` finds the price tag **inside this card** and takes its text, such as `"$49.50"`.
      * `products.append({...})` adds one dictionary to the list. A dictionary pairs names with values: `"sku"` with `card["data-sku"]`, `"name"` with the cleaned name, and `"price_usd"` with the price as a number.
      * `float(price_text.replace("$", ""))` removes the dollar sign and converts the text to a number (section 7.4).
      * The last loop prints each dictionary on its own line.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      {'sku': 'SKU-801', 'name': 'Office Desk Chair', 'price_usd': 49.5}
      {'sku': 'SKU-804', 'name': 'Wireless Router', 'price_usd': 320.0}
      {'sku': 'SKU-807', 'name': 'Compact Refrigerator', 'price_usd': 429.0}
      {'sku': 'SKU-810', 'name': 'Cordless Drill', 'price_usd': 195.0}
      ```

      * 4 cards became 4 rows, each with its code, name, and price kept together.
      * `49.5` has no quotation marks: it is now a number. `pd.DataFrame(products)` would turn this list straight into a table with columns `sku`, `name`, and `price_usd`.
  * *Quick Check 2 (`.get_text(strip=True)`):*
    * New tool: `.get_text(strip=True)` returns the words inside a tag, with spaces and line breaks removed from both ends. Why: one call does the work of `.text.strip()`.
    * Task: in part **2. Process**, change `"name": card.select_one(".name").text.strip(),` to `"name": card.select_one(".name").________(strip=True),`
    * Check: the output does not change; the first line is still `{'sku': 'SKU-801', 'name': 'Office Desk Chair', 'price_usd': 49.5}`.
  * *Micro-Example 3 (From Scraped Prices to a Comparison Table):* `YourName_S21_demo_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: prices already scraped from 3 competitor sites
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_02_01_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: price after the advertised discount
    df["Effective_Price_USD"] = (
        df["List_Price_USD"] * (1 - df["Promotion_Discount_Pct"] / 100)
    ).round(2)
    electronics = df.loc[df["Category_Name"] == "Electronics"]

    # 3. Output: compare competitors, then save the table
    print(electronics[["Competitor_Name", "List_Price_USD",
                       "Effective_Price_USD"]])
    electronics.to_csv("data/electronics_prices.csv", index=False)
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * The CSV holds prices already scraped from 3 competitor sites, one row per product.
      * `df["List_Price_USD"] * (1 - df["Promotion_Discount_Pct"] / 100)` calculates the price a customer actually pays after the advertised discount, for all 12 rows at once. Example: `299.99 x (1 - 10 / 100) = 269.99`.
      * `.round(2)` keeps 2 decimals, as for money.
      * `df.loc[df["Category_Name"] == "Electronics"]` keeps the 3 Electronics rows, one per competitor.
      * `electronics.to_csv("data/electronics_prices.csv", index=False)` saves those rows as a new CSV file; `index=False` leaves out the row labels, which are not data.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
        Competitor_Name  List_Price_USD  Effective_Price_USD
      0     AlphaRetail          299.99               269.99
      4        BetaMart          320.00               281.60
      8     GammaGlobal          310.00               263.50
      ```

      * AlphaRetail has the lowest list price, but GammaGlobal is cheapest after its 15% discount (`263.50`).
      * A comparison on list prices alone would pick the wrong competitor: always compare the price the customer actually pays.
  * *Quick Check 3 (`.min()`):*
    * New tool: `.min()` returns the smallest value in a column; `.max()` returns the largest. Why: answers "what is the lowest competitor price?" in one step.
    * Task: add two lines to part **3. Output**: `lowest = electronics["Effective_Price_USD"].___()` and `print("Lowest price:", lowest)`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `Lowest price: 263.5` (GammaGlobal).

* **10. Official Python Documentation & References:**
  * [Beautiful Soup 4 Official Documentation](https://beautiful-soup-4.readthedocs.io/en/latest/).
  * [Requests: HTTP for Humans Official Documentation](https://requests.readthedocs.io/en/latest/).
  * [World Wide Web Consortium (W3C) HTML5 Standards](https://www.w3.org/TR/html52/).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Competitor Price Benchmark:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S21_benchmark.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S21_benchmark.py`).
  * *Step 2: Benchmark Competitors by Category:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: prices scraped from 3 competitor sites
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_02_01_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: effective price, then average by category and competitor
    df["Effective_Price_USD"] = (
        df["List_Price_USD"] * (1 - df["Promotion_Discount_Pct"] / 100)
    ).round(2)
    benchmark = df.groupby(["Category_Name", "Competitor_Name"]).agg(
        Avg_Discount_Pct=("Promotion_Discount_Pct", "mean"),
        Avg_Effective_Price=("Effective_Price_USD", "mean"),
    )

    # 3. Output: one row per category and competitor
    print("Competitor Category Pricing Benchmark:\n", benchmark)
    ```
  * *Step 3: Analyze Business Insights:*
    In Electronics and Hardware, find the competitor with the deepest discount and the competitor with the lowest effective price. Are they the same?
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:*
    Run `python YourName_S21_benchmark.py` in Panel `[4]` (Terminal) and confirm return code `0`.
  * *Success Check:* Electronics: GammaGlobal has both the deepest discount (15%) and the lowest price (`263.5`). Hardware: GammaGlobal has the deepest discount (15%), but AlphaRetail has the lowest price (`157.5`). The deepest discount is not always the lowest price.

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Scrape Stock Levels Safely:**

The SKU-807 card has no stock tag, so `.select_one(".stock")` returns `None` and Python stops with `AttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'text'`. Capture the traceback with `Win + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and review the AI's explanation.
  * *Task 3 (Safe Extraction and Export):* Save the tag first with `stock_tag = card.select_one(".stock")`. If `stock_tag is not None`, use `stock_tag.text.strip()`; else use `"not shown"`. Save the table with `pd.DataFrame(products).to_csv("data/competitor_price_audit.csv", index=False)`.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - Detect a Price Change on a New Site:**
  * *Goal:* Scrape a second competitor page that uses **different tags**, compare it with last week's data in the CSV, and report which product's discount changed.
  * *What you need:*
    * Saved web page: [data/session_02_01_gamma.html](data/session_02_01_gamma.html), a fictional GammaGlobal page with 4 products.
    * Last week's data: `data/session_02_01_dataset.csv` (use only the rows where `Competitor_Name` is `"GammaGlobal"`).
    * Script name: `YourName_S21_challenge.py`.
    * Tools you already know: `BeautifulSoup`, `.select()`, `.select_one()`, `None` checks, `float()`, `pd.DataFrame()`, `pd.merge()`, and `.to_csv()`.
  * *How the GammaGlobal page is built (open the file in the Code Editor to see it):*
    * Each product is an `li` tag with class `item`, and its SKU is stored in `data-sku`.
    * The product name is in a tag with class `title`.
    * Every product has a `list-price` tag, such as `$189.00`.
    * A product **on sale** also has a `sale-price` tag. A product without a sale has **no** `sale-price` tag.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. For each product, collect `sku`, `name`, `List_Price_Web` (the list price as a number), and `Current_Price_Web`: the sale price if the `sale-price` tag exists, otherwise the list price.
    2. Add `Discount_Pct_Web` = (`List_Price_Web` - `Current_Price_Web`) / `List_Price_Web` x 100, rounded to 1 decimal place.
    3. Turn the list into a DataFrame. Merge it with the GammaGlobal rows of the CSV, matching `sku` on the web side with `Product_SKU` on the CSV side.
    4. Add `Discount_Changed` = `True` when `Discount_Pct_Web` is not equal to `Promotion_Discount_Pct`.
    5. Print `sku`, `name`, `Current_Price_Web`, `Discount_Pct_Web`, `Promotion_Discount_Pct`, and `Discount_Changed`, then save the table to `data/gamma_price_check.csv` without row labels.
    6. Print one summary line, for example `Products with a changed discount: 1`.
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * `.select(".item")` works for any class name; only the name changes from the BetaMart page (`.product`) to this page (`.item`).
    * Merging when the key columns have **different names**: `pd.merge(web_df, gamma_df, left_on="sku", right_on="Product_SKU", how="left")`. `left_on` names the key in the first table and `right_on` the key in the second.
    * `!=` means "not equal". On two columns, it gives one `True` / `False` per row.
  * *Hints:*
    * Read the sale tag first with `sale_tag = card.select_one(".sale-price")`, then use `if sale_tag is not None:` to choose the current price.
    * Filter the CSV before merging: `gamma_df = df.loc[df["Competitor_Name"] == "GammaGlobal"]`.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Current prices on the page: `SKU-802` `160.65`, `SKU-805` `45.0` (no sale tag), `SKU-808` `263.5`, `SKU-811` `391.0`.
    * Only `SKU-811` (Electric Oven) changed: the web shows `15.0`% off, while last week's CSV says `10.0`%.
    * Summary line: `Products with a changed discount: 1`. Business meaning: GammaGlobal deepened its oven discount this week, so our pricing team should review our appliance prices.

<a id="session-02-02"></a>
### Session 2.2: Business Data Visualization: Exploratory Plotting and Executive Dashboards

![Session 2.2 Map](assets/session_02_02_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_02_02_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_02_02_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 6 (115/10/18 - 115/10/24) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **1. Session Purpose:**
  * The marketing team keeps 12 months of results in one table: revenue, marketing spend, cost per new customer, and customer loyalty
  * Executives often have only a minute or two per chart, and a table of 72 numbers hides the trend
  * The CMO (Chief Marketing Officer) asks: is our marketing spend paying off, and are customers getting happier? Today, Python turns the table into charts that answer this at a glance

* **2. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "Is revenue growing month by month?": draw a line chart with Matplotlib (Python's main charting library) using `fig, ax = plt.subplots()` (`fig` is the whole image; `ax` is one chart inside it)
  2. "In which months did we spend the most on marketing?": draw a bar chart with Seaborn (a library built on Matplotlib for quick, clean statistical charts)
  3. "Which numbers move together?": read a correlation heatmap (a colored grid of correlations, each from -1 to +1), and fix an `IndexError` in a 2x2 dashboard

* **3. Business Context: A Chart Answers One Question:**
  * A table makes the reader hunt for the pattern; a chart shows it. In our table, revenue rises from `12.4` to `25.8` (USD millions), but the jump in November and December is easy to miss among 72 numbers.
  * Five rules for charts that executives trust:
    * One message per chart, and the title states it, for example "Revenue More Than Doubled from Jan to Dec"
    * Label every axis with its unit, such as "USD M" (millions of U.S. Dollars)
    * Bar charts start at zero; a cut-off axis makes small differences look huge
    * No 3D effects or decoration; every mark on the chart should carry data
    * Use colors that still work in black-and-white print and for color-blind readers

* **4. Data Basics: Pick the Chart from the Question:**

  | Business question | Chart | Python |
  |---|---|---|
  | How does a number change over time? | Line chart | `ax.plot()` |
  | Which category is biggest? | Bar chart | `sns.barplot()` or `ax.bar()` |
  | Do two numbers move together? | Scatter plot | `ax.scatter()` |
  | Which of many numbers move together? | Correlation heatmap | `sns.heatmap()` |

  * **Correlation** measures how closely two columns move together, on a scale from -1 to +1:
    * `+1`: when one goes up, the other always goes up
    * `0`: no straight-line pattern
    * `-1`: when one goes up, the other always goes down
  * Correlation is not causation. In our data, marketing spend and revenue have a correlation of `0.996`, but both also simply grew through the year. A high correlation alone does not prove that the spending caused the revenue.

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * File Link: [data/session_02_02_dataset.csv](data/session_02_02_dataset.csv)
  * Audited 12-row monthly performance dataset (fictional teaching data), one row per month:
    * `Month_Index`: Month number, 1 to 12.
    * `Month_Name`: Three-letter month name, `Jan` through `Dec`.
    * `Organic_Revenue_USD_M`: Organic revenue in millions of U.S. Dollars. "Organic" means revenue from the company's own operations, not from buying other companies.
    * `Paid_Marketing_Spend_USD_M`: Paid advertising spend in millions of U.S. Dollars.
    * `Customer_Acquisition_Cost_USD`: CAC (Customer Acquisition Cost): the average marketing cost to win one new customer, in U.S. Dollars.
    * `Net_Promoter_Score`: NPS (Net Promoter Score): a customer loyalty score from -100 to +100, based on how likely customers are to recommend the company. Our values range from `58` to `74`.

* **6. Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `Matplotlib` and `Seaborn`:
    * What: Python charting libraries; Seaborn adds quick, clean statistical charts on top of Matplotlib
    * Why: they turn a DataFrame into a chart image with a few lines of code
  * `Figure` and `Axes`:
    * What: the `Figure` is the whole image; each `Axes` is one chart inside it
    * Why: you draw on a specific `Axes`, so a dashboard can hold several charts
  * `Line Chart`:
    * What: points joined in time order
    * Why: shows trends, such as monthly revenue
  * `Bar Chart`:
    * What: one bar per category; the bar length is the value
    * Why: compares sizes, such as spend by month
  * `Scatter Plot`:
    * What: one dot per row, with one column on each axis
    * Why: shows whether two numbers move together
  * `Correlation Heatmap`:
    * What: a grid of correlations, colored from -1 to +1
    * Why: spots which of many numbers move together, in one picture

* **7. Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * **7.1 Figure and Axes: the canvas and the charts on it**
    * *The idea:* Matplotlib draws in two layers. The **Figure** is the whole image, like a blank sheet of paper. An **Axes** is one chart drawn on that sheet, with its own x-axis, y-axis, title, and data. One Figure can hold one Axes or many. `fig, ax = plt.subplots()` creates both at once and gives you a name for each, so every later instruction says exactly which chart it changes.
    * *See it on real data:* the revenue chart in Micro-Example 1.

      | Instruction | Goes to | Effect |
      |---|---|---|
      | `fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 5))` | creates both | a 10 x 5 inch image with one chart area |
      | `ax.plot(df["Month_Name"], df["Organic_Revenue_USD_M"])` | the chart | draws 12 points joined by a line |
      | `ax.set_title("Revenue More Than Doubled from Jan to Dec")` | the chart | the chart's title |
      | `ax.set_ylabel("Organic Revenue (USD M)")` | the chart | the y-axis label |
      | `fig.savefig("data/revenue_trend.png")` | the whole image | writes the image to a file |

    * *Common mistake:* mixing the two styles. Many online examples write `plt.plot()` and `plt.title()`, which draw on "whatever chart is active". With several charts, that is often the wrong one. Always draw on a named `ax`.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `fig, ax = plt.subplots()` | one `Figure`, one `Axes` | the most common start for one chart |
      | `fig, (ax1, ax2) = plt.subplots(1, 2)` | 2 charts side by side | 1 row, 2 columns; each gets its own name |
      | `fig, axes = plt.subplots(2, 2)` | `axes.shape` is `(2, 2)` | 4 charts, stored as a 2 x 2 table |
      | `len(axes.flatten())` | `4` | `.flatten()` lines the 4 charts up in one row, for a loop |
      | `ax.set_xlabel("")` | no x-axis label | remove a label that adds nothing |

    * *Remember:* the Figure is the page; each Axes is a chart; draw on a named `ax`.
  * **7.2 Choose the chart from the question**
    * *The idea:* Each chart type answers one kind of question well. Start from the question the manager asks, then pick the chart; do not start from the chart you like.

      | Question | Chart | Example from our data |
      |---|---|---|
      | How does a number change over time? | line (`ax.plot()`) | revenue from Jan (`12.4`) to Dec (`25.8`) |
      | Which category is biggest? | bar (`sns.barplot()`, `ax.bar()`) | marketing spend peaks in Dec (`6.4`) |
      | Do two numbers move together? | scatter (`ax.scatter()`) | cost per new customer vs loyalty |
      | Which of many numbers move together? | heatmap (`sns.heatmap()`) | all 4 measures at once |

    * *Common mistake:* a line chart for categories that have no order (such as regions), or a pie chart with many slices. A line suggests a trend from one point to the next; it only makes sense when the x-axis has an order, such as months.
    * *More examples:*

      | Question | Best chart | Why |
      |---|---|---|
      | Did revenue grow every month? | line | the eye follows the slope from month to month |
      | Which 3 months had the most spend? | bar, sorted | lengths are easy to compare |
      | Does a higher CAC go with a higher NPS? | scatter | one dot per month shows the pattern |
      | Is any measure moving against the others? | heatmap | a blue cell would stand out among red ones |

    * *Remember:* question first, chart second.
  * **7.3 Correlation: how closely two columns move together**
    * *The idea:* The correlation of two columns is one number from `-1` to `+1`. Near `+1`, when one goes up the other goes up too; near `-1`, when one goes up the other goes down; near `0`, there is no straight-line pattern. `df["A"].corr(df["B"])` computes it for two columns, and `df[cols].corr()` for every pair at once.
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Pair of columns | Correlation | Meaning |
      |---|---|---|
      | marketing spend and revenue | `0.996` | they rose almost perfectly together |
      | month number and revenue | `0.946` | revenue grew through the year |
      | CAC and NPS | `0.976` | months with costlier customers also had happier customers |
      | month number and revenue per marketing dollar | `-0.95` | each dollar of marketing brought in less as the year went on |

    * *Common mistake:* reading correlation as cause. Spend and revenue both grew month by month, so they correlate strongly, but the data cannot tell whether the spend **caused** the revenue, or whether both simply followed the holiday season.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `pd.Series([1, 2, 3]).corr(pd.Series([2, 4, 6]))` | `1.0` | a perfect straight-line rise |
      | `pd.Series(range(1, 13)).corr(-pd.Series(range(1, 13)))` | `-1.0` | a perfect opposite movement |
      | `df["Month_Index"].corr(df["Net_Promoter_Score"])` | `0.963` | loyalty also rose through the year |
      | `df[cols].corr()` | a 4 x 4 table | every pair at once; the diagonal is always `1.0` |

    * *Remember:* correlation measures moving together, not cause and effect.
  * **7.4 Saving a chart: size and sharpness**
    * *The idea:* `fig.savefig(file, dpi=...)` writes the chart to an image file. The final size in pixels is the figure size in inches times the dpi (dots per inch). More dots mean a sharper image and a larger file.
    * *See it on real data:* a `figsize=(10, 5)` chart saved three ways.

      | `dpi` | Image size in pixels | Good for |
      |---|---|---|
      | `100` | 1000 x 500 | a quick look |
      | `150` | 1500 x 750 | screens and slides |
      | `300` | 3000 x 1500 | printed reports |

    * *Other settings:* `bbox_inches="tight"` trims empty white borders. The file type follows the name: `.png` for images, `.pdf` for documents that stay sharp at any zoom.
    * *Common mistake:* calling `plt.savefig()` after `plt.show()`. In many setups, showing the chart clears it, so the saved file is empty. Save first.
    * *More examples:*

      | Settings | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `figsize=(6, 4)`, `dpi=100` | 600 x 400 pixels | a small image for a quick check |
      | `figsize=(6, 4)`, `dpi=300` | 1800 x 1200 pixels | the same chart, sharp enough for print |
      | `figsize=(10, 5)`, `dpi=150`, `bbox_inches="tight"` | 1233 x 674 pixels | smaller than 1500 x 750, because the empty border was trimmed |
      | `fig.savefig("data/chart.pdf")` | a PDF file | stays sharp at any zoom, good for reports |

    * *Remember:* inches x dpi = pixels; save before you show.
  * **7.5 Grids of charts: rows and columns start at `0`**
    * *The idea:* `fig, axes = plt.subplots(2, 2)` makes 4 charts, and `axes` is a small table of them with 2 rows and 2 columns. You reach one chart with its row number and column number, both counted from `0`, just like `.iloc[]` in Session 1.3.

      | Position | Code |
      |---|---|
      | top left | `axes[0, 0]` |
      | top right | `axes[0, 1]` |
      | bottom left | `axes[1, 0]` |
      | bottom right | `axes[1, 1]` |

    * *Common mistake:* `axes[2]` asks for a third row, which does not exist, so Python stops with `IndexError: index 2 is out of bounds for axis 0 with size 2`. "Axis 0" means the rows; "size 2" means there are only rows `0` and `1`.
    * *Spacing:* `plt.tight_layout()` moves the charts apart so that titles and axis labels do not overlap.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `axes[1, 0]` | bottom-left chart | row 1 (second row), column 0 (first column) |
      | `axes[-1, -1]` | bottom-right chart | `-1` means "the last", for rows and columns alike |
      | `axes.flatten()[2]` | bottom-left chart | after flattening, the charts are numbered 0 to 3, row by row |
      | `fig, axes = plt.subplots(3, 1)` | `axes.shape` is `(3,)` | one column of 3 charts: now `axes[2]` exists |
      | `axes[2]` with a 2 x 2 grid | `IndexError` | there is no third row |

    * *Remember:* `axes[row, column]`, both from `0`.
  * **7.6 Honest charts: bars start at zero**
    * *The idea:* A bar's length is read as its size. If the y-axis starts above zero, the bars are cut off and small differences look huge.
    * *See it on real data:* marketing spend in Oct (`4.1`) and Nov (`5.2`).

      | y-axis starts at | Nov bar compared with Oct bar | What a reader believes |
      |---|---|---|
      | `0` | `1.27` times as long | Nov spend was about a quarter higher (true) |
      | `4.0` | `12.0` times as long | Nov spend was 12 times higher (false) |

    * *Other rules:* one message per chart, stated in the title; every axis labeled with its unit; no 3D effects; colors that still work in black-and-white print.
    * *More examples:*

      | Chart | What the reader sees | Honest? |
      |---|---|---|
      | Jan (`2.1`) vs Dec (`6.4`), axis from `0` | Dec bar `3.05` times as long | true: spend tripled |
      | the same, axis from `2.0` | Dec bar `44.0` times as long | false: the cut axis exaggerates fifteenfold |
      | revenue line chart, axis from `12` | the line fills the chart | acceptable: a line shows change, not size, so its axis need not start at `0` |
      | a 3D bar chart | bars at the back look smaller | avoid 3D: depth distorts the lengths |

    * *Remember:* if the axis is cut, the bar chart lies.

* **8. Development Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE:**
  * `[1]` File Explorer: create your script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S22_demo_1.py`), and check the dataset is in `data/`. Saved charts appear here as `.png` files; click one to open it.
  * `[2]` Code Editor: write a few lines, run, repeat. Small steps keep errors small.
  * `[3]` AI Agent Chat: on an error, capture the traceback (Python's error report) with `Win + Shift + S` (macOS: `Cmd + Shift + 4`), paste it with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why it failed.
  * `[4]` Terminal: run `python` followed by your script name. Return code `0` means the script finished with no errors. First time only: `pip install matplotlib seaborn`.

* **9. Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * Every example has three parts: **1. Input**, **2. Process**, and **3. Output**. Name each file with your own name and the session code, for example `Reacher_S22_demo_1.py` (pattern: `YourName_S22_demo_1.py`).
  * The examples save each chart as a `.png` file in `data/`. Open the file in Panel `[1]` to see the chart.
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Line Chart: Revenue Trend):* `YourName_S22_demo_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the monthly results
    import pandas as pd
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_02_02_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: one figure with one chart, revenue by month
    fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 5))
    ax.plot(df["Month_Name"], df["Organic_Revenue_USD_M"],
            color="#991B1B", marker="o")
    ax.set_title("Revenue More Than Doubled from Jan to Dec")
    ax.set_ylabel("Organic Revenue (USD M)")

    # 3. Output: save the chart as an image file
    fig.savefig("data/revenue_trend.png", dpi=150, bbox_inches="tight")
    print("Saved: data/revenue_trend.png")
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `import matplotlib.pyplot as plt` loads Matplotlib's charting tools under the short name `plt`.
      * `plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 5))` creates the Figure (10 inches wide, 5 tall) and one Axes, named `fig` and `ax` (section 7.1).
      * `ax.plot(x, y, color="#991B1B", marker="o")` draws the line: month names along the x-axis, revenue up the y-axis. `color` is a color code (`#991B1B` is a dark red) and `marker="o"` puts a dot on each month.
      * `ax.set_title(...)` writes the message of the chart, not just its topic: a reader knows the conclusion before looking at the line.
      * `fig.savefig(..., dpi=150, bbox_inches="tight")` writes a 1500 x 750 pixel image and trims the white border (section 7.4).
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Saved: data/revenue_trend.png
      ```

      * The Terminal only confirms the file; the chart itself is in `data/revenue_trend.png`. Open it from Panel `[1]`.
      * The line climbs from `12.4` in Jan to `25.8` in Dec, `2.08` times higher, with the steepest rise in Nov and Dec.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`ax.grid()`):*
    * New tool: `ax.grid(True, linestyle=":")` adds light dotted guide lines behind the chart. Why: readers can line up a point with its value on the axis.
    * Task: add one line at the end of part **2. Process**: `ax.____(True, linestyle=":")`
    * Check: open `data/revenue_trend.png`; dotted grid lines now sit behind the revenue line.
  * *Micro-Example 2 (Bar Chart: Marketing Spend by Month):* `YourName_S22_demo_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the monthly results
    import pandas as pd
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
    import seaborn as sns
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_02_02_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: one bar per month
    fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 4))
    sns.barplot(data=df, x="Month_Name", y="Paid_Marketing_Spend_USD_M",
                color="#475569", ax=ax)
    ax.set_title("Marketing Spend Peaks in November and December")
    ax.set_xlabel("")
    ax.set_ylabel("Paid Marketing Spend (USD M)")

    # 3. Output: save the chart
    fig.savefig("data/marketing_spend_bar.png", dpi=150, bbox_inches="tight")
    print("Saved: data/marketing_spend_bar.png")
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `import seaborn as sns` loads Seaborn, which draws common statistical charts with less code than Matplotlib alone.
      * `sns.barplot(data=df, x="Month_Name", y="Paid_Marketing_Spend_USD_M", ...)` reads the columns by name from the DataFrame and draws one bar per month. `ax=ax` tells Seaborn to draw on our named chart.
      * Seaborn's bars start at zero, so their lengths can be compared honestly (section 7.6).
      * `ax.set_xlabel("")` removes the automatic x-axis label (`Month_Name`), because the month names already explain the axis.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Saved: data/marketing_spend_bar.png
      ```

      * In the image, the bars grow through the year, and the last two are clearly the tallest: Nov `5.2` and Dec `6.4`, against `2.1` in Jan.
      * The title states this finding, so the chart can go straight into a report.
  * *Quick Check 2 (`.nlargest()`):*
    * New tool: `df.nlargest(3, "Column_Name")` returns the 3 rows with the largest values in that column. Why: confirms the chart's message with exact numbers.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(df.________(3, "Paid_Marketing_Spend_USD_M")[["Month_Name", "Paid_Marketing_Spend_USD_M"]])`
    * Check: the Terminal lists `Dec` (`6.4`), `Nov` (`5.2`), and `Oct` (`4.1`).
  * *Micro-Example 3 (Correlation Heatmap):* `YourName_S22_demo_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the monthly results
    import pandas as pd
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
    import seaborn as sns
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_02_02_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: correlation between every pair of 4 columns
    cols = ["Organic_Revenue_USD_M", "Paid_Marketing_Spend_USD_M",
            "Customer_Acquisition_Cost_USD", "Net_Promoter_Score"]
    corr = df[cols].corr().round(2)
    fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 6))
    sns.heatmap(corr, annot=True, cmap="RdBu_r", vmin=-1, vmax=1, ax=ax)
    ax.set_title("All Four Measures Rose Together in 2024")

    # 3. Output: print the numbers, then save the chart
    print(corr)
    fig.savefig("data/kpi_correlation_heatmap.png", dpi=150,
                bbox_inches="tight")
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `cols = [...]` lists the 4 measures to compare. A list keeps the code short and makes it easy to add a column later.
      * `df[cols].corr()` computes the correlation of every pair, a 4 x 4 table (section 7.3). `.round(2)` keeps 2 decimals.
      * `sns.heatmap(corr, annot=True, cmap="RdBu_r", vmin=-1, vmax=1, ax=ax)` colors each cell: red for positive, blue for negative, white near zero. `annot=True` writes the number in each cell, and `vmin` / `vmax` fix the color scale from `-1` to `+1`, so a pale cell really means a weak correlation.
      * `print(corr)` shows the same numbers in the Terminal.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
                                     Organic_Revenue_USD_M  ...  Net_Promoter_Score
      Organic_Revenue_USD_M                           1.00  ...                0.98
      Paid_Marketing_Spend_USD_M                      1.00  ...                0.97
      Customer_Acquisition_Cost_USD                   1.00  ...                0.98
      Net_Promoter_Score                              0.98  ...                1.00
      ```

      * The `...` hides middle columns that do not fit the Terminal width; the image shows all 16 cells.
      * Every value is between `0.97` and `1.00`: all four measures rose together through the year, so the heatmap is almost entirely dark red.
      * This is a reminder of section 7.3: when everything grows over time, everything correlates, and correlation alone cannot say what caused what.
  * *Quick Check 3 (`.corr()` on two columns):*
    * New tool: `column_a.corr(column_b)` returns one correlation number for two columns. Why: answers a single question without a whole heatmap.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(round(df["Paid_Marketing_Spend_USD_M"].____(df["Organic_Revenue_USD_M"]), 3))`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `0.996`.

* **10. Official Python Documentation & References:**
  * [Matplotlib Official Documentation: User Guide](https://matplotlib.org/stable/users/index.html).
  * [Seaborn Official Documentation: Statistical Data Visualization](https://seaborn.pydata.org/).
  * [Edward Tufte: The Visual Display of Quantitative Information (Principles of Information Design)](https://www.edwardtufte.com/tufte/books_vdqi).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Two-Panel Executive Dashboard:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S22_dashboard.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S22_dashboard.py`).
  * *Step 2: Build Two Charts Side by Side:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the monthly results
    import pandas as pd
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_02_02_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: one figure, two charts side by side
    fig, (ax1, ax2) = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(14, 5))

    # Left chart: revenue trend
    ax1.plot(df["Month_Name"], df["Organic_Revenue_USD_M"],
             color="#991B1B", marker="s", linewidth=2)
    ax1.set_title("Monthly Revenue (USD M)")
    ax1.grid(True, linestyle=":")

    # Right chart: cost per new customer vs customer loyalty
    ax2.scatter(df["Customer_Acquisition_Cost_USD"],
                df["Net_Promoter_Score"], color="#1E293B", s=80)
    ax2.set_title("Loyalty (NPS) vs Cost per New Customer (CAC)")
    ax2.set_xlabel("CAC (USD)")
    ax2.set_ylabel("Net Promoter Score")
    ax2.grid(True, linestyle=":")

    # 3. Output: save the dashboard
    plt.tight_layout()
    fig.savefig("data/executive_dashboard_2panel.png", dpi=200)
    print("Dashboard saved: data/executive_dashboard_2panel.png")
    ```
    * `plt.subplots(1, 2)` makes 1 row with 2 charts, and `(ax1, ax2)` names them left and right.
    * `ax.scatter(x, y)` draws one dot per month; `s=80` sets the dot size.
  * *Step 3: Inspect Output:* Open `data/executive_dashboard_2panel.png` in Panel `[1]` and check that titles and axis labels do not overlap.
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:* Run `python YourName_S22_dashboard.py` in Panel `[4]` and confirm return code `0`.
  * *Success Check:* the image has 2 charts. The right chart's dots climb from bottom-left to top-right: months with a higher cost per new customer also had higher loyalty, which matches their correlation of `0.98`.

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Four-Panel Boardroom Dashboard:**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In `YourName_S22_practice.py`, build a 2x2 dashboard with 4 charts: revenue trend (line), marketing spend by month (bar), cost per new customer by month (line), and the correlation heatmap.
  * *Task 1 (2x2 Layout):* Create the grid with `fig, axes = plt.subplots(2, 2, figsize=(16, 10))`. Draw each chart on its own position: `axes[0, 0]`, `axes[0, 1]`, `axes[1, 0]`, and `axes[1, 1]`. Pass `ax=axes[1, 1]` to `sns.heatmap()`.
  * *Task 2 (Deliberate IndexError & AI Diagnosis):* Change one position to `axes[2].plot(...)`. Run the script and read `IndexError: index 2 is out of bounds for axis 0 with size 2`. Capture the traceback with `Win + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and review the AI's explanation of row and column numbers.
  * *Task 3 (Fix and Export):* Fix the position, call `plt.tight_layout()`, and save the dashboard to `data/boardroom_dashboard.png` with `dpi=300`.
  * *Success Check:* `data/boardroom_dashboard.png` shows 4 charts with no overlapping titles, and the script ends with return code `0`.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - Is Marketing Getting Less Efficient?:**
  * *Goal:* Answer the CMO's question with a new measure and one honest chart: does each marketing dollar still bring in as much revenue at the end of the year as at the start?
  * *What you need:*
    * File: `data/session_02_02_dataset.csv` (described in **5. Lab Practice Dataset**).
    * Script name: `YourName_S22_challenge.py`.
    * Tools you already know: column math, `.round()`, `.mean()`, `.sort_values()`, a `for` loop with `if` / `else` (Session 1.2), `fig, ax = plt.subplots()`, and `fig.savefig()`.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. Add `Revenue_per_Marketing_USD` = `Organic_Revenue_USD_M` / `Paid_Marketing_Spend_USD_M`, rounded to 2 decimal places. It shows how many dollars of revenue came with each dollar of paid marketing in that month.
    2. Compute the 12-month average of that column and round it to 2 decimal places.
    3. Build a list of 12 colors with a `for` loop: `"#991B1B"` (red) for months **below** the average, `"#94A3B8"` (grey) for all others.
    4. Draw a bar chart with `ax.bar()`: month names on the x-axis, `Revenue_per_Marketing_USD` as the bar heights, and your color list.
    5. Add a dashed horizontal line at the average, then a title that states your finding and a y-axis label with its unit ("USD of revenue per USD of marketing").
    6. Save the chart to `data/marketing_efficiency.png` with `dpi=150`.
    7. Print the average, then the best and the worst month with their values.
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * `ax.bar(x_values, heights, color=color_list)`: Matplotlib's own bar chart. When `color` is a list, bar 1 gets color 1, bar 2 gets color 2, and so on.
    * `ax.axhline(y=5.08, linestyle="--", color="black")`: draws a horizontal line across the chart at height `y`, useful for marking an average or a target.
    * Building a list in a loop: start with `colors = []`, then `colors.append("#991B1B")` adds one item to the end.
  * *Hints:*
    * Loop over the column itself: `for value in df["Revenue_per_Marketing_USD"]:`.
    * For the best and worst month, sort the DataFrame by the new column and read the first and last rows, for example with `.head(1)` and `.tail(1)`.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Average: `5.08`. Best month: `Jan` (`5.9`). Worst month: `Dec` (`4.03`).
    * The 6 red bars are `Jul` through `Dec`: every month in the second half of the year is below average.
    * Business meaning: spending rose faster than revenue. Revenue doubled, but each marketing dollar brought in about 32% less revenue in December than in January, and the cost per new customer rose from `48.5` to `68.0`. The CMO should ask whether the November and December campaigns were worth their extra cost.
