<a id="session-02-03"></a>
### Session 2.3: Midterm Applied Analytics Formulation Part 1: Problem Structuring and Data Audit

![Session 2.3 Map](assets/session_02_03_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_02_03_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_02_03_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 7 (115/10/25 - 115/10/31) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **1. Session Purpose:**
  * An IT (Information Technology) services company holds 15 enterprise client contracts worth $10.7 million a year
  * The account director has a vague worry: "some clients are unhappy and may leave", but a worry is not something data can test
  * The account director asks: which contracts are at risk of leaving, and how much revenue is at stake? Today, you turn that worry into a testable question, check that the data can be trusted, and measure the starting point. This is step 1 of your midterm project

* **2. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "What exactly should we test?": turn a vague business problem into a testable hypothesis (a clear statement that data can support or prove wrong)
  2. "Can we trust this data?": audit a dataset for missing values (`.isnull()`), duplicates (`.duplicated()`), and impossible values, such as a percentage above 100
  3. "How do we stop bad data automatically?": write `assert` checks (rules the data must pass) and handle an `AssertionError` with `try` / `except`

* **3. Business Context: From a Vague Worry to a Testable Question:**
  * Managers rarely hand you a clean question. A useful analysis moves through four steps:

    | Step | Example |
    |---|---|
    | Vague worry | "Some clients are unhappy and may leave." |
    | Business question | "Is poor service linked to clients at risk of leaving?" |
    | Testable hypothesis | "Accounts below 95% SLA compliance have more billing disputes and a higher retention risk than other accounts." |
    | Measure today (baseline) | "Revenue in High-risk accounts today: $930,000." |

  * SLA (Service Level Agreement): the service promise in a contract, such as "the system is available 99% of the time". SLA compliance is the share of that promise actually delivered.
  * Baseline: today's value of the measure you care about. Without it, nobody can prove later that an action improved anything.
  * **Midterm Project, Step 1 of 2: your Question Card.** Choose a business problem with a dataset you can audit (for example, any dataset from Sessions 1.3 to 2.2), and fill in five lines:
    1. Vague worry: what the manager is concerned about
    2. Business question: what decision the answer supports
    3. Hypothesis: one sentence the data can support or prove wrong
    4. Data and audit: the file you use, and the result of the three checks in section 4
    5. Baseline: one number that describes today
  * Bring your completed Question Card and your audit script to Session 2.4, where you present your findings.

* **4. Data Basics: Three Checks Before Any Analysis:**
  * Bad data produces confident but wrong answers ("garbage in, garbage out"). Run three checks before you analyze:

    | Check | Question | pandas |
    |---|---|---|
    | Completeness | Are any values missing? | `df.isnull().sum()` |
    | Uniqueness | Does each ID appear only once? | `df["Account_ID"].duplicated().sum()` |
    | Validity | Are all values possible? | `(df["SLA_Compliance_Pct"] > 100).sum()` |

  * `NaN` ("Not a Number") is how pandas shows an empty cell. `.isnull()` marks each `NaN` as `True`.
  * A rule for validity comes from business logic: a percentage must be between 0 and 100, and a contract value must be above 0.

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * Two versions of the same 15 client contracts (company names are fictional):
    * Clean file: [data/session_02_03_dataset.csv](data/session_02_03_dataset.csv), 15 rows that pass every check. Use it for analysis.
    * Raw file: [data/session_02_03_raw.csv](data/session_02_03_raw.csv), 16 rows as they arrived from the billing system, with a few problems for you to find. Use it for auditing.
  * Columns (the same in both files):
    * `Account_ID`: Unique client account code (e.g., `ACC-301`).
    * `Enterprise_Client`: Client company name (Titan Tech, Beacon Health, Vanguard Retail, and others). One company can hold several accounts.
    * `Contract_Value_USD`: Yearly contract value in U.S. Dollars.
    * `SLA_Compliance_Pct`: Share of the service promise delivered, in percent.
    * `Billing_Discrepancy_Count`: Number of billing disputes (invoices the client challenged) during the contract.
    * `Client_Retention_Risk`: The account team's rating of how likely the client is to leave: `Low`, `Medium`, or `High`.

* **6. Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `Hypothesis`:
    * What: one clear statement that data can support or prove wrong
    * Why: turns a vague worry into a test with a yes or no answer
  * `Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA)`:
    * What: a first look at the data with counts, averages, and charts before any formal test
    * Why: shows what the data can answer, and where it looks strange
  * `Data Audit`:
    * What: the three checks: completeness, uniqueness, and validity
    * Why: one bad row can change a total by millions
  * `Baseline`:
    * What: today's value of the measure you care about
    * Why: the starting point for proving any later improvement
  * `assert`:
    * What: a line that stops the script with an `AssertionError` when a rule is broken, such as `assert (df["Contract_Value_USD"] > 0).all()`
    * Why: bad data is caught at the door, before it reaches a report
  * `try` / `except`:
    * What: runs code in `try`; if a chosen error happens, the code in `except` runs instead of stopping the script
    * Why: the script can report the problem clearly and keep going

* **7. Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * **7.1 From a worry to a hypothesis you can test**
    * *The idea:* A hypothesis is one sentence that data can support or prove wrong. A useful hypothesis names what you measure, the groups you compare, and the difference you expect. "Some clients are unhappy" cannot be tested; "accounts below 95% SLA compliance have more billing disputes than the others" can, because the data has a column for each part.
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Part of the hypothesis | In our data |
      |---|---|
      | What is measured | `Billing_Discrepancy_Count` |
      | The two groups | SLA below 95% (`4` accounts) vs 95% or above (`11` accounts) |
      | The expected difference | the first group has more disputes |
      | The result | `4.75` vs `0.64` disputes on average: supported |

    * *Common mistake:* a hypothesis that cannot fail, such as "service quality matters". If no result could ever prove it wrong, it does not help anyone decide.
    * *More examples:*

      | Vague worry | Testable hypothesis | Column needed |
      |---|---|---|
      | "Our big clients are risky." | Accounts above $800,000 have a higher share of High risk ratings. | `Contract_Value_USD`, `Client_Retention_Risk` |
      | "Billing is a mess." | Accounts with 3 or more disputes are rated Medium or High risk. | `Billing_Discrepancy_Count`, `Client_Retention_Risk` |
      | "Service is slipping." | High-risk accounts have a lower average SLA than Low-risk accounts. | `SLA_Compliance_Pct`, `Client_Retention_Risk` |

      In our data, the third one is supported (High-risk accounts average `90.97`% SLA, Low-risk `98.84`%), while the first is not: none of the 6 accounts above $800,000 is rated High risk. A hypothesis that turns out wrong is still useful: it rules out a wrong explanation.
    * *Remember:* a good hypothesis names the measure, the groups, and the expected difference.
  * **7.2 The three data checks**
    * *The idea:* Before any analysis, check that the data is complete (no missing values), unique (no record counted twice), and valid (every value is possible). Each check is a rule on a column that gives `True` / `False` per row; `.sum()` counts the `True` values, because Python counts `True` as 1.
    * *See it on real data:* the raw file (16 rows).

      | Check | Code | Result | The problem |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | Completeness | `raw.isnull().sum().sum()` | `1` | `ACC-306` has no SLA value |
      | Uniqueness | `raw["Account_ID"].duplicated().sum()` | `1` | `ACC-304` appears twice |
      | Validity | `(raw["SLA_Compliance_Pct"] > 100).sum()` | `1` | `ACC-311` shows `105.0`% |
      | Validity | `(raw["Contract_Value_USD"] <= 0).sum()` | `1` | `ACC-313` has a negative value |

    * *Why it matters:* these 4 small problems change the totals. The raw file adds up to `$11,290,000` of contracts; the corrected file to `$10,710,000`. The average SLA looks like `97.45`% in the raw file but is really `96.48`%.
    * *Common mistake:* `.duplicated()` marks only the **second** copy as `True`. To see both copies of `ACC-304`, use `raw.duplicated("Account_ID", keep=False)`.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `raw.isnull().sum()` | `1` in `SLA_Compliance_Pct`, `0` elsewhere | which column has the gap |
      | `raw.loc[raw["SLA_Compliance_Pct"].isnull(), "Account_ID"]` | `ACC-306` | which account has the gap |
      | `raw["Account_ID"].is_unique` | `False` | a one-word answer to "are the IDs unique?" |
      | `raw.loc[raw["SLA_Compliance_Pct"] > 100, "Account_ID"]` | `ACC-311` | which account has an impossible percentage |
      | `(~raw["SLA_Compliance_Pct"].between(0, 100)).sum()` | `2` | `.between()` treats a missing value as outside the range too |

    * *Remember:* complete, unique, valid; check all three before you calculate.
  * **7.3 Rules the data must pass: `assert`**
    * *The idea:* `assert rule, "message"` checks a rule. If the rule is `True`, nothing happens and the script continues. If it is `False`, the script stops with an `AssertionError` that shows your message. Rules turn your data checks into a gate: bad data cannot reach the report unnoticed.
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Code | On the raw file | On the clean file |
      |---|---|---|
      | `assert (df["Contract_Value_USD"] > 0).all(), "Contract value must be above 0"` | stops: `AssertionError: Contract value must be above 0` | passes |
      | `assert df["Account_ID"].is_unique, "Account IDs must be unique"` | stops | passes |

    * *`.all()` and `.any()`:* a rule on a column gives many `True` / `False` values, but `assert` needs one answer. `.all()` is `True` only if every value is `True`; `.any()` is `True` if at least one is.
    * *Common mistake:* leaving out the message. `assert rule` alone stops with an empty `AssertionError`, and the reader has to guess which rule failed.
    * *More examples:*

      | Rule | On the raw file | On the clean file |
      |---|---|---|
      | `assert (df["SLA_Compliance_Pct"] <= 100).all()` | fails (`105.0`, and a missing value counts as `False`) | passes |
      | `assert len(df) == 15` | fails (16 rows) | passes |
      | `assert df["Client_Retention_Risk"].isin(["Low", "Medium", "High"]).all()` | passes | passes |
      | `assert not df.isnull().any().any()` | fails (1 gap) | passes |

    * *Remember:* one `assert` per rule, always with a message.
  * **7.4 Handling a failure: `try` / `except`**
    * *The idea:* Sometimes you want the script to report the problem and keep going, for example to finish a cleaning step. Put the risky lines under `try:`. If an `AssertionError` happens, Python jumps to the lines under `except AssertionError as error:` instead of stopping, and `error` holds the message.

      ```text
      try:
          assert (raw["Contract_Value_USD"] > 0).all(), "Contract value must be above 0"
      except AssertionError as error:
          print("Data problem:", error)
      ```

      On the raw file this prints `Data problem: Contract value must be above 0`, and the script continues.
    * *Common mistake:* catching every error with a bare `except:`. It also hides typos and real bugs. Name the error you expect.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code inside `try:` | Error raised | The matching `except` line |
      |---|---|---|
      | `int("abc")` | `ValueError: invalid literal for int() with base 10: 'abc'` | `except ValueError:` |
      | `df["Missing"]` | `KeyError: 'Missing'` | `except KeyError:` |
      | `1 / 0` | `ZeroDivisionError: division by zero` | `except ZeroDivisionError:` |
      | `assert False, "Bad data"` | `AssertionError: Bad data` | `except AssertionError:` |

    * *Remember:* `try` the check, `except` the expected error, and report it clearly.
  * **7.5 The baseline: today's number**
    * *The idea:* A baseline is the value of your measure today. It is the starting point that any later improvement is compared with. Without it, nobody can prove that an action worked.
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Baseline | Value today |
      |---|---|
      | Revenue in High-risk accounts | `$930,000` |
      | Share of all revenue at High risk | `8.7`% |
      | Accounts below 95% SLA | `4` of `15` (`26.7`%) |
      | Billing disputes in total | `26` |

      If the company improves service next quarter and the High-risk revenue falls to, say, $500,000, the baseline shows the size of the improvement.
    * *Common mistake:* measuring the baseline on raw data. With the raw file, High-risk revenue looks like `$310,000` (a negative typo hides `$620,000`): any later "improvement" would be measured from a false start.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `df["SLA_Compliance_Pct"].mean()` | `96.48` | average service level today |
      | `df["Billing_Discrepancy_Count"].mean()` | `1.73` | average disputes per account |
      | `len(df[df["Client_Retention_Risk"] == "High"])` | `3` | accounts rated High risk |
      | share of revenue in Low-risk accounts | `72.5`% | the safe part of the portfolio |

    * *Remember:* audit first, then record the baseline.

* **8. Development Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE:**
  * `[1]` File Explorer: create your script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S23_demo_1.py`), and check both files are in `data/`.
  * `[2]` Code Editor: write a few lines, run, repeat. Small steps keep errors small.
  * `[3]` AI Agent Chat: on an error, capture the traceback (Python's error report) with `Win + Shift + S` (macOS: `Cmd + Shift + 4`), paste it with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why it failed.
  * `[4]` Terminal: run `python` followed by your script name. Return code `0` means the script finished with no errors.

* **9. Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * Every example has three parts: **1. Input**, **2. Process**, and **3. Output**. Name each file with your own name and the session code, for example `Reacher_S23_demo_1.py` (pattern: `YourName_S23_demo_1.py`).
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Audit the Raw File):* `YourName_S23_demo_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the raw file, as it arrived
    import pandas as pd
    raw = pd.read_csv("data/session_02_03_raw.csv")

    # 2. Process: the three checks, as counts
    missing_values = raw.isnull().sum().sum()
    duplicate_ids = raw["Account_ID"].duplicated().sum()
    sla_above_100 = (raw["SLA_Compliance_Pct"] > 100).sum()
    value_not_positive = (raw["Contract_Value_USD"] <= 0).sum()

    # 3. Output: one line per check
    print("Rows:", len(raw))
    print("Missing values:", missing_values)
    print("Duplicate account IDs:", duplicate_ids)
    print("SLA above 100%:", sla_above_100)
    print("Contract value of 0 or less:", value_not_positive)
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * The script reads the **raw** file, as it came from the billing system, before anything is cleaned.
      * `raw.isnull()` marks every empty cell `True`. The first `.sum()` counts per column, and the second adds the columns together (section 7.2).
      * `raw["Account_ID"].duplicated()` marks a row `True` when its ID already appeared above; `.sum()` counts them.
      * `(raw["SLA_Compliance_Pct"] > 100)` and `(raw["Contract_Value_USD"] <= 0)` mark impossible values; `.sum()` counts them.
      * Part 3 prints one line per check, so the report reads like a checklist.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Rows: 16
      Missing values: 1
      Duplicate account IDs: 1
      SLA above 100%: 1
      Contract value of 0 or less: 1
      ```

      * The file has 16 rows, but only 15 real contracts: one row is a copy.
      * Each check finds exactly 1 problem, 4 in total. Section 7.2 shows which accounts they are.
      * None of these problems raises an error by itself: the file loads normally. Only the checks reveal them.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`.drop_duplicates()`):*
    * New tool: `raw.drop_duplicates()` returns the table without rows that exactly repeat an earlier row. Why: a copied row counts the same contract twice.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print("Rows after removing duplicates:", len(raw.________________()))`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `Rows after removing duplicates: 15`.
  * *Micro-Example 2 (Test the Hypothesis on the Clean File):* `YourName_S23_demo_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the clean file
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_02_03_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: mark SLA breaches, then compare the two groups
    df["SLA_Breach"] = df["SLA_Compliance_Pct"] < 95.0
    comparison = df.groupby("SLA_Breach").agg(
        Accounts=("Account_ID", "count"),
        Avg_Disputes=("Billing_Discrepancy_Count", "mean"),
    ).round(2)

    # 3. Output: breach (True) vs no breach (False)
    print(comparison)
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * The script reads the **clean** file: analysis is done on data that passed the checks.
      * `df["SLA_Compliance_Pct"] < 95.0` marks each account `True` (breach) or `False`, and stores the result as a new column, `SLA_Breach`.
      * `df.groupby("SLA_Breach")` splits the accounts into the 2 groups (Session 1.4). `.agg()` counts the accounts and averages the disputes in each group.
      * `.round(2)` keeps 2 decimals.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
                  Accounts  Avg_Disputes
      SLA_Breach
      False             11          0.64
      True               4          4.75
      ```

      * The 4 accounts below the 95% promise average `4.75` billing disputes; the other 11 average `0.64`, about 7 times fewer.
      * The first part of the hypothesis is supported: poor service and billing disputes go together. Quick Check 2 tests the second part, the link with retention risk.
  * *Quick Check 2 (`pd.crosstab()`):*
    * New tool: `pd.crosstab(column_a, column_b)` counts the rows for every pair of values, such as breach yes or no against risk Low, Medium, or High. Why: tests the second half of the hypothesis in one table.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(pd.________(df["SLA_Breach"], df["Client_Retention_Risk"]))`
    * Check: in the `True` row, `High` is `3` and `Medium` is `1`: 3 of the 4 breached accounts are rated High risk, and no non-breached account is.
  * *Micro-Example 3 (Measure the Baseline):* `YourName_S23_demo_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the clean file
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_02_03_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: contract value by risk rating, and the High-risk share
    value_by_risk = df.groupby("Client_Retention_Risk")["Contract_Value_USD"].sum()
    high_risk_value = value_by_risk["High"]
    high_risk_share = high_risk_value / df["Contract_Value_USD"].sum() * 100

    # 3. Output: today's baseline
    print(value_by_risk)
    print("High-risk share of revenue (%):", round(high_risk_share, 1))
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `df.groupby("Client_Retention_Risk")["Contract_Value_USD"].sum()` adds up contract value for each risk rating. Choosing one column after `groupby()` gives a simple list of totals instead of a table.
      * `value_by_risk["High"]` reads one value from that list, by its label.
      * The share divides the High-risk total by all contract value and multiplies by 100.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Client_Retention_Risk
      High       930000.0
      Low       7770000.0
      Medium    2010000.0
      Name: Contract_Value_USD, dtype: float64
      High-risk share of revenue (%): 8.7
      ```

      * `$930,000` a year, `8.7`% of revenue, sits in accounts rated High risk. This is the baseline (section 7.5).
      * Most revenue (`$7,770,000`) is in Low-risk accounts, so the business is not in immediate danger, but the 3 High-risk accounts are where action pays off first.
      * The last line of the list, `Name: ... dtype: float64`, is pandas describing the list: its name and its type. It is not data.
  * *Quick Check 3 (f-string number format):*
    * New tool: `f"${value:,.0f}"` writes a number with thousands separators and no decimals, such as `$930,000`. Why: managers read money faster in this form.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(f"Revenue at High risk: ${high_risk_value:___}")`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `Revenue at High risk: $930,000`.

* **10. Official Python Documentation & References:**
  * [Python Tutorial: Errors and Exceptions (`try` / `except`)](https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/errors.html).
  * [Python Reference: The `assert` Statement](https://docs.python.org/3/reference/simple_stmts.html#the-assert-statement).
  * [Pandas User Guide: Working with Missing Data](https://pandas.pydata.org/docs/user_guide/missing_data.html).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Hypothesis Test Script:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S23_hypothesis.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S23_hypothesis.py`).
  * *Step 2: Test Two Hypotheses and Measure the Baseline:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the clean file
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_02_03_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: two tests and one baseline
    # Hypothesis 1: higher-risk accounts have more billing disputes
    disputes_by_risk = (
        df.groupby("Client_Retention_Risk")["Billing_Discrepancy_Count"]
        .mean().round(2)
    )
    # Hypothesis 2: lower SLA compliance goes with more disputes
    sla_vs_disputes = df["SLA_Compliance_Pct"].corr(
        df["Billing_Discrepancy_Count"]
    )
    # Baseline: revenue in High-risk accounts today
    high_risk_value = df.loc[
        df["Client_Retention_Risk"] == "High", "Contract_Value_USD"
    ].sum()

    # 3. Output: a short report
    print("Hypothesis 1: average disputes by risk rating\n", disputes_by_risk)
    print(f"Hypothesis 2: correlation of SLA and disputes: {sla_vs_disputes:.3f}")
    print(f"Baseline: revenue at High risk: ${high_risk_value:,.0f}")
    ```
    * `df.loc[rows, "Column_Name"]` selects the rows and one column at the same time.
    * `{sla_vs_disputes:.3f}` shows the number with 3 decimals.
  * *Step 3: Interpret:* Write two comment lines at the end of the script: does each result support its hypothesis?
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:* Run `python YourName_S23_hypothesis.py` in Panel `[4]` and confirm return code `0`.
  * *Success Check:* average disputes are `5.33` for High, `2.0` for Medium, and `0.25` for Low; the correlation is `-0.982` (lower SLA goes with more disputes); the baseline is `$930,000`. Both hypotheses are supported.

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Data Gatekeeper:**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In `YourName_S23_practice.py`, write checks that stop bad data, then clean the raw file so it passes.
  * *Task 1 (Write the Rules):* Load `data/session_02_03_raw.csv`. Write three `assert` lines with messages:
    * `assert raw["SLA_Compliance_Pct"].between(0, 100).all(), "SLA must be between 0 and 100"`
    * `assert (raw["Contract_Value_USD"] > 0).all(), "Contract value must be above 0"`
    * `assert not raw["Account_ID"].duplicated().any(), "Account IDs must be unique"`
    * `.between(0, 100)` is `True` when a value is from 0 to 100; a missing value counts as `False`.
  * *Task 2 (Read the AssertionError & AI Diagnosis):* Run the script. It stops at the first rule with `AssertionError: SLA must be between 0 and 100`. Capture the traceback with `Win + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and ask which rows break the rule.
  * *Task 3 (Clean, Re-check, Save):* Put the three `assert` lines inside `try:` and print the message under `except AssertionError as error:`. Then build `clean` from `raw`: remove duplicate rows with `.drop_duplicates()`, keep only rows where the SLA is between 0 and 100, and keep only rows where the contract value is above 0. Run the three checks again on `clean`, and save it to `data/contracts_clean.csv` with `index=False`.
  * *Success Check:* the first run prints `SLA must be between 0 and 100`; `clean` has `12` rows and passes all three checks; the script ends with return code `0`.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - How Much Did Bad Data Hide?:**
  * *Goal:* Show the account director, in numbers, how the raw file's problems distort the risk report, and list exactly which accounts the data team must fix.
  * *What you need:*
    * Files: `data/session_02_03_raw.csv` (with problems) and `data/session_02_03_dataset.csv` (the corrected version of the same contracts).
    * Script name: `YourName_S23_challenge.py`.
    * Tools you already know: the three checks, `groupby()`, `.sum()`, Boolean masks with `|`, `.loc[]`, `pd.DataFrame()`, and f-strings.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. Write a function `audit(table)` that returns a dictionary with four counts: `"missing"`, `"duplicate_ids"`, `"sla_out_of_range"` (SLA above 100), and `"value_not_positive"` (contract value of 0 or less).
    2. Call `audit()` on both files and print both dictionaries.
    3. Make a list of every `Account_ID` in the raw file that breaks at least one rule. Each account should appear only once, sorted from smallest to largest.
    4. For each file, sum `Contract_Value_USD` by `Client_Retention_Risk`. Put the two results side by side in one DataFrame with the columns `Raw_USD` and `Correct_USD`, and add `Difference_USD` = `Raw_USD` - `Correct_USD`.
    5. Print the table and one sentence that states the biggest distortion in plain words, using an f-string with thousands separators.
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * Defining a function: `def audit(table):` followed by indented lines, ending with `return {...}`. Calling `audit(raw)` runs those lines on `raw` and gives back the dictionary.
    * `|` means "or" for masks: `(rule_1) | (rule_2)` is `True` when at least one rule is `True`. `.isnull()` on one column marks its missing values.
    * Unique, sorted values: `sorted(set(raw.loc[mask, "Account_ID"]))`. `set()` keeps each value once; `sorted()` puts them in order.
    * Side-by-side columns: `pd.DataFrame({"Raw_USD": raw_sums, "Correct_USD": correct_sums})`, where both sums come from `groupby()`, lines up the values by risk rating.
  * *Hints:*
    * For requirement 3, build one mask that is `True` for a duplicate, a missing value, an SLA above 100, or a value of 0 or less. Use `raw.duplicated("Account_ID", keep=False)` so that both copies of a duplicated account are marked.
    * Print each intermediate result before you combine them.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Raw file: `{"missing": 1, "duplicate_ids": 1, "sla_out_of_range": 1, "value_not_positive": 1}`; clean file: all four counts are `0`.
    * Accounts to fix: `['ACC-304', 'ACC-306', 'ACC-311', 'ACC-313']`.
    * Differences: `High` `-620000.0`, `Low` `1200000.0`, `Medium` `0.0`.
    * Business meaning: one negative typo (`ACC-313`) made High-risk revenue look like `$310,000` instead of `$930,000`, hiding two-thirds of the real risk, while one duplicated row added `$1,200,000` to Low risk. The data must be fixed before anyone acts on the risk report.

<a id="session-02-04"></a>
### Session 2.4: Midterm Analytics Pipeline Defense Part 2: Exploratory Findings and Executive Review

![Session 2.4 Map](assets/session_02_04_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_02_04_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_02_04_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 8 (115/11/01 - 115/11/07) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **1. Session Purpose:**
  * An online marketplace earns a fee on every sale its sellers make, in three regions: North America, Europe, and Asia Pacific
  * Big sales are not the same as cash in the bank: the region that sells the most may not be the one that generates the most cash
  * The CFO (Chief Financial Officer) asks: which region turns its sales into the most cash, and where should we invest next year? Today, you answer with one script that anyone can rerun, and present your midterm findings in the same way

* **2. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "Can someone else get the same numbers?": build a reproducible pipeline (one script that goes from the raw file to the final report with no manual steps), organized into functions
  2. "Which region turns sales into the most cash?": calculate revenue and the operating cash flow margin by region and quarter
  3. "How do I explain it to the CFO?": write findings as finding, evidence, and recommendation; review a classmate's script; and fix a `TypeError` (mixing text and numbers)

* **3. Business Context: How a Marketplace Makes Money and Cash:**
  * Four terms, one example (North America, 2024-Q1):

    | Term | Meaning | Example |
    |---|---|---|
    | GMV (Gross Merchandise Value) | the total value of everything sold on the platform | `145.2` USD M |
    | Take rate | the share of GMV the platform keeps as its fee | `12.5`% |
    | Revenue | GMV x take rate / 100: the platform's own income | `18.15` USD M |
    | OCF (Operating Cash Flow) | the cash the business actually generated after paying its operating costs | `5.6` USD M |

  * OCF margin = OCF / revenue x 100. It answers "of every dollar of revenue, how many cents became cash?" Example: `5.6 / 18.15 x 100 = 30.9`%.
  * **Midterm Project, Step 2 of 2: present your findings.** In a short presentation, walk through:
    1. Your Question Card from Session 2.3 (worry, question, hypothesis, data, baseline)
    2. Your audit result: what you checked and what you fixed
    3. Your findings, each written in three parts:
       * **Finding:** one sentence a manager can repeat. "North America turns the most revenue into cash."
       * **Evidence:** the number behind it. "Its OCF margin is 31.0%, against 24.7% in Europe and 18.5% in Asia Pacific."
       * **Recommendation:** what to do. "Study North America's cost structure before expanding in Asia Pacific."
    4. A live run of your script, from the raw file to the final report

* **4. Data Basics: What Makes a Pipeline Reproducible:**
  * A reproducible pipeline runs from top to bottom and produces the same numbers on any computer. It has four stages:

    | Stage | What it does | Example in today's script |
    |---|---|---|
    | Load | Read the raw file | `pd.read_csv("data/session_02_04_dataset.csv")` |
    | Check | Stop if the data breaks a rule | `assert (df["Gross_Merchandise_Value_USD_M"] > 0).all()` |
    | Calculate | Add the measures | revenue and OCF margin |
    | Report | Print and save the result | `summary.to_csv("data/region_summary.csv")` |

  * Three habits that make a script reproducible:
    * Use relative paths such as `data/...`, never paths that only exist on your computer, such as `C:/Users/Reacher/Desktop/...`
    * Calculate every number from the data; never type a result into the code by hand
    * Before presenting, delete your output files and run the script again from the start

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * File Link: [data/session_02_04_dataset.csv](data/session_02_04_dataset.csv)
  * Audited 12-row quarterly marketplace dataset (fictional teaching data): 3 regions x 4 quarters, one row per region and quarter:
    * `Quarter_Code`: Reporting quarter, `2024-Q1` through `2024-Q4`.
    * `Market_Segment`: Region: `North_America`, `Europe`, or `Asia_Pacific`.
    * `Gross_Merchandise_Value_USD_M`: GMV (Gross Merchandise Value) in millions of U.S. Dollars.
    * `Platform_Take_Rate_Pct`: The platform's fee as a percentage of GMV.
    * `Operating_Cash_Flow_USD_M`: OCF (Operating Cash Flow) in millions of U.S. Dollars.

* **6. Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `Reproducible Pipeline`:
    * What: one script that goes from the raw file to the final report, with no manual steps
    * Why: anyone can rerun it and check your numbers
  * `Function` (`def` / `return`):
    * What: a named block of code that takes inputs and gives back a result
    * Why: each pipeline stage gets a clear name, and you can reuse it
  * `Relative Path`:
    * What: a file path that starts from the project folder, such as `data/session_02_04_dataset.csv`
    * Why: works on every computer that has the project folder
  * `Data Type` and `TypeError`:
    * What: text (`str`) and numbers (`float`, `int`) are different types; `"Total: " + 204.3` raises a `TypeError`
    * Why: printing a result is where this mistake happens most
  * `Executive Finding`:
    * What: a finding, its evidence, and a recommendation
    * Why: managers act on clear statements backed by numbers, not on code
  * `Peer Review`:
    * What: a classmate checks your script against a short checklist
    * Why: a second reader catches mistakes the author no longer sees

* **7. Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * **7.1 Functions: name a step, then reuse it**
    * *The idea:* A function is a named block of code. You give it inputs (parameters), it does its work, and `return` hands back the result. Once a step is a function, you can run it again with one line, on any data, and a reader can understand the pipeline from the function names alone.

      ```text
      def revenue_usd_m(gmv, take_rate_pct):
          return gmv * take_rate_pct / 100
      ```

      `def` starts the function, the name follows, then the parameters in brackets and a colon. The indented lines are the body.
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Call | Result | What happened |
      |---|---|---|
      | `revenue_usd_m(145.2, 12.5)` | `18.15` | North America, Q1: `145.2 x 12.5 / 100` |
      | `revenue_usd_m(160.8, 13.0)` | `20.9` (rounded) | North America, Q4: same function, new inputs |
      | `revenue_usd_m(df["Gross_Merchandise_Value_USD_M"], df["Platform_Take_Rate_Pct"])` | 12 values | the same function works on whole columns |

    * *Common mistake 1, forgetting `return`:* a function without `return` gives back `None`, Python's "nothing". The calculation happens, but the result is thrown away.
    * *Common mistake 2, using an inside name outside:* a variable created inside a function exists only there. After `def f(x): y = x * 2`, the line `print(y)` outside the function raises `NameError: name 'y' is not defined`. Use `return y` and store the result: `y = f(3)`.
    * *More examples:*

      | Function | Call | Result |
      |---|---|---|
      | `def ocf_margin(ocf, revenue): return round(ocf / revenue * 100, 1)` | `ocf_margin(5.6, 18.15)` | `30.9` |
      | `def load_data(path): return pd.read_csv(path)` | `load_data("data/session_02_04_dataset.csv")` | the 12-row DataFrame |
      | `def is_weak(margin, limit=20): return margin < limit` | `is_weak(18.6)` | `True`; `limit` uses its default value, `20` |
      | the same | `is_weak(18.6, limit=15)` | `False`; a different limit passed in |

    * *Remember:* `def` to define, `return` to hand back, and store the result when you call it.
  * **7.2 A reproducible pipeline: load, check, calculate, report**
    * *The idea:* A pipeline is one script that goes from the raw file to the final report with no manual step in between. "Reproducible" means anyone can run it again, on any computer, and get exactly the same numbers. That is what lets a manager trust your midterm results.

      | Stage | Function in Micro-Example 2 | Why it is separate |
      |---|---|---|
      | Load and check | `load_data(path)` | bad data stops here, before any number is calculated |
      | Calculate | `add_metrics(df)` | one place for every formula |
      | Summarize | `summarize(df)` | the table the manager reads |
      | Report | `print(summary)` and `.to_csv()` | the result on screen and in a file |

    * *Three habits:* use relative paths such as `data/...` (a path like `C:/Users/Reacher/Desktop/...` only exists on one computer); calculate every number from the data, never type a result by hand; before you present, delete the output files and run the script again from the top.
    * *Common mistake:* running cells or lines out of order while developing, so the script works on your screen but fails when run from the start. The test of a pipeline is a clean run, top to bottom.
    * *More examples:*

      | Path or habit | Works on | Verdict |
      |---|---|---|
      | `"data/session_02_04_dataset.csv"` | every computer with the project folder | relative path: use this |
      | `"C:/Users/Reacher/Desktop/data.csv"` | only Reacher's computer | absolute path: breaks for everyone else |
      | a total typed into the code as `204.3` | only until the data changes | hard-coded result: calculate it instead |
      | output files left from an earlier run | hides missing steps | delete outputs, then rerun from the top |

    * *Remember:* one script, top to bottom, same numbers every time.
  * **7.3 Text and numbers do not mix: `TypeError`**
    * *The idea:* Python keeps text (`str`) and numbers (`int`, `float`) apart. `+` adds two numbers or joins two texts, but it refuses to combine a text with a number, because it cannot know whether you want to add or to join.
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Code | Result |
      |---|---|
      | `"Total: " + 204.3` | `TypeError: can only concatenate str (not "float") to str` |
      | `"Rows: " + len(df)` | `TypeError: can only concatenate str (not "int") to str` |
      | `"Total: " + str(204.3)` | `Total: 204.3` |
      | `f"Total: {204.3}"` | `Total: 204.3` |

      "Concatenate" means "join texts together".
    * *Formatting numbers inside f-strings:* after the value, a colon and a format code control how the number looks.

      | Code | Result | Format |
      |---|---|---|
      | `f"{204.3049:.2f}"` | `204.30` | 2 decimals |
      | `f"{1234567.8:,.0f}"` | `1,234,568` | thousands separators, no decimals |
      | `f"{0.2467:.1%}"` | `24.7%` | a share shown as a percentage |

    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `str(12) + " stores"` | `12 stores` | convert the number to text first |
      | `f"{12} rows"` | `12 rows` | an f-string converts for you |
      | `"Q" + str(4)` | `Q4` | build a label from text and a number |
      | `int("12") + 1` | `13` | convert text to a number before adding |
      | `float("18.15") * 2` | `36.3` | decimal text becomes a `float` |
      | `"5" * 3` | `555` | a surprise: text times a number repeats the text |

    * *Remember:* put numbers into text with an f-string, and format them for the reader.
  * **7.4 Hard-coded numbers: believable but wrong**
    * *The idea:* A hard-coded number is a value typed directly into the code, such as `0.125` for the take rate. It is risky because it looks reasonable and produces believable results, even when the real values differ from row to row.
    * *See it on real data:* revenue for the year, by region, with the real take rate of each row vs a fixed `12.5`%.

      | Region | With each row's take rate | With a fixed `0.125` | Error |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | North America | `65.57` | `67.16` | `+1.59` |
      | Europe | `68.34` | `69.28` | `+0.94` |
      | Asia Pacific | `70.39` | `70.49` | `+0.10` |

      Our take rates range from `10.5`% to `13.5`%, so no single number is right. Nothing in the output warns you: the error is easy to miss.
    * *More examples:*

      | What you need | From the data | The hard-coded risk |
      |---|---|---|
      | total revenue for the year | `204.31` | `206.92` with a fixed `0.125` |
      | number of quarters | `len(df)` reads `12` | typing `12` breaks when a 13th row arrives |
      | list of regions | `df["Market_Segment"].unique()` | a typed list misses a new region |
      | a business rule | `MARGIN_LIMIT = 20` at the top of the script | a named rule is visible and easy to change |

    * *Remember:* read every input from the data; type a number into code only when it is a rule, and give it a name, such as `MARGIN_LIMIT = 20`.
  * **7.5 Writing findings for managers**
    * *The idea:* Managers act on clear statements, not on code. Write each finding in three parts: the **finding** (one sentence a manager can repeat), the **evidence** (the number behind it), and the **recommendation** (what to do).
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Part | Weak version | Strong version |
      |---|---|---|
      | Finding | "Margins vary by region." | "North America turns the most revenue into cash." |
      | Evidence | "See the table." | "Its OCF margin is 31.0%, against 24.7% in Europe and 18.5% in Asia Pacific." |
      | Recommendation | "Look into it." | "Study North America's cost structure before expanding in Asia Pacific." |

    * *Common mistake:* averaging percentages. The 4 quarterly margins of Asia Pacific average `18.1`%, but its true margin for the year, total OCF / total revenue, is `18.5`%. Always compute a group's ratio from its totals.
    * *More examples:*

      | Finding | Evidence | Recommendation |
      |---|---|---|
      | Europe has a mid-level cash margin. | `24.7`%, between North America and Asia Pacific | Keep Europe's investment level unchanged. |
      | Asia Pacific is improving fast. | its margin rose from `15.0`% in Q1 to `22.0`% in Q4: `+7.0` points | Keep investing, with a margin target for next year. |
      | Q4 was the strongest quarter. | GMV `524.4` and OCF `17.9`, both the year's highest | Plan capacity for next year's Q4 peak. |

    * *Remember:* finding, evidence, recommendation, and every number from the data.

* **8. Development Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE:**
  * `[1]` File Explorer: create your script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S24_demo_1.py`), and check the dataset is in `data/`.
  * `[2]` Code Editor: write a few lines, run, repeat. Small steps keep errors small.
  * `[3]` AI Agent Chat: on an error, capture the traceback (Python's error report) with `Win + Shift + S` (macOS: `Cmd + Shift + 4`), paste it with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why it failed.
  * `[4]` Terminal: run `python` followed by your script name. Return code `0` means the script finished with no errors.

* **9. Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * Every example has three parts: **1. Input**, **2. Process**, and **3. Output**. Name each file with your own name and the session code, for example `Reacher_S24_demo_1.py` (pattern: `YourName_S24_demo_1.py`).
  * *Micro-Example 1 (From GMV to Cash, Row by Row):* `YourName_S24_demo_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the quarterly results
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_02_04_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: revenue, then OCF margin, for every row
    df["Revenue_USD_M"] = (
        df["Gross_Merchandise_Value_USD_M"] * df["Platform_Take_Rate_Pct"] / 100
    ).round(2)
    df["OCF_Margin_Pct"] = (
        df["Operating_Cash_Flow_USD_M"] / df["Revenue_USD_M"] * 100
    ).round(1)

    # 3. Output: the 3 rows with the lowest margin
    lowest = df.sort_values("OCF_Margin_Pct").head(3)
    print(lowest[["Quarter_Code", "Market_Segment", "OCF_Margin_Pct"]])
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * The first calculation multiplies each row's GMV by that row's take rate and divides by 100: revenue in USD millions. No take rate is typed by hand (section 7.4).
      * The second divides each row's OCF by its revenue: the OCF margin, the share of revenue that became cash. `.round(1)` keeps 1 decimal.
      * `df.sort_values("OCF_Margin_Pct")` orders the rows from the lowest margin to the highest, and `.head(3)` keeps the 3 lowest.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
        Quarter_Code Market_Segment  OCF_Margin_Pct
      8      2024-Q1   Asia_Pacific            15.0
      5      2024-Q2   Asia_Pacific            16.8
      2      2024-Q3   Asia_Pacific            18.6
      ```

      * The 3 weakest quarters all belong to Asia Pacific: in Q1, only `15.0` cents of every revenue dollar became cash.
      * The margins rise from Q1 to Q3: Asia Pacific is weak but improving. Quick Check 1 shows Q4 too.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`.pivot_table()`):*
    * New tool: `df.pivot_table(index="A", columns="B", values="C")` makes a grid with one row per value of `A` and one column per value of `B`, like an Excel pivot table. Why: shows all 12 margins in one readable grid.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(df.___________(index="Market_Segment", columns="Quarter_Code", values="OCF_Margin_Pct"))`
    * Check: the `Asia_Pacific` row reads `15.0`, `16.8`, `18.6`, `22.0`: its margin improved every quarter.
  * *Micro-Example 2 (The Same Work as a Pipeline of Functions):* `YourName_S24_demo_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: a function that loads and checks the raw file
    import pandas as pd

    def load_data(path):
        df = pd.read_csv(path)
        assert (df["Gross_Merchandise_Value_USD_M"] > 0).all(), "GMV must be above 0"
        return df

    # 2. Process: functions that add measures and summarize by region
    def add_metrics(df):
        df["Revenue_USD_M"] = (
            df["Gross_Merchandise_Value_USD_M"] * df["Platform_Take_Rate_Pct"] / 100
        ).round(2)
        return df

    def summarize(df):
        summary = df.groupby("Market_Segment").agg(
            Revenue_USD_M=("Revenue_USD_M", "sum"),
            OCF_USD_M=("Operating_Cash_Flow_USD_M", "sum"),
        )
        summary["OCF_Margin_Pct"] = (
            summary["OCF_USD_M"] / summary["Revenue_USD_M"] * 100
        ).round(1)
        return summary

    # 3. Output: run the stages in order, then print and save
    df = load_data("data/session_02_04_dataset.csv")
    df = add_metrics(df)
    summary = summarize(df)
    print(summary)
    summary.to_csv("data/region_summary.csv")
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `load_data(path)` reads the file and checks with `assert` that every GMV is above 0; bad data stops the pipeline here (section 7.2).
      * `add_metrics(df)` adds the revenue column and returns the table.
      * `summarize(df)` groups by region, sums revenue and OCF, and computes each region's margin **from its totals** (section 7.5).
      * The last 5 lines run the stages in order. Read them alone and you know what the whole script does: load, add metrics, summarize, print, save.
      * `summary.to_csv("data/region_summary.csv")` saves the result so the numbers can be checked later.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
                      Revenue_USD_M  OCF_USD_M  OCF_Margin_Pct
      Market_Segment
      Asia_Pacific            70.39       13.0            18.5
      Europe                  68.34       16.9            24.7
      North_America           65.57       20.3            31.0
      ```

      * Asia Pacific earns the most revenue (`70.39`) but keeps the least as cash (`18.5`%).
      * North America earns the least revenue (`65.57`) but generates the most cash (`20.3`), with a margin of `31.0`%.
      * Bigger is not the same as better: the CFO's question needs both columns.
  * *Quick Check 2 (Docstring):*
    * New tool: a docstring, a text in triple quotes on the first line inside a function. `help(function_name)` prints it. Why: a reviewer learns what each stage does without reading its code.
    * Task: add one line directly under `def add_metrics(df):`, indented like the line below it: `"""Add revenue in USD M from GMV and the take rate."""`. Then add `help(add_metrics)` to part **3. Output**.
    * Check: the Terminal prints a help page that includes `Add revenue in USD M from GMV and the take rate.`
  * *Micro-Example 3 (Write the Findings for the CFO):* `YourName_S24_demo_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the data and build the region summary
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_02_04_dataset.csv")
    df["Revenue_USD_M"] = (
        df["Gross_Merchandise_Value_USD_M"] * df["Platform_Take_Rate_Pct"] / 100
    ).round(2)
    summary = df.groupby("Market_Segment").agg(
        Revenue_USD_M=("Revenue_USD_M", "sum"),
        OCF_USD_M=("Operating_Cash_Flow_USD_M", "sum"),
    )

    # 2. Process: margin, then the best region by margin
    summary["OCF_Margin_Pct"] = (
        summary["OCF_USD_M"] / summary["Revenue_USD_M"] * 100
    ).round(1)
    ranked = summary.sort_values("OCF_Margin_Pct", ascending=False)
    best_region = ranked.index[0]
    best_margin = ranked["OCF_Margin_Pct"].iloc[0]

    # 3. Output: finding, evidence, recommendation
    print(f"Finding: {best_region} turns the most revenue into cash.")
    print(f"Evidence: its OCF margin is {best_margin}%.")
    print("Recommendation: study its cost structure before expanding elsewhere.")
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * Part 1 rebuilds the region summary, as in Micro-Example 2.
      * `summary.sort_values("OCF_Margin_Pct", ascending=False)` puts the best margin first.
      * `ranked.index[0]` reads the first row label, here the name of the best region. After `groupby()`, the region names are the row labels.
      * `ranked["OCF_Margin_Pct"].iloc[0]` reads the first value of the margin column by position.
      * The three `print()` lines write the finding, the evidence, and the recommendation (section 7.5), with the numbers filled in by f-strings.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Finding: North_America turns the most revenue into cash.
      Evidence: its OCF margin is 31.0%.
      Recommendation: study its cost structure before expanding elsewhere.
      ```

      * The region and the margin come from the data, not from typing: if next year's file changes, the sentence changes with it.
      * The recommendation line is still written by a person. The script supplies the evidence; the judgement stays with the analyst.
  * *Quick Check 3 (`.idxmax()`):*
    * New tool: `column.idxmax()` returns the row label of the largest value, in one step. Why: finds "which region?" without sorting.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print("Most revenue:", summary["Revenue_USD_M"].______())`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `Most revenue: Asia_Pacific`. The region with the most revenue is not the one with the best margin.

* **10. Official Python Documentation & References:**
  * [Python Tutorial: Defining Functions](https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/controlflow.html#defining-functions).
  * [PEP 257: Docstring Conventions](https://peps.python.org/pep-0257/).
  * [Pandas API Reference: `DataFrame.pivot_table`](https://pandas.pydata.org/docs/reference/api/pandas.DataFrame.pivot_table.html).
  * [Cookiecutter Data Science: A Logical, Reasonably Standardized Project Structure](https://drivendata.github.io/cookiecutter-data-science/).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Quarterly Review Pipeline:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S24_defense.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S24_defense.py`).
  * *Step 2: Build the Quarterly Review:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the quarterly results
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_02_04_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: revenue per row, then totals per quarter
    df["Revenue_USD_M"] = (
        df["Gross_Merchandise_Value_USD_M"] * df["Platform_Take_Rate_Pct"] / 100
    ).round(2)
    quarterly = df.groupby("Quarter_Code").agg(
        GMV_USD_M=("Gross_Merchandise_Value_USD_M", "sum"),
        Revenue_USD_M=("Revenue_USD_M", "sum"),
        OCF_USD_M=("Operating_Cash_Flow_USD_M", "sum"),
    )
    quarterly["OCF_Margin_Pct"] = (
        quarterly["OCF_USD_M"] / quarterly["Revenue_USD_M"] * 100
    ).round(1)

    # 3. Output: one row per quarter
    print("Quarterly review:\n", quarterly)
    ```
  * *Step 3: Write Two Findings:* Under the code, write two comment lines in the three-part form (finding, evidence, recommendation): one about the quarter with the highest GMV, and one about how the total OCF margin changed during the year.

  * *Success Check:* `2024-Q4` has the highest GMV (`524.4`) and OCF (`17.9`); Q2 has the lowest GMV (`312.4`). The total OCF margin is `23.7`% in Q1, `23.7`% in Q2, `24.6`% in Q3, and `25.8`% in Q4.

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Peer Review a Classmate's Script:**
  * *Coding Challenge:* A classmate sends you this script for their midterm. Copy it into `YourName_S24_practice.py`, review it, and fix it.

Capture the traceback with `Win + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why text and a number cannot be joined with `+`. (`numpy.float64` is the number type that pandas returns; it behaves like a normal `float`.)

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - Where Should We Invest Next Year?:**
  * *Goal:* Build the region report the CFO needs for next year's budget: size, cash efficiency, and trend for every region, plus a list of weak quarters.
  * *What you need:*
    * File: `data/session_02_04_dataset.csv` (described in **5. Lab Practice Dataset**).
    * Script name: `YourName_S24_challenge.py`.
    * Tools you already know: functions with `def` / `return`, `groupby()` + `.agg()`, `.pivot_table()`, `.loc[]`, `.sort_values()`, f-strings, and `.to_csv()`.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. Write `load_data(path)` that reads the file and checks with `assert` that GMV, take rate, and OCF are all above 0.
    2. Write `add_metrics(df)` that adds `Revenue_USD_M` (2 decimals) and `OCF_Margin_Pct` (1 decimal) to every row.
    3. Write `region_report(df)` that returns one row per region with `GMV_USD_M`, `Revenue_USD_M`, `OCF_USD_M` (sums), `OCF_Margin_Pct` (from the sums, 1 decimal), and `Margin_Change_Pts`: the region's Q4 margin minus its Q1 margin, in percentage points, 1 decimal.
    4. Write `weak_quarters(df, limit)` that returns the rows with `OCF_Margin_Pct` below `limit`, sorted from lowest to highest. Call it with `limit=20`.
    5. Sort the region report by `OCF_Margin_Pct`, highest first. Print it, print the weak quarters, and save the report to `data/region_investment_report.csv`.
    6. Print one recommendation line built with an f-string from the report's numbers (not typed in by hand).
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * Percentage points: the difference between two percentages. A margin moving from `15.0`% to `22.0`% rose by `7.0` percentage points (pts), not by 7%.
    * Reading one cell of a pivot table: `margins = df.pivot_table(index="Market_Segment", columns="Quarter_Code", values="OCF_Margin_Pct")`, then `margins["2024-Q4"] - margins["2024-Q1"]` gives the change for every region at once.
    * Adding a column that lines up by row label: `report["Margin_Change_Pts"] = (margins["2024-Q4"] - margins["2024-Q1"]).round(1)` works because both use the region names as row labels.
    * A function parameter with a value: `weak_quarters(df, limit=20)` passes `20` into `limit`.
  * *Hints:*
    * Build and print each function's result before you write the next function.
    * The margin of a region must come from its total OCF divided by its total revenue, not from averaging the 4 quarterly margins.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Report order: `North_America` (margin `31.0`, change `+0.2` pts), `Europe` (`24.7`, `-1.1` pts), `Asia_Pacific` (`18.5`, `+7.0` pts).
    * Weak quarters (margin below 20%): `Asia_Pacific` in `2024-Q1` (`15.0`), `2024-Q2` (`16.8`), and `2024-Q3` (`18.6`).
    * Business meaning: Asia Pacific is the biggest region by GMV (`563.9`) and revenue, but the least cash-efficient, and also the fastest-improving (`+7.0` pts). North America is the cash engine. A sound recommendation: keep investing in Asia Pacific's growth, tied to a target such as reaching North America's cost structure, and protect North America's margin.
