## Module 3: Artificial Intelligence, Machine Learning Fundamentals, and Scikit-Learn

Module 3 introduces managerial artificial intelligence and machine learning frameworks. Students explore how machine learning models generate enterprise value, prepare data through feature engineering, build predictive models using Scikit-Learn, and evaluate model trade-offs between precision, recall, and financial cost.

<a id="session-03-01"></a>
### Session 3.1: Foundations of Artificial Intelligence: Enterprise Value Creation and Decision Automation

![Session 3.1 Map](assets/session_03_01_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_03_01_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_03_01_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 9 (115/11/08 - 115/11/14) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **1. Session Purpose:**
  * Five departments have proposed 10 AI projects, costing between $80,000 and $220,000 each
  * The total request is $1.5 million, but this year's AI budget is only $500,000
  * The COO (Chief Operating Officer) asks: which AI projects should we fund with $500,000, and how fast will they pay back? Today, you learn what AI can and cannot do, and use Python to compare the proposals on the same financial terms

* **2. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "Is this project really AI?": tell apart AI (Artificial Intelligence), ML (Machine Learning), and DL (Deep Learning), and decide when a simple rule is enough
  2. "Which projects pay back fastest?": calculate the payback period (months until the savings repay the cost) and the 3-year ROI (Return on Investment)
  3. "Which projects fit in $500,000?": allocate a fixed budget with a loop, and fix a `ZeroDivisionError` (dividing by zero)

* **3. Business Context: How to Judge an AI Project:**
  * Every AI proposal is an investment: money now, savings later. Five numbers make proposals comparable (example: `AI-PRJ-101`, Supply Chain):

    | Term | Meaning | Example |
    |---|---|---|
    | Implementation cost | one-time cost to build the system | `150,000` |
    | Running cost | yearly cost to keep it working (cloud computing, updates); here 15% of the build cost | `22,500` |
    | Net annual benefit | yearly savings minus the running cost | `294,000 - 22,500 = 271,500` |
    | Payback period | months until the benefit repays the build cost: cost / net benefit x 12 | `6.6` months |
    | 3-year ROI | (net benefit x 3 - cost) / cost x 100 | `443.0`% |

  * Savings are forecasts, not facts. Two habits keep forecasts honest: include running costs, and compare every project with the same formula.
  * Labor hours saved are not the same as money saved: hours only become savings if the freed time is used for other valuable work.

* **4. Data Basics: AI, ML, DL, and When a Rule Is Enough:**
  * Three nested ideas:

    | Term | What it is | Business example |
    |---|---|---|
    | AI (Artificial Intelligence) | any computer system that does a task we link to human thinking | a chatbot answering customers |
    | ML (Machine Learning) | AI that **learns a rule from past examples** instead of being told the rule | flagging likely fraud from past fraud cases |
    | DL (Deep Learning) | ML with many-layered neural networks, for images, sound, and text | reading scanned invoices |

  * Rule or ML? Ask how stable and clear the decision is:
    * **Use a rule** when the policy is fixed and written down: "sales tax is 5% of the price". A rule is cheaper, exact, and easy to explain.
    * **Use ML** when the pattern is complex or changes over time, and you have many past examples with known outcomes: "which invoices look like fraud?"
  * ML makes a prediction; a person or a business rule still decides what to do with it.

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * File Link: [data/session_03_01_dataset.csv](data/session_03_01_dataset.csv)
  * Audited 10-row AI project proposal dataset (fictional teaching data), one row per proposal:
    * `Project_ID`: Proposal code (e.g., `AI-PRJ-101`).
    * `Department_Name`: Sponsoring department (Supply Chain, Customer Service, Marketing, Finance, Fraud Operations).
    * `Implementation_Cost_USD`: One-time build cost in U.S. Dollars.
    * `Annual_Labor_Hours_Saved_Count`: Staff hours the system is expected to save per year.
    * `Projected_Cost_Savings_USD`: Expected yearly savings in U.S. Dollars.
    * `Payback_Period_Months_Count`: The department's own payback estimate. It ignores running costs, so it is shorter than the payback you calculate today (for example, `6.1` against `6.6` months for `AI-PRJ-101`).

* **6. Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `Artificial Intelligence (AI)`:
    * What: computer systems that do tasks we link to human thinking
    * Why: the umbrella term in every vendor pitch; ask which kind of AI is inside
  * `Machine Learning (ML)`:
    * What: a system that learns a decision rule from past examples
    * Why: handles patterns too complex to write down by hand
  * `Deep Learning (DL)`:
    * What: ML with many-layered neural networks
    * Why: needed for images, sound, and free text; costly to build and run
  * `Rule-Based System`:
    * What: code that follows rules a person wrote, such as `if amount > 250000:`
    * Why: the right choice when the policy is clear and fixed
  * `Payback Period`:
    * What: months until the net benefit repays the build cost
    * Why: shows how fast the money comes back
  * `ROI (Return on Investment)`:
    * What: total gain compared with the cost, in percent
    * Why: compares projects of different sizes on one scale

* **7. Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * **7.1 Rule or learning: two ways to make a decision**
    * *The idea:* A computer can decide in two ways. In a **rule-based** system, a person writes the rule, such as "flag every invoice above $250,000". In a **machine learning** system, the computer finds the rule itself by trying many candidate rules on past examples with known answers and keeping the one that makes the fewest mistakes. Machine learning is useful when the right rule is hard to see by eye; a rule is better when the policy is clear and fixed.
    * *See it on real data:* the 10 past invoices in Micro-Example 2 (5 honest, 5 fraud).

      | Approach | The rule | Mistakes on the 10 invoices |
      |---|---|---|
      | Rule-based | a person chose "above 250,000" | `1` (the honest `260000` invoice is flagged) |
      | Learned | the search tried every amount as a threshold and found "300,000 or more" | `0` |

    * *Common mistake:* treating zero mistakes on past data as a promise. A rule fitted to old examples can miss tomorrow's new pattern. Session 3.3 tests models on data they have not seen.
    * *More examples:*

      | Decision | Rule or ML? | Why |
      |---|---|---|
      | Add 5% sales tax to every order | rule | the policy is written down and never guesses |
      | Approve expenses under $500 automatically | rule | a clear, fixed company policy |
      | Which invoices look like fraud? | ML | the pattern is complex and changes as fraudsters adapt |
      | Which customers will cancel next month? | ML | many signals combine in ways no one can write down |
      | Read the amount on a scanned invoice | ML (deep learning) | images need many-layered models |

    * *Remember:* clear, fixed policy: write a rule; complex pattern with many past answers: learn it.
  * **7.2 Payback and ROI: comparing investments on the same terms**
    * *The idea:* Every proposal is money now for savings later. To compare proposals fairly, apply the same formulas to all of them, including the yearly running cost (here 15% of the build cost).

      | Measure | Formula | `AI-PRJ-101` |
      |---|---|---|
      | Net annual benefit | savings - 0.15 x cost | `294000 - 22500 = 271500` |
      | Payback (months) | cost / net benefit x 12 | `150000 / 271500 x 12 = 6.6` |
      | 3-year ROI (%) | (net benefit x 3 - cost) / cost x 100 | `443.0` |

    * *Common mistake:* ignoring the running cost. The department's own estimate for `AI-PRJ-101` is `6.1` months, which is `150000 / 294000 x 12`: it leaves out the `$22,500` a year needed to keep the system working.
    * *More examples:*

      | Project | Cost | Net benefit | Payback | 3-year ROI |
      |---|---|---|---|---|
      | `AI-PRJ-110` (Fraud Operations) | `190000` | `515500` | `4.4` months | `713.9`% |
      | `AI-PRJ-102` (Customer Service) | `80000` | `143000` | `6.7` months | `436.2`% |
      | `AI-PRJ-103` (Marketing) | `120000` | `178000` | `8.1` months | `345.0`% |
      | `AI-PRJ-104` (Finance) | `200000` | `362000` | `6.6` months | `443.0`% |

      The cheapest project (`AI-PRJ-102`) is not the best return, and the most expensive one (`AI-PRJ-110`) pays back fastest: size alone says little.
    * *Remember:* same formula for every project, running costs included.
  * **7.3 Division by zero: an error in Python, a silent `inf` in pandas**
    * *The idea:* Dividing by zero has no answer. Python and pandas react differently:

      | Code | Result |
      |---|---|
      | `150000.0 / 0.0` | stops: `ZeroDivisionError: float division by zero` |
      | `pd.Series([4200.0]) / pd.Series([0.0])` | `inf` (infinity), with no error |

    * *Why the pandas case is dangerous:* `inf` counts as larger than every real number, so a project with zero cost would jump to the top of an "hours per dollar" ranking: sorting `[3.0, inf, 1.0]` from largest gives `[inf, 3.0, 1.0]`.
    * *The fix:* check before dividing, for example `if annual_benefit > 0:`; for columns, remove or flag zero values before you divide.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `pd.Series([10.0, 20.0]) / pd.Series([2.0, 0.0])` | `[5.0, inf]` | the second division silently becomes infinity |
      | `pd.Series([0.0]) / pd.Series([0.0])` | `[NaN]` | zero divided by zero is "not a number" |
      | `np.isinf(pd.Series([5.0, np.inf]))` | `[False, True]` | find the infinite values |
      | `150000.0 / 0.0` | `ZeroDivisionError` | plain Python stops instead |

    * *Remember:* a zero in the bottom of a fraction must be caught, not ignored.
  * **7.4 Choosing under a budget: the greedy method**
    * *The idea:* With more good projects than money, you must choose. The **greedy** method is the simplest plan: sort the projects by value per dollar, go down the list, and fund each project that still fits in the remaining budget.
    * *See it on real data:* sorting by hours saved per $1,000 and funding within $500,000.

      | Step | Project | Cost | Fits? | Budget used |
      |---|---|---|---|---|
      | 1 | `AI-PRJ-102` | `80000` | yes | `80000` |
      | 2 | `AI-PRJ-107` | `90000` | yes | `170000` |
      | 3 | `AI-PRJ-110` | `190000` | yes | `360000` |
      | 4 | `AI-PRJ-105` | `175000` | no (`535000`) | `360000` |
      | ... | ... | ... | ... | ... |
      | last | `AI-PRJ-103` | `120000` | yes | `480000` |

      Result: 4 projects, `16,100` hours a year.
    * *Common mistake:* assuming greedy is always best. It never looks back, so it can fill the last gap with a weak project. Exercise 3 checks every combination and finds `16,900` hours for $495,000.
    * *More examples:* the result depends on what you sort by.

      | Greedy order | Funded (`AI-PRJ-...`) | Cost and hours |
      |---|---|---|
      | hours saved per $1,000 (Exercise 2) | `102`, `107`, `110`, `103` | `$480,000`, `16,100` hours |
      | 3-year ROI | `110`, `105`, `102` | `$445,000`, `16,100` hours |
      | most hours first | `110`, `105`, `107` | `$455,000`, `16,400` hours |
      | cheapest first | `102`, `107`, `103`, `108` | `$420,000`, `12,300` hours |

    * *Remember:* greedy is fast and simple; with few options, check the combinations too.

* **8. Development Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE:**
  * `[1]` File Explorer: create your script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S31_demo_1.py`), and check the dataset is in `data/`.
  * `[2]` Code Editor: write a few lines, run, repeat. Small steps keep errors small.
  * `[3]` AI Agent Chat: on an error, capture the traceback (Python's error report) with `Win + Shift + S` (macOS: `Cmd + Shift + 4`), paste it with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why it failed.
  * `[4]` Terminal: run `python` followed by your script name. Return code `0` means the script finished with no errors.

* **9. Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * Every example has three parts: **1. Input**, **2. Process**, and **3. Output**. Name each file with your own name and the session code, for example `Reacher_S31_demo_1.py` (pattern: `YourName_S31_demo_1.py`).
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Payback and ROI for Every Proposal):* `YourName_S31_demo_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the 10 proposals
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_01_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: net benefit, payback, and 3-year ROI
    df["Net_Annual_Benefit_USD"] = (
        df["Projected_Cost_Savings_USD"] - df["Implementation_Cost_USD"] * 0.15
    )
    df["Payback_Months"] = (
        df["Implementation_Cost_USD"] / df["Net_Annual_Benefit_USD"] * 12
    ).round(1)
    df["ROI_3yr_Pct"] = (
        (df["Net_Annual_Benefit_USD"] * 3 - df["Implementation_Cost_USD"])
        / df["Implementation_Cost_USD"] * 100
    ).round(1)

    # 3. Output: the 3 proposals with the highest ROI
    top3 = df.sort_values("ROI_3yr_Pct", ascending=False).head(3)
    print(top3[["Project_ID", "Department_Name", "Payback_Months", "ROI_3yr_Pct"]])
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `df["Implementation_Cost_USD"] * 0.15` is the yearly running cost; subtracting it from the projected savings gives the net annual benefit, for all 10 proposals at once.
      * The payback divides the build cost by the net benefit and multiplies by 12 to turn years into months.
      * The 3-year ROI compares 3 years of net benefit with the build cost, in percent (section 7.2).
      * `sort_values("ROI_3yr_Pct", ascending=False).head(3)` keeps the 3 best returns.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
         Project_ID   Department_Name  Payback_Months  ROI_3yr_Pct
      9  AI-PRJ-110  Fraud Operations             4.4        713.9
      4  AI-PRJ-105  Fraud Operations             4.5        705.3
      5  AI-PRJ-106      Supply Chain             6.6        445.6
      ```

      * Both Fraud Operations projects pay back in under 5 months and return about 7 times their cost over 3 years.
      * There is a big gap after them: the third-best return (`445.6`%) is far lower. Fraud Operations saves about $2.8 a year for every $1 of build cost; the other projects save between $1.6 and $2.0.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`.rank()`):*
    * New tool: `column.rank(ascending=False)` gives each row its place, 1 for the largest. Equal values share the average place, such as `4.5`. Why: a ranking column is easy to show in a decision memo.
    * Task: add one line at the end of part **2. Process**: `df["ROI_Rank"] = df["ROI_3yr_Pct"].____(ascending=False)`, then add `"ROI_Rank"` to the printed columns.
    * Check: `AI-PRJ-110` has rank `1.0` and `AI-PRJ-105` has rank `2.0`.
  * *Micro-Example 2 (A Fixed Rule vs a Rule Learned from Examples):* `YourName_S31_demo_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: 10 past invoices, checked by auditors (fictional)
    import pandas as pd
    past = pd.DataFrame({
        "Amount_USD": [120000, 180000, 210000, 240000, 260000,
                       300000, 330000, 410000, 450000, 520000],
        "Was_Fraud": [False] * 5 + [True] * 5,
    })

    # 2. Process: a rule a person chose vs a threshold learned from the data
    rule_flags = past["Amount_USD"] > 250000
    rule_mistakes = (rule_flags != past["Was_Fraud"]).sum()

    best_threshold, best_mistakes = None, len(past)
    for threshold in past["Amount_USD"]:
        flags = past["Amount_USD"] >= threshold
        mistakes = (flags != past["Was_Fraud"]).sum()
        if mistakes < best_mistakes:
            best_threshold, best_mistakes = threshold, mistakes

    # 3. Output: compare the mistakes
    print("Fixed rule (above 250,000) mistakes:", rule_mistakes)
    print("Learned threshold:", best_threshold, "mistakes:", best_mistakes)
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `pd.DataFrame({...})` builds 10 fictional past invoices with known answers. `[False] * 5 + [True] * 5` makes a list of 5 `False` followed by 5 `True`.
      * `past["Amount_USD"] > 250000` applies the person's rule and gives one `True` / `False` per invoice.
      * `(rule_flags != past["Was_Fraud"]).sum()` counts the rows where the rule and the auditors disagree: the mistakes.
      * The `for` loop tries each invoice amount as a candidate threshold, counts its mistakes, and keeps the threshold with the fewest. This search is the core idea of machine learning (section 7.1).
      * `best_threshold, best_mistakes = threshold, mistakes` updates two variables in one line.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Fixed rule (above 250,000) mistakes: 1
      Learned threshold: 300000 mistakes: 0
      ```

      * The person's rule flags the honest `260000` invoice: 1 mistake.
      * The search found that "300,000 or more" separates honest and fraudulent invoices perfectly in these examples.
      * With only 10 examples, this is an illustration of learning, not a rule to deploy.
  * *Quick Check 2 (`.mean()` of True/False):*
    * New tool: `.mean()` on a `True` / `False` column gives the share of `True` values, because `True` counts as 1. Why: tells a manager what share of invoices a rule would send to review.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print("Share flagged by the fixed rule:", rule_flags.____())`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `Share flagged by the fixed rule: 0.6`, so 6 of 10 invoices would go to review.
  * *Micro-Example 3 (A Shortlist That Does Not Fit the Budget):* `YourName_S31_demo_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the proposals and add the payback period
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_01_dataset.csv")
    df["Net_Annual_Benefit_USD"] = (
        df["Projected_Cost_Savings_USD"] - df["Implementation_Cost_USD"] * 0.15
    )
    df["Payback_Months"] = (
        df["Implementation_Cost_USD"] / df["Net_Annual_Benefit_USD"] * 12
    ).round(1)

    # 2. Process: fast payback AND many hours saved
    shortlist = df[
        (df["Payback_Months"] <= 7.0)
        & (df["Annual_Labor_Hours_Saved_Count"] >= 4000)
    ]
    shortlist_cost = shortlist["Implementation_Cost_USD"].sum()

    # 3. Output: compare the shortlist with the budget
    print(shortlist[["Project_ID", "Department_Name", "Payback_Months"]])
    print(f"Shortlist cost: ${shortlist_cost:,.0f} against a budget of $500,000")
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * Part 1 recomputes the net benefit and payback, as in Micro-Example 1.
      * The two conditions keep projects that pay back within 7 months **and** save at least 4,000 hours a year; each condition is in its own brackets, joined with `&` (Session 1.4).
      * `shortlist["Implementation_Cost_USD"].sum()` adds up what the shortlist would cost.
      * The f-string `${shortlist_cost:,.0f}` prints the total with thousands separators.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
         Project_ID   Department_Name  Payback_Months
      0  AI-PRJ-101      Supply Chain             6.6
      3  AI-PRJ-104           Finance             6.6
      4  AI-PRJ-105  Fraud Operations             4.5
      5  AI-PRJ-106      Supply Chain             6.6
      8  AI-PRJ-109           Finance             6.7
      9  AI-PRJ-110  Fraud Operations             4.4
      Shortlist cost: $1,095,000 against a budget of $500,000
      ```

      * 6 projects pass both rules, but together they cost more than twice the budget.
      * Filtering tells you which projects are good; it does not tell you which ones to fund. That needs a budget method (section 7.4).
  * *Quick Check 3 (`.isin()`):*
    * New tool: `column.isin([...])` is `True` when the value is in the list. Why: answers questions about a few named groups, such as "show only Finance and Fraud Operations".
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(len(df[df["Department_Name"].____(["Finance", "Fraud Operations"])]))`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `4`.

* **10. Official Python Documentation & References:**
  * [Scikit-Learn: Machine Learning in Python Official Overview](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/).
  * [Russell & Norvig: Artificial Intelligence: A Modern Approach (Reference Standards)](https://aima.cs.berkeley.edu/).
  * [Agrawal, Gans, Goldfarb: Prediction Machines: The Simple Economics of AI (Harvard Business Review Press)](https://www.predictionmachines.ai/).
  * [Python Library: `itertools.combinations`](https://docs.python.org/3/library/itertools.html#itertools.combinations).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - AI Portfolio Ranking:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S31_portfolio.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S31_portfolio.py`).
  * *Step 2: Rank the Portfolio and Compare Departments:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the 10 proposals
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_01_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: net benefit and 3-year ROI, then the average by department
    df["Net_Annual_Benefit_USD"] = (
        df["Projected_Cost_Savings_USD"] - df["Implementation_Cost_USD"] * 0.15
    )
    df["ROI_3yr_Pct"] = (
        (df["Net_Annual_Benefit_USD"] * 3 - df["Implementation_Cost_USD"])
        / df["Implementation_Cost_USD"] * 100
    ).round(1)
    by_department = (
        df.groupby("Department_Name")["ROI_3yr_Pct"].mean().round(1)
        .sort_values(ascending=False)
    )

    # 3. Output: the full ranking and the department view
    ranked = df.sort_values("ROI_3yr_Pct", ascending=False)
    print(ranked[["Project_ID", "Department_Name", "ROI_3yr_Pct"]])
    print("\nAverage 3-year ROI by department:\n", by_department)
    ```
  * *Step 3: Interpret:* In two comment lines, explain why Fraud Operations ranks first, and which numbers you would ask the Marketing team to double-check.
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:* Run `python YourName_S31_portfolio.py` in Panel `[4]` and confirm return code `0`.
  * *Success Check:* department averages are Fraud Operations `709.6`, Supply Chain `444.3`, Finance `440.2`, Customer Service `429.0`, and Marketing `342.3`. Fraud Operations saves the most money per dollar spent (about $2.8 of yearly savings per $1 of build cost).

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Fund Projects Within $500,000:**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In `YourName_S31_practice.py`, fund projects in order of hours saved per dollar until the $500,000 budget runs out, then check a new proposal's payback safely.
  * *Task 1 (Greedy Allocation):*
    * Add `Hours_per_1000_USD` = `Annual_Labor_Hours_Saved_Count` / `Implementation_Cost_USD` x 1000, and sort by it from highest to lowest.
    * Start with `budget = 500000`, `spent = 0`, and `funded = []`.
    * Loop over the sorted rows with `for project, cost in zip(df["Project_ID"], df["Implementation_Cost_USD"]):`. If `spent + cost <= budget`, add the project to `funded` and add `cost` to `spent`.
    * `zip(a, b)` walks through two columns side by side, giving one pair of values per row.
    * Print the funded projects, the amount spent, and the total hours saved by the funded projects.
  * *Task 2 (Read the ZeroDivisionError & AI Diagnosis):* Write a function `payback_months(cost, annual_benefit)` that returns `cost / annual_benefit * 12`. Call it for a new proposal with no proven savings yet: `payback_months(50000.0, 0.0)`. The script stops with `ZeroDivisionError: float division by zero`. Capture the traceback with `Win + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why.
  * *Task 3 (Safe Division):* Change the function: if `annual_benefit <= 0`, return `None`; otherwise return the payback. When the result is `None`, print `"No payback: no proven savings"`.
  * *Success Check:* the loop funds `AI-PRJ-102`, `AI-PRJ-107`, `AI-PRJ-110`, and `AI-PRJ-103` for `$480,000` and `16,100` hours a year; `payback_months(50000.0, 0.0)` now prints `No payback: no proven savings`; return code `0`.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - Is the Greedy Choice the Best Choice?:**
  * *Goal:* Test the simple "most hours per dollar first" method from Exercise 2 against every possible combination of projects, and tell the COO whether the simple method left value on the table.
  * *What you need:*
    * File: `data/session_03_01_dataset.csv` (described in **5. Lab Practice Dataset**).
    * Script name: `YourName_S31_challenge.py`.
    * Your Exercise 2 result (`16,100` hours for `$480,000`).
    * Tools you already know: lists, `for` loops, `if`, `.sum()`, `.isin()`, and f-strings.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. List every possible group of projects: all groups of 1 project, of 2 projects, and so on, up to all 10.
    2. For each group, add up the build cost. Skip groups that cost more than `$500,000`.
    3. For the groups that fit, add up `Annual_Labor_Hours_Saved_Count`, and keep the group with the most hours. If two groups tie on hours, keep the cheaper one.
    4. Count how many groups you checked in total and how many fit the budget.
    5. Print the best group's project IDs, cost, and hours, then the difference in hours compared with the greedy result.
    6. Print a two-line memo for the COO: what to fund, and why the simple method was not enough.
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * `from itertools import combinations` loads a tool that lists groups. `combinations(["A", "B", "C"], 2)` gives `("A", "B")`, `("A", "C")`, and `("B", "C")`.
    * `for size in range(1, 11):` repeats with `size` = 1, 2, ..., 10; inside it, `for group in combinations(project_ids, size):` walks through every group of that size.
    * `list(group)` turns a group into a list, so you can use `df[df["Project_ID"].isin(list(group))]` to select its rows.
  * *Hints:*
    * Make `project_ids = list(df["Project_ID"])` first.
    * Keep the best result in three variables, `best_hours`, `best_cost`, and `best_group`, and update them inside the loop.
    * With 10 projects there are `1,023` groups, so the loop finishes in a few seconds.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Groups checked: `1023`; groups that fit the budget: `163`.
    * Best group: `AI-PRJ-101`, `AI-PRJ-102`, `AI-PRJ-105`, `AI-PRJ-107`, costing `$495,000` and saving `16,900` hours a year.
    * That is `800` more hours than the greedy result. Business meaning: "most hours per dollar first" picked the cheap Marketing project `AI-PRJ-103` to fill the last gap, while a different mix used the budget better. With few options, checking every combination is cheap and finds the true best plan.

<a id="session-03-02"></a>
### Session 3.2: Advanced Data Analysis: Feature Engineering, Scaling, and Business Data Preprocessing

![Session 3.2 Map](assets/session_03_02_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_03_02_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_03_02_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 10 (115/11/15 - 115/11/21) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **1. Session Purpose:**
  * A bank's credit team wants a model that predicts which loan applicants will default (fail to repay their loan)
  * Its applicant records mix text, gaps, and numbers on very different scales, such as an income of `65,000` next to a utilization of `24.5`
  * The credit risk manager asks: can we turn our applicant records into data a model can learn from, without cheating on the test? Today, you prepare the data step by step, so that Session 3.3 can train a model on it

* **2. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "What do we predict, and from what?": split a table into features `X` (the inputs) and the target `y` (the answer to predict)
  2. "How do text and gaps become numbers?": fill missing values (imputation), turn text categories into 0/1 columns (one-hot encoding), and put numbers on a common scale (standardization)
  3. "How do we test honestly?": split the data into a training set and a test set before learning anything from it, so that no test information leaks into training (data leakage)

* **3. Business Context: Why Data Preparation Decides Model Quality:**
  * A model is only as good as the data it learns from ("garbage in, garbage out"). Data preparation usually takes more of a project's time than building the model itself.
  * A model reads only numbers, with no gaps. Our raw data breaks both rules:
    * `Education_Tier` is text (`Bachelor`, `Master`, `Doctorate`)
    * One income and one utilization value are missing
  * A model must be tested on applicants it has never seen, just as a student is tested on new exam questions, not on the practice questions. Otherwise the bank believes the model is better than it is, and approves risky loans.
  * Credit utilization: the share of an applicant's credit limit that is in use. `48.2`% means $48.20 of every $100 of available credit is borrowed.

* **4. Data Basics: From a Raw Row to a Model-Ready Row:**
  * Applicant `CLI-701`, before and after preparation:

    | Column | Raw value | Model-ready value | Step |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | `Age_Years_Count` | `45` | `0.41` | standardized |
    | `Annual_Income_USD` | `125000.0` | `0.44` | standardized |
    | `Credit_Utilization_Pct` | `48.2` | `0.82` | standardized |
    | `Education_Tier` | `Master` | `Education_Tier_Master` = `1`, `Education_Tier_Doctorate` = `0` | one-hot encoded |

  * The standardized values use the training rows' averages and spreads (Micro-Example 3). Micro-Example 3 also standardizes the 0/1 columns; Exercise 3 shows how to scale only the number columns.
  * **One-hot encoding:** one 0/1 column per category. With `drop_first=True`, the first category (`Bachelor`) gets no column: a row with `0` in both remaining columns is a Bachelor. Never code categories as `1`, `2`, `3`; that would tell the model a Doctorate is "three times" a Bachelor.
  * **Standardization:** `(value - average) / standard deviation`. The result says how many standard deviations a value is above (`+`) or below (`-`) the average, so income and utilization become comparable. Standard deviation measures how spread out a column is.
  * **Train/test split:** the model learns from the training set; the test set is kept aside and used once, at the end, to measure the model honestly.

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * File Link: [data/session_03_02_dataset.csv](data/session_03_02_dataset.csv)
  * Audited 12-row loan applicant dataset (fictional teaching data), one row per applicant, with 2 missing values on purpose:
    * `Client_ID`: Applicant code (e.g., `CLI-701`). An ID, not a feature: it says nothing about the applicant.
    * `Age_Years_Count`: Age in years.
    * `Annual_Income_USD`: Yearly income in U.S. Dollars (missing for `CLI-707`).
    * `Credit_Utilization_Pct`: Share of the credit limit in use, in percent (missing for `CLI-709`).
    * `Education_Tier`: Highest education: `Bachelor`, `Master`, or `Doctorate`.
    * `Defaulted_Binary`: The target: `1` = defaulted, `0` = repaid. 3 of the 12 applicants defaulted.

* **6. Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `Features (X)` and `Target (y)`:
    * What: `X` holds the input columns; `y` holds the outcome to predict
    * Why: every Scikit-Learn model expects this split
  * `Imputation`:
    * What: filling missing values, for example with the column's median (the middle value)
    * Why: models cannot learn from rows with gaps
  * `One-Hot Encoding`:
    * What: one 0/1 column per text category
    * Why: turns text into numbers without inventing an order
  * `Standardization`:
    * What: rescaling a column to an average of 0 and a standard deviation of 1
    * Why: stops large-unit columns, such as income, from drowning out small ones
  * `Train/Test Split`:
    * What: keeping some rows aside to test the model at the end
    * Why: the only honest measure of how the model will do on new applicants
  * `Data Leakage`:
    * What: information from the test set slipping into training
    * Why: makes a model look better than it really is

* **7. Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * **7.1 Features and target: what goes in, what comes out**
    * *The idea:* A prediction model learns a link between inputs and an answer. The inputs are called **features** and are stored in a table named `X`; the answer to predict is the **target**, stored in `y`. Every row is one example: one applicant's features and that applicant's real outcome.
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Column | Role | Why |
      |---|---|---|
      | `Age_Years_Count`, `Annual_Income_USD`, `Credit_Utilization_Pct`, `Education_Tier` | features (`X`) | facts known when the application arrives |
      | `Defaulted_Binary` | target (`y`) | the outcome we want to predict |
      | `Client_ID` | neither | a label; it says nothing about risk |

    * *Common mistake:* leaving the target inside `X`. The model would "predict" default by looking at the answer itself, score perfectly in testing, and be useless on new applicants, who have no answer yet.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `X.shape` | `(12, 4)` | 12 applicants, 4 features |
      | `y.value_counts()` | `0`: `9`, `1`: `3` | 3 of 12 applicants defaulted |
      | `y.mean()` | `0.25` | the default rate: the mean of a 0/1 column is the share of 1s |
      | `X.isnull().sum()` | `1` in income, `1` in utilization | the gaps to fill before training |

    * *Remember:* `X` = what you know in advance; `y` = what you want to predict.
  * **7.2 Filling gaps: imputation**
    * *The idea:* Models cannot learn from empty cells. Imputation fills each gap with a typical value of its column. The **median** (the middle value) is a safe default, because a few extreme values do not pull it, unlike the average (mean).
    * *See it on real data:* the income column, with `CLI-707`'s income missing.

      | Statistic of the 11 known incomes | Value |
      |---|---|
      | mean | `115727.27` |
      | median | `110000.0` |
      | smallest and largest | `58000.0` and `185000.0` |

      The 3 high incomes of the Doctorate applicants pull the mean up; the median stays in the middle.
    * *Common mistake:* deleting every row with a gap. With 12 rows, dropping 2 loses one-sixth of the data. Fill gaps when you can; drop rows only when too much of the row is missing.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `df["Credit_Utilization_Pct"].median()` | `36.5` | the value that would fill `CLI-709`'s gap, using all rows |
      | `SimpleImputer(strategy="median").fit(X_train).statistics_` | `[38.0, 96000.0, 33.15, 0.0, 0.0]` | the medians learned from the 9 training rows only |
      | `SimpleImputer(strategy="mean")` | fills with averages | another option for columns without extreme values |

      The medians from all rows and from the training rows differ (`36.5` vs `33.15`), because they are computed from different applicants. Section 7.5 explains why the training version is the correct one.
    * *Remember:* fill gaps with the median, learned from the training rows.
  * **7.3 Text categories become 0/1 columns: one-hot encoding**
    * *The idea:* A model computes with numbers, so the text in `Education_Tier` must become numbers. Writing `Bachelor = 1`, `Master = 2`, `Doctorate = 3` would wrongly tell the model that a Doctorate is "three times" a Bachelor. One-hot encoding instead makes one 0/1 column per category. With `drop_first=True`, the first category gets no column: it is the row with `0` everywhere.
    * *See it on real data:*

      | `Education_Tier` | `Education_Tier_Doctorate` | `Education_Tier_Master` |
      |---|---|---|
      | `Bachelor` | `0` | `0` |
      | `Master` | `0` | `1` |
      | `Doctorate` | `1` | `0` |

      Categories are put in alphabetical order, so `Bachelor` comes first and is the one dropped.
    * *Common mistake:* a category appearing in new data that was not in the training data, such as `"Associate"`. `pd.get_dummies()` would create a column the model has never seen. Exercise 3 shows a tool that handles this safely.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `df["Education_Tier"].value_counts()` | Master `5`, Bachelor `4`, Doctorate `3` | how many 1s each new column will have |
      | `pd.get_dummies(X, columns=["Education_Tier"], drop_first=True).shape` | `(12, 5)` | 1 text column replaced by 2 number columns |
      | the same with `drop_first=False` | `(12, 6)` | 3 columns, one per category |

    * *Remember:* one 0/1 column per category, never 1-2-3 codes.
  * **7.4 Putting numbers on one scale: standardization**
    * *The idea:* Income runs from `58,000` to `185,000`; utilization from `15.2` to `62.1`. Some models compare rows by distance and would treat a difference of 1,000 dollars as far bigger than a difference of 10 percentage points, only because of the units. Standardization rewrites each value as "how many standard deviations above or below the average", so all columns use the same scale. The standard deviation measures how spread out a column is.
    * *See it on real data:* age, average `42.5` years, standard deviation `10.66` years (all 12 applicants).

      | Applicant age | Calculation | Standardized |
      |---|---|---|
      | `28` | `(28 - 42.5) / 10.66` | `-1.36` |
      | `45` | `(45 - 42.5) / 10.66` | `0.23` |
      | `63` | `(63 - 42.5) / 10.66` | `1.92` |

      After standardization, every column has an average of `0` and a standard deviation of `1`.
    * *More examples:* income, average `115727.27`, standard deviation `43668.36` (the 11 known values).

      | Value | Calculation | Standardized |
      |---|---|---|
      | income `58000` | `(58000 - 115727.27) / 43668.36` | `-1.32`: the lowest income |
      | income `110000` | `(110000 - 115727.27) / 43668.36` | `-0.13`: just below average |
      | income `185000` | `(185000 - 115727.27) / 43668.36` | `1.59`: well above average |
      | `df["Annual_Income_USD"].std()` | `45799.76` | pandas divides by n - 1 by default; the scaler uses n (`43668.36`) |

    * *Remember:* standardized value = (value - average) / standard deviation.
  * **7.5 Honest testing: split first, then learn**
    * *The idea:* The test set plays the role of next month's new applicants: the model and its preparation steps must not see it during training. So the order is fixed: split the rows first; `fit` the imputer and the scaler on the training rows only; then `transform` both sets with what was learned. Using information from the test set during training is called **data leakage**, and it makes a model look better than it really is.
    * *See it on real data:* `train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.25, random_state=42, stratify=y)`.

      | Setting | Effect here |
      |---|---|
      | `test_size=0.25` | 3 of 12 rows go to the test set: `CLI-704`, `CLI-710`, `CLI-708` |
      | `stratify=y` | 2 defaults in training and 1 in test: the same 25% rate in both |
      | `random_state=42` | the same split every time you run the script |
      | `random_state=7` instead | a different test set: `CLI-710`, `CLI-705`, `CLI-701` |

    * *Common mistake:* `scaler.fit_transform(X_test)`. The scaler then learns the test rows' own average, and the test applicants all look "average", even though they are older and earn more than the training applicants. Exercise 2 shows the numbers.
    * *More examples:*

      | Setting | Train / test rows | Defaults in train / test |
      |---|---|---|
      | `test_size=0.25, stratify=y` (ours) | 9 / 3 | 2 / 1 |
      | `test_size=0.25`, no `stratify` | 9 / 3 | 2 / 1 here, but not guaranteed; the test set is `CLI-710`, `CLI-709`, `CLI-700` |
      | `test_size=0.5, stratify=y` | 6 / 6 | 2 / 1: 3 defaults cannot split evenly |
      | scaler fitted on train, test averages | `0.74`, `0.73`, `0.57`, ... | correct: the test applicants differ |
      | scaler fitted on test (leakage) | all `0.0` | the difference has disappeared |

    * *Remember:* split, fit on train, transform both.

* **8. Development Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE:**
  * `[1]` File Explorer: create your script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S32_demo_1.py`), and check the dataset is in `data/`.
  * `[2]` Code Editor: write a few lines, run, repeat. Small steps keep errors small.
  * `[3]` AI Agent Chat: on an error, capture the traceback (Python's error report) with `Win + Shift + S` (macOS: `Cmd + Shift + 4`), paste it with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why it failed.
  * `[4]` Terminal: run `python` followed by your script name. Return code `0` means the script finished with no errors. First time only: `pip install scikit-learn`.

* **9. Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * Every example has three parts: **1. Input**, **2. Process**, and **3. Output**. Name each file with your own name and the session code, for example `Reacher_S32_demo_1.py` (pattern: `YourName_S32_demo_1.py`).
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Features, Target, and Gaps):* `YourName_S32_demo_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the applicants
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_02_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: inputs (X) and the answer to predict (y)
    X = df.drop(columns=["Client_ID", "Defaulted_Binary"])
    y = df["Defaulted_Binary"]

    # 3. Output: size, outcome counts, and missing values
    print("X shape (rows, columns):", X.shape)
    print("Defaults (1) and repaid (0):\n", y.value_counts())
    print("Missing values per column:\n", X.isnull().sum())
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `df.drop(columns=["Client_ID", "Defaulted_Binary"])` returns a copy of the table without the ID and the answer: the features `X` (section 7.1). The original `df` is unchanged.
      * `df["Defaulted_Binary"]` picks the answer column: the target `y`.
      * `y.value_counts()` counts each answer; `X.isnull().sum()` counts the gaps per column.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      X shape (rows, columns): (12, 4)
      Defaults (1) and repaid (0):
       Defaulted_Binary
      0    9
      1    3
      Missing values per column:
       Age_Years_Count           0
      Annual_Income_USD         1
      Credit_Utilization_Pct    1
      Education_Tier            0
      ```

      * 4 features for 12 applicants.
      * 3 defaults out of 12: a 25% default rate, the first number a credit manager asks for.
      * One income and one utilization value are missing: they must be filled before training (section 7.2).
  * *Quick Check 1 (`value_counts(normalize=True)`):*
    * New tool: `.value_counts(normalize=True)` gives each value's share instead of its count. Why: the default rate is the first number a credit manager asks for.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(y.value_counts(_________=True))`
    * Check: `0` has `0.75` and `1` has `0.25`: a 25% default rate.
  * *Micro-Example 2 (One-Hot Encoding):* `YourName_S32_demo_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the applicants and keep the features
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_02_dataset.csv")
    X = df.drop(columns=["Client_ID", "Defaulted_Binary"])

    # 2. Process: one 0/1 column per education level, except the first
    X_encoded = pd.get_dummies(X, columns=["Education_Tier"],
                               drop_first=True, dtype=float)

    # 3. Output: the new column names and two rows
    print(X_encoded.columns.tolist())
    print(X_encoded.head(2))
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `pd.get_dummies(X, columns=["Education_Tier"], drop_first=True, dtype=float)` replaces the text column with 0/1 columns (section 7.3). `dtype=float` writes them as `0.0` and `1.0`.
      * `X_encoded.columns.tolist()` lists the new column names.
      * `.head(2)` shows the first two applicants, a Bachelor and a Master.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      ['Age_Years_Count', 'Annual_Income_USD', 'Credit_Utilization_Pct', 'Education_Tier_Doctorate', 'Education_Tier_Master']
         Age_Years_Count  ...  Education_Tier_Master
      0               28  ...                    0.0
      1               45  ...                    1.0
      ```

      * `Education_Tier` is gone; two number columns took its place. There is no `Education_Tier_Bachelor`: a Bachelor is the row with `0.0` in both.
      * Applicant `0` (`CLI-700`) is a Bachelor (`0.0` in the Master column); applicant `1` (`CLI-701`) is a Master (`1.0`).
  * *Quick Check 2 (`drop_first`):*
    * New tool: the `drop_first` setting. `True` leaves out the first category; `False` keeps a column for every category. Why: shows what "the first category is implied" means.
    * Task: in part **2. Process**, change `drop_first=True` to `drop_first=_____`.
    * Check: a third column, `Education_Tier_Bachelor`, appears, and each row has exactly one `1.0` among the three education columns. Change it back to `True` afterwards.
  * *Micro-Example 3 (Split First, Then Fill and Scale):* `YourName_S32_demo_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load, encode, and split before learning anything
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
    from sklearn.impute import SimpleImputer
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_02_dataset.csv")
    X = pd.get_dummies(df.drop(columns=["Client_ID", "Defaulted_Binary"]),
                       columns=["Education_Tier"], drop_first=True, dtype=float)
    y = df["Defaulted_Binary"]
    X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
        X, y, test_size=0.25, random_state=42, stratify=y
    )

    # 2. Process: learn medians and scales from the training rows only
    imputer = SimpleImputer(strategy="median")
    X_train_filled = imputer.fit_transform(X_train)
    X_test_filled = imputer.transform(X_test)
    scaler = StandardScaler()
    X_train_scaled = scaler.fit_transform(X_train_filled)
    X_test_scaled = scaler.transform(X_test_filled)

    # 3. Output: sizes and the medians used to fill the gaps
    print("Train rows:", len(X_train), "| Test rows:", len(X_test))
    print("Medians learned from training rows:", imputer.statistics_.round(2).tolist())
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * Part 1 encodes the text column, then splits the rows **before** anything is learned (section 7.5).
      * `SimpleImputer(strategy="median")` creates an empty gap-filler. `.fit_transform(X_train)` learns the training medians and fills the training gaps; `.transform(X_test)` fills the test gaps with those same training medians.
      * `StandardScaler()` works the same way: it learns each column's training average and standard deviation, then applies them to both sets (section 7.4).
      * `imputer.statistics_` holds the medians it learned; the trailing `_` marks a value learned during `fit`.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Train rows: 9 | Test rows: 3
      Medians learned from training rows: [38.0, 96000.0, 33.15, 0.0, 0.0]
      ```

      * 9 applicants for training and 3 kept aside for the final test.
      * The medians are, in column order: age `38.0`, income `96000.0`, utilization `33.15`, and `0.0` for both education columns. Any gap, in either set, is filled with these values.
  * *Quick Check 3 (`scaler.mean_`):*
    * New tool: `scaler.mean_` holds the column averages the scaler learned from the training rows. Why: shows exactly what "fit on the training set" means.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print("Averages learned by the scaler:", scaler._____.round(2).tolist())`
    * Check: the first three values are `40.44` (age), `106444.44` (income), and `35.82` (utilization).

* **10. Official Python Documentation & References:**
  * [Scikit-Learn Preprocessing Guide: StandardScaler and OneHotEncoder](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/preprocessing.html).
  * [Scikit-Learn Imputation of Missing Values: SimpleImputer](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/impute.html).
  * [Scikit-Learn Model Selection: train_test_split](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.model_selection.train_test_split.html).
  * [Scikit-Learn: Common Pitfalls (Data Leakage)](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/common_pitfalls.html).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Credit Data Preparation Pipeline:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S32_preprocessing.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S32_preprocessing.py`).
  * *Step 2: Build the Full Preparation Sequence:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load, encode, and split
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
    from sklearn.impute import SimpleImputer
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_02_dataset.csv")
    X = pd.get_dummies(df.drop(columns=["Client_ID", "Defaulted_Binary"]),
                       columns=["Education_Tier"], drop_first=True, dtype=float)
    y = df["Defaulted_Binary"]
    X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
        X, y, test_size=0.25, random_state=42, stratify=y
    )

    # 2. Process: fill, then scale; fit on training rows only
    imputer = SimpleImputer(strategy="median")
    scaler = StandardScaler()
    X_train_scaled = pd.DataFrame(
        scaler.fit_transform(imputer.fit_transform(X_train)), columns=X.columns
    )
    X_test_scaled = pd.DataFrame(
        scaler.transform(imputer.transform(X_test)), columns=X.columns
    )

    # 3. Output: check the training columns
    print("Training averages:\n", X_train_scaled.mean().round(2))
    print("Training spread:\n", X_train_scaled.std(ddof=0).round(2))
    print("Defaults in train:", y_train.sum(), "| in test:", y_test.sum())
    ```
    * `.std(ddof=0)` uses the same standard deviation formula as the scaler. pandas' default formula gives a slightly larger value (`1.06` here) for small tables.
  * *Step 3: Validate the Scaling:* Every training average should be `0.0` (a printed `-0.0` is the same as `0.0`) and every spread `1.0`.
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:* Run `python YourName_S32_preprocessing.py` in Panel `[4]` and confirm return code `0`.
  * *Success Check:* 5 columns, each with average `0.0` and spread `1.0`; `2` defaults in the training set and `1` in the test set, thanks to `stratify=y`.

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Feature Engineering and a Leakage Check:**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In `YourName_S32_practice.py`, add one engineered feature, then see with your own eyes what data leakage hides.
  * *Task 1 (Engineer a Feature):* Add `High_Utilization_Flag` = `1` when `Credit_Utilization_Pct` is above `50`, else `0`: `(df["Credit_Utilization_Pct"] > 50).astype(int)`. `.astype(int)` turns `True` / `False` into `1` / `0`. Print `pd.crosstab(df["High_Utilization_Flag"], df["Defaulted_Binary"])`.
  * *Task 2 (Read the NotFittedError & AI Diagnosis):* Starting from Micro-Example 3, create a new `StandardScaler()` and call `.transform(X_test_filled)` on it before any `.fit()`. The script stops with `NotFittedError: This StandardScaler instance is not fitted yet.` Capture the traceback with `Win + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and ask what "fitted" means.
  * *Task 3 (See the Leakage):* Scale the test rows two ways and print the column averages of each, rounded to 2 decimals:
    * Correct: `scaler.transform(X_test_filled)`, with `scaler` fitted on the training rows
    * Leaky: `StandardScaler().fit_transform(X_test_filled)`
    * `array.mean(axis=0)` gives the average of each column of a Scikit-Learn result.
  * *Success Check:*
    * Task 1: the flag splits the applicants perfectly: all 3 flagged applicants defaulted and all 9 others repaid. With only 12 rows, treat a perfect pattern as a warning sign, not as proof.
    * Task 3: the correct averages are `0.74`, `0.73`, `0.57`, `0.27`, `0.71`, showing that the test applicants are older, earn more, and use more credit than the training applicants. The leaky averages are all `0.0`: the difference has disappeared.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - A Reusable Preparation Pipeline:**
  * *Goal:* Build one preparation object that handles numbers and text in one step, can be fitted on training rows and reused on any new applicant, and does not crash on a category it has never seen.
  * *What you need:*
    * File: `data/session_03_02_dataset.csv` (described in **5. Lab Practice Dataset**).
    * Script name: `YourName_S32_challenge.py`.
    * Tools you already know: `train_test_split` (same settings as Micro-Example 3), `SimpleImputer`, `StandardScaler`, and `.fit()` / `.transform()`.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. Split the raw features (before any encoding) and the target with `test_size=0.25`, `random_state=42`, and `stratify=y`.
    2. Define `numeric_cols = ["Age_Years_Count", "Annual_Income_USD", "Credit_Utilization_Pct"]` and `text_cols = ["Education_Tier"]`.
    3. Build a `Pipeline` for the numeric columns: fill with the median, then standardize.
    4. Combine it with a `OneHotEncoder(handle_unknown="ignore")` for the text column in one `ColumnTransformer`.
    5. Fit the combined object on the training rows only. Print the output column names, then the transformed test rows, rounded to 2 decimals.
    6. Transform one new applicant: age `36`, income missing (`None`), utilization `57.0`, education `"Associate"`. Print the result, rounded to 2 decimals.
    7. In a comment, explain what happened to the missing income and to the unseen education level.
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * `from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline`: `Pipeline([("impute", SimpleImputer(strategy="median")), ("scale", StandardScaler())])` runs its steps in order, and fits them in order.
    * `from sklearn.compose import ColumnTransformer`: sends each group of columns to its own tool and joins the results side by side, for example:

      ```text
      ColumnTransformer([
          ("num", numeric_pipeline, numeric_cols),
          ("cat", OneHotEncoder(handle_unknown="ignore"), text_cols),
      ])
      ```
    * `from sklearn.preprocessing import OneHotEncoder`: the Scikit-Learn version of one-hot encoding. `handle_unknown="ignore"` writes `0` in every category column for a value it never saw, instead of raising an error.
    * `.get_feature_names_out()` returns the names of the output columns.
    * A one-row DataFrame for the new applicant: `pd.DataFrame({"Age_Years_Count": [36], "Annual_Income_USD": [None], "Credit_Utilization_Pct": [57.0], "Education_Tier": ["Associate"]})`.
  * *Hints:*
    * Wrap a result in `pd.DataFrame(result, columns=prep.get_feature_names_out())` to read it with column names.
    * Without `handle_unknown="ignore"`, the new applicant raises `ValueError: Found unknown categories ['Associate']`. Try it once to see why the setting matters.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Output columns (6): `num__Age_Years_Count`, `num__Annual_Income_USD`, `num__Credit_Utilization_Pct`, `cat__Education_Tier_Bachelor`, `cat__Education_Tier_Doctorate`, `cat__Education_Tier_Master`.
    * Transformed test rows: shape `(3, 6)`; the second test row is the Doctorate applicant with `1.41`, `1.63`, `1.49`.
    * New applicant: `-0.4`, `-0.25`, `1.4`, then `0.0`, `0.0`, `0.0`. The missing income was filled with the training median (`96000.0`) before scaling, and the unseen `"Associate"` level became all zeros instead of crashing the script. Business meaning: the same object can now prepare every future application in one call, exactly as it prepared the training data.

<a id="session-03-03"></a>
### Session 3.3: Practical Machine Learning with Scikit-Learn: Model Training and Validation

![Session 3.3 Map](assets/session_03_03_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_03_03_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_03_03_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 11 (115/11/22 - 115/11/28) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **1. Session Purpose:**
  * A commercial property valuation firm prices office buildings by hand, which takes an expert several days per property
  * It has the sale records of 14 recent deals: size, number of rooms, distance to the city center, tax rate, and price
  * The valuation manager asks: can a model estimate a property's sale price from its size and location, and how far off will it be? Today, you train your first machine learning model and measure its error honestly

* **2. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "Can a model learn prices from past deals?": train a linear regression model with Scikit-Learn's `.fit()` and read its coefficients (how much the price changes for one more unit of each feature)
  2. "How far off are the predictions?": measure the error with RMSE (Root Mean Squared Error) and MAE (Mean Absolute Error), and check stability with cross-validation (testing on several different splits)
  3. "What will this new building sell for?": predict the price of one new property, and fix the `ValueError` a one-row prediction often raises

* **3. Business Context: When a Price Model Is Worth Using:**
  * An estimate is useful if its typical error is small compared with the price. Our properties sell for about $513,000 on average, so a typical error of $10,000 (about 2%) is useful for a first quote, while a typical error of $100,000 would not be.
  * A model learns from past deals only. It should not be trusted far outside them, for example for a 10,000 square-foot tower when the largest past deal was 3,400 square feet.
  * Regression vs classification:
    * **Regression** predicts a number, such as a price. Today's model, linear regression, is one example.
    * **Classification** predicts a category, such as "fraud" or "not fraud". Logistic regression, used in Session 3.4, is one example.

* **4. Data Basics: How Linear Regression Works:**
  * Linear regression finds the best straight-line formula: `price = intercept + coefficient_1 x feature_1 + coefficient_2 x feature_2 + ...`. "Best" means the smallest total squared error on the training rows.
  * Reading a coefficient: the change in price for one more unit of that feature, **while the other features stay the same**. Example from Micro-Example 1: `+180.1` per square foot.
  * Coefficients can surprise you. On its own, distance rises with price (correlation `+0.98`), because the big buildings in our data are far from the center. Once size is in the model, each extra mile **lowers** the price by about $1,900: compared at the same size, a farther building is cheaper.
  * Three error measures, in plain words:

    | Measure | Meaning | Lower or higher is better? |
    |---|---|---|
    | MAE (Mean Absolute Error) | the average size of the errors, in dollars | lower |
    | RMSE (Root Mean Squared Error) | like MAE, but large errors count extra | lower |
    | R-squared | the share of the price differences the model explains, from 0 to 1 | higher |

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * File Link: [data/session_03_03_dataset.csv](data/session_03_03_dataset.csv)
  * Audited 14-row commercial property sales dataset (fictional teaching data), one row per sale:
    * `Property_ID`: Sale code (e.g., `PROP-401`).
    * `Square_Feet_Units`: Usable floor area in square feet.
    * `Bedrooms_Count`: Number of separate rooms or office suites.
    * `Distance_To_CBD_Miles`: Distance to the CBD (Central Business District, the city's main business center), in miles.
    * `Local_Tax_Rate_Pct`: Local property tax rate, in percent.
    * `Sale_Price_USD`: The price the property sold for, in U.S. Dollars. This is the target.

* **6. Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `Estimator` (`.fit()` / `.predict()`):
    * What: any Scikit-Learn model; `.fit(X, y)` learns from examples, `.predict(X)` makes predictions
    * Why: every model in the library uses the same two methods
  * `Linear Regression`:
    * What: a model that predicts a number with a straight-line formula
    * Why: simple, fast, and its coefficients are easy to explain to a manager
  * `Logistic Regression`:
    * What: a model that predicts the probability of a yes/no outcome
    * Why: the classification partner of linear regression (Session 3.4)
  * `RMSE` and `MAE`:
    * What: the typical prediction error, in the target's own unit (here, dollars)
    * Why: tells the manager how far off an estimate can be
  * `Cross-Validation`:
    * What: splitting the data into K parts and testing K times, each time on a different part
    * Why: one lucky split can make a model look better than it is
  * `Overfitting`:
    * What: a model that memorizes its training rows and does worse on new data
    * Why: the main risk when data is small; testing on unseen rows reveals it

* **7. Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * **7.1 The estimator pattern: create, fit, predict**
    * *The idea:* Every model in Scikit-Learn, from the simplest to the most advanced, is used in the same three steps. Learn them once and you can use hundreds of models:
      1. **Create** an empty model: `model = LinearRegression()`.
      2. **Fit** it to examples with known answers: `model.fit(X_train, y_train)`. This is where the model learns.
      3. **Predict** answers for rows it has not seen: `model.predict(X_test)`.
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Step | Code | What happens |
      |---|---|---|
      | Create | `model = LinearRegression()` | an empty model, nothing learned yet |
      | Fit | `model.fit(X_train, y_train)` | learns the price formula from 10 sales |
      | Predict | `model.predict(X_test)` | 4 estimated prices for the 4 test sales |
      | Score | `model.score(X_test, y_test)` | `0.996`, the share of price differences explained |

    * *Common mistake:* calling `.predict()` before `.fit()`. The model has nothing learned yet and stops with `NotFittedError`.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `model.intercept_` | `123568.1` | the formula's starting value |
      | `model.coef_` | `[180.1, 14703.6, -1915.8]` | one coefficient per feature, in column order |
      | `model.score(X_train, y_train)` | `0.999` | on the training rows: always looks better |
      | `model.score(X_test, y_test)` | `0.996` | on unseen rows: the honest measure |

    * *Remember:* create, fit, predict; learned values end with `_`.
  * **7.2 Reading the price formula**
    * *The idea:* Linear regression learns a formula of the form price = intercept + coefficient x feature + ... Each coefficient is the change in price for one more unit of that feature **while the other features stay the same**.
    * *See it on real data:* our formula is `123568.1 + 180.1 x square feet + 14703.6 x rooms - 1915.8 x miles`. For a building of 1,500 square feet, 3 rooms, 5 miles from the center:

      | Part | Calculation | Amount |
      |---|---|---|
      | intercept | | `123568.1` |
      | size | `180.1 x 1500` | `270150.0` |
      | rooms | `14703.6 x 3` | `44110.8` |
      | distance | `-1915.8 x 5` | `-9579.0` |
      | **price** | sum | about `428,250` |

      `model.predict()` gives `428,269`: the small difference comes from rounding the coefficients to one decimal in the table.
    * *More examples:* change one feature at a time, starting from that building.

      | Change | Predicted price | Difference |
      |---|---|---|
      | none (1,500 sq ft, 3 rooms, 5 miles) | `428,269` | |
      | 1,000 more square feet | `608,382` | `+180,113`: 1,000 x `180.1` |
      | one more room | `442,973` | `+14,704` |
      | 5 more miles from the center | `418,690` | `-9,579`: 5 x `-1915.8` |

    * *Common mistake:* reading a coefficient alone. On its own, distance rises with price (correlation `+0.98`), and a model with distance as its only feature even gives it a coefficient of `+30,026.8` per mile, because the big buildings in our data are far from the center. Only when size is in the model does the true effect of distance at the same size appear: `-1,915.8` per mile.
    * *Remember:* a coefficient is the effect of one feature with the others held fixed.
  * **7.3 Measuring error: MAE, RMSE, and R-squared**
    * *The idea:* An error is predicted price minus actual price. Three summaries turn the errors into one number each.

      | Measure | How it is computed | Our test set |
      |---|---|---|
      | MAE (Mean Absolute Error) | average of the error sizes | `$9,668` |
      | RMSE (Root Mean Squared Error) | square each error, average, take the square root | `$11,539` |
      | R-squared | share of the price differences the model explains | `0.996` |

    * *See it on real data:* the 4 test errors are `-4,732`, `+15,735`, `+16,064`, and `-2,141` dollars. MAE simply averages their sizes. RMSE squares them first, so the two large errors weigh more, and RMSE comes out higher.
    * *A useful comparison:* a "model" that always guesses the average training price would have a test RMSE of `$186,691`. Ours, at `$11,539`, is about 16 times better.
    * *Common mistake:* trusting R-squared on the training rows (`0.999`). It measures how well the model fits rows it has already seen.
    * *More examples:*

      | Errors | MAE | RMSE | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | errors `+10` and `-10` | `10` | `10` | equal errors: MAE and RMSE agree |
      | errors `0` and `20` | `10` | `14.14` | one large error: RMSE grows, MAE does not |
      | our 4 test errors | `9,668` | `11,539` | two larger errors raise RMSE |
      | always guessing the average price |  | `186,691` | the benchmark any model must beat |

    * *Remember:* MAE is the plain average error; RMSE punishes large errors; test on unseen rows.
  * **7.4 One split is not enough: cross-validation**
    * *The idea:* With only 14 sales, the result depends on which 4 land in the test set. K-fold cross-validation cuts the data into K parts (folds), trains K times, and tests each time on a different fold, so every sale is tested once.
    * *See it on real data:* `cv=5` cuts our 14 sales into 5 folds, in file order.

      | Fold | Test sales | R-squared |
      |---|---|---|
      | 1 | `PROP-400`, `PROP-401`, `PROP-402` | `0.994` |
      | 2 | `PROP-403`, `PROP-404`, `PROP-405` | `0.992` |
      | 3 | `PROP-406`, `PROP-407`, `PROP-408` | `0.999` |
      | 4 | `PROP-409`, `PROP-410`, `PROP-411` | `0.996` |
      | 5 | `PROP-412`, `PROP-413` | `0.999` |

    * *More examples:*

      | Setting | R-squared per fold | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `cv=3` | `[0.994, 0.986, 0.998]` | `0.993` |
      | `cv=5` (ours) | `[0.994, 0.992, 0.999, 0.996, 0.999]` | `0.996` |
      | `cv=7` | `[0.982, 0.986, 0.996, 1.0, 0.959, 0.996, 0.999]` | `0.988`: smaller folds, more variation |
      | `cv=KFold(5, shuffle=True, random_state=1)` | `[0.981, 0.999, 0.998, 0.983, 0.969]` | shuffled folds: different sales in each test |

    * *Remember:* report the average over the folds, and look at the worst fold too.
  * **7.5 One row is still a table: the 2D rule**
    * *The idea:* `.predict()` always expects a table: rows and columns, called 2D (two-dimensional). A single property is a table with one row. A plain list like `[1500, 3, 5.0]` is 1D (one dimension) and is refused.

      | Input | Shape | Result |
      |---|---|---|
      | `[1500, 3, 5.0]` | 1D | `ValueError: Expected 2D array, got 1D array instead` |
      | `[[1500, 3, 5.0]]` | 2D, no column names | a price, plus a warning about missing column names |
      | `pd.DataFrame([[1500, 3, 5.0]], columns=features)` | 2D, with names | `428,269`, no warning |

    * *Common mistake:* predicting far outside the training data. A 10,000 square-foot building gets a "price" of about `$1,959,228`, but our largest sale was 3,400 square feet: the model has never seen such a building, and the number should not be trusted.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `np.array([1500, 3, 5.0]).shape` | `(3,)` | 1D: three values, no rows |
      | `np.array([1500, 3, 5.0]).reshape(1, -1).shape` | `(1, 3)` | 2D: one row, three columns |
      | `model.predict(pd.DataFrame([[1500, 3, 5.0], [2500, 4, 10.0]], columns=features))` | `[428269, 613506]` | two buildings in one call |
      | `model.predict(df[features].iloc[[0]])` | `[361064]` | one existing row; double brackets keep it a table (actual price: `345000`) |

    * *Remember:* one row = a one-row DataFrame with the same column names.

* **8. Development Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE:**
  * `[1]` File Explorer: create your script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S33_demo_1.py`), and check the dataset is in `data/`.
  * `[2]` Code Editor: write a few lines, run, repeat. Small steps keep errors small.
  * `[3]` AI Agent Chat: on an error, capture the traceback (Python's error report) with `Win + Shift + S` (macOS: `Cmd + Shift + 4`), paste it with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why it failed.
  * `[4]` Terminal: run `python` followed by your script name. Return code `0` means the script finished with no errors. First time only: `pip install scikit-learn`.

* **9. Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * Every example has three parts: **1. Input**, **2. Process**, and **3. Output**. Name each file with your own name and the session code, for example `Reacher_S33_demo_1.py` (pattern: `YourName_S33_demo_1.py`).
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Train a Price Model):* `YourName_S33_demo_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the sales and split them
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
    from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_03_dataset.csv")
    features = ["Square_Feet_Units", "Bedrooms_Count", "Distance_To_CBD_Miles"]
    X = df[features]
    y = df["Sale_Price_USD"]
    X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
        X, y, test_size=0.25, random_state=42
    )

    # 2. Process: learn the price formula from the training rows
    model = LinearRegression()
    model.fit(X_train, y_train)

    # 3. Output: the learned formula
    print("Intercept:", round(model.intercept_, 1))
    for name, coef in zip(features, model.coef_):
        print(f"{name}: {coef:,.1f}")
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `features = [...]` lists the 3 input columns; `X = df[features]` and `y = df["Sale_Price_USD"]` separate inputs and answer (Session 3.2).
      * `train_test_split(..., test_size=0.25, random_state=42)` keeps 4 of the 14 sales aside for testing.
      * `model = LinearRegression()` creates an empty model; `model.fit(X_train, y_train)` learns the formula from the 10 training sales (section 7.1).
      * `zip(features, model.coef_)` walks through the feature names and their coefficients side by side, so each line prints a name with its number.
      * `{coef:,.1f}` prints the number with thousands separators and 1 decimal.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Intercept: 123568.1
      Square_Feet_Units: 180.1
      Bedrooms_Count: 14,703.6
      Distance_To_CBD_Miles: -1,915.8
      ```

      * Each extra square foot adds about `$180`, each extra room about `$14,700`, and each extra mile from the center subtracts about `$1,900`, with the other features held fixed (section 7.2).
      * The intercept is the formula's starting point. On its own it has no business meaning: no building has 0 square feet.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`.score()`):*
    * New tool: `model.score(X_test, y_test)` returns R-squared on the test rows in one call. Why: a quick first check of how well the model explains prices it has not seen.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print("Test R-squared:", round(model._____(X_test, y_test), 3))`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `Test R-squared: 0.996`.
  * *Micro-Example 2 (Predict the Test Sales and Measure the Error):* `YourName_S33_demo_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load, split, and train (same as Micro-Example 1)
    import numpy as np
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
    from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
    from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_03_dataset.csv")
    features = ["Square_Feet_Units", "Bedrooms_Count", "Distance_To_CBD_Miles"]
    X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
        df[features], df["Sale_Price_USD"], test_size=0.25, random_state=42
    )
    model = LinearRegression().fit(X_train, y_train)

    # 2. Process: predict the 4 test sales and compute the RMSE
    y_pred = model.predict(X_test)
    rmse = np.sqrt(mean_squared_error(y_test, y_pred))
    comparison = pd.DataFrame({
        "Property_ID": df.loc[X_test.index, "Property_ID"],
        "Actual_USD": y_test,
        "Predicted_USD": y_pred.round(0),
    })

    # 3. Output: actual vs predicted, then the typical error
    print(comparison)
    print(f"Test RMSE: ${rmse:,.0f}")
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * Part 1 loads, splits, and trains, as in Micro-Example 1. `LinearRegression().fit(...)` creates and fits in one line.
      * `model.predict(X_test)` returns 4 estimated prices.
      * `mean_squared_error()` averages the squared errors; `np.sqrt()` (square root, from NumPy) brings the result back to dollars: the RMSE (section 7.3).
      * `pd.DataFrame({...})` builds a comparison table. `df.loc[X_test.index, "Property_ID"]` finds the IDs of the test rows, because the split keeps the original row labels.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
         Property_ID  Actual_USD  Predicted_USD
      9     PROP-409    465000.0       460268.0
      11    PROP-411    330000.0       345735.0
      0     PROP-400    345000.0       361064.0
      12    PROP-412    780000.0       777859.0
      Test RMSE: $11,539
      ```

      * The model is close on the large building (`PROP-412`: off by `$2,141`) and on `PROP-409`.
      * It overprices the two small buildings, `PROP-411` and `PROP-400`, by about `$16,000` each: small buildings deserve a manual check.
      * A typical error of about `$11,500` on prices averaging `$513,000` is about 2%: good enough for a first quote.
  * *Quick Check 2 (`mean_absolute_error()`):*
    * New tool: `mean_absolute_error(y_test, y_pred)` returns the average error size in dollars. Why: the easiest error number to explain: "on average, we are off by this much".
    * Task: change the import line to `from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error, mean_absolute_error`, then add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(f"Test MAE: ${___________________(y_test, y_pred):,.0f}")`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `Test MAE: $9,668`, lower than the RMSE because RMSE weighs the two larger errors (about $16,000 each) more heavily.
  * *Micro-Example 3 (Five Splits Instead of One):* `YourName_S33_demo_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the sales
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
    from sklearn.model_selection import cross_val_score
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_03_dataset.csv")
    features = ["Square_Feet_Units", "Bedrooms_Count", "Distance_To_CBD_Miles"]

    # 2. Process: train and test 5 times, each time on a different part
    scores = cross_val_score(LinearRegression(), df[features],
                             df["Sale_Price_USD"], cv=5, scoring="r2")

    # 3. Output: the 5 scores and their average
    print("R-squared per fold:", scores.round(3).tolist())
    print(f"Average R-squared: {scores.mean():.3f}")
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `cross_val_score(LinearRegression(), X, y, cv=5, scoring="r2")` does all the work of section 7.4: it cuts the 14 sales into 5 folds, trains 5 fresh models, and tests each one on its own fold.
      * It returns 5 scores, one per fold. `.round(3).tolist()` prints them as a plain list.
      * `scores.mean()` averages them.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      R-squared per fold: [0.994, 0.992, 0.999, 0.996, 0.999]
      Average R-squared: 0.996
      ```

      * Every fold scores above `0.99`: the model does well no matter which sales are held out, not just on one lucky split.
      * The average, `0.996`, equals the single split's score from Quick Check 1, which confirms that result.
  * *Quick Check 3 (RMSE per fold):*
    * New tool: `scoring="neg_root_mean_squared_error"` makes `cross_val_score()` return the RMSE of each fold as a negative number (Scikit-Learn treats higher scores as better), so put a minus sign in front to read it. Why: shows the error in dollars for every fold.
    * Task: add two lines to part **3. Output**: `rmse = -cross_val_score(LinearRegression(), df[features], df["Sale_Price_USD"], cv=5, scoring="______________________________")` and `print(rmse.round(0).tolist())`
    * Check: `[7862.0, 15224.0, 4004.0, 8201.0, 3374.0]`. The error ranges from about $3,400 to $15,200 depending on which properties are tested.

  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S33_regression.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S33_regression.py`).
  * *Step 2: Train, Predict, and Evaluate:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: load the sales and split them
    import numpy as np
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
    from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
    from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error, r2_score
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_03_dataset.csv")
    features = ["Square_Feet_Units", "Bedrooms_Count", "Distance_To_CBD_Miles"]
    X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
        df[features], df["Sale_Price_USD"], test_size=0.25, random_state=42
    )

    # 2. Process: train, then predict the test sales
    reg = LinearRegression()
    reg.fit(X_train, y_train)
    y_pred = reg.predict(X_test)
    rmse = np.sqrt(mean_squared_error(y_test, y_pred))
    r2 = r2_score(y_test, y_pred)

    # 3. Output: the formula and the test error
    print("Coefficients:", dict(zip(features, reg.coef_.round(1).tolist())))
    print(f"Test RMSE: ${rmse:,.0f} | R-squared: {r2:.3f}")
    ```
  * *Step 3: Interpret for the Manager:* In three comment lines, explain each coefficient in plain words, using "while the other features stay the same".

  * *Success Check:* coefficients `180.1` (square feet), `14703.6` (rooms), and `-1915.8` (distance); `Test RMSE: $11,539 | R-squared: 0.996`. Each extra mile from the center lowers the price when size and rooms stay the same.

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Error per Property and a One-Row Prediction:**

Use `abs(y_test - y_pred)` for the absolute error. Then print the average `Error_Pct`, called MAPE (Mean Absolute Percentage Error).
