Capture the traceback with `Win + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and ask what "2D" means.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - Does a Bigger Model Price Better?:**
  * *Goal:* The firm could collect more features for every quote, but collecting data costs money. Find out, with 5-fold cross-validation, whether extra features really reduce the pricing error.
  * *What you need:*
    * File: `data/session_03_03_dataset.csv` (described in **5. Lab Practice Dataset**).
    * Script name: `YourName_S33_challenge.py`.
    * Tools you already know: `LinearRegression`, `cross_val_score()` with `scoring="neg_root_mean_squared_error"` (Quick Check 3), dictionaries, `for` loops, and `pd.DataFrame()`.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. Define four candidate feature lists in a dictionary:
       * `"A: size only"`: `["Square_Feet_Units"]`
       * `"B: size + distance"`: `["Square_Feet_Units", "Distance_To_CBD_Miles"]`
       * `"C: size + rooms + distance"`: `["Square_Feet_Units", "Bedrooms_Count", "Distance_To_CBD_Miles"]`
       * `"D: all four"`: the three above plus `"Local_Tax_Rate_Pct"`
    2. For each candidate, run 5-fold cross-validation with `cv=5` and RMSE scoring, and record the average RMSE and the largest fold RMSE, both rounded to whole dollars, plus the number of features.
    3. Collect the results in one DataFrame with the columns `Model`, `Features`, `Avg_RMSE_USD`, and `Worst_Fold_RMSE_USD`, sorted by `Avg_RMSE_USD` from lowest to highest.
    4. Apply the firm's rule: choose the model with the lowest average RMSE, **unless** a model with fewer features is within `$500` of it; then choose the model with the fewest features among those within `$500`.
    5. Print the table and the chosen model with one sentence that explains the choice.
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * Looping over a dictionary: `for name, cols in candidates.items():` gives each key (`name`) and its value (`cols`) in turn.
    * Collecting rows: start with `rows = []`, append one dictionary per model, such as `rows.append({"Model": name, "Features": len(cols), ...})`, and finish with `pd.DataFrame(rows)`.
    * `.max()` on the fold results gives the worst fold.
  * *Hints:*
    * First print the 5 fold values of each model before you average them.
    * For the rule, first keep the rows with `Avg_RMSE_USD <= best + 500`, then sort them by `Features`.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Average RMSE: C `$7,733`, B `$7,837`, A `$7,957`, D `$9,296`. Worst folds: C `$15,224`, B `$10,415`, A `$10,279`, D `$15,463`.
    * Within `$500` of the best (`$7,733`): C, B, and A. Chosen: **A: size only**.
    * Business meaning: in these 14 deals, adding rooms and distance cut the average error by only about $224, while adding the tax rate made it worse by about $1,563. The firm can quote from floor area alone and spend its data budget on collecting more deals instead.

<a id="session-03-04"></a>
### Session 3.4: Core Concepts of Machine Learning: Supervised vs. Unsupervised Learning and Evaluation Metrics

![Session 3.4 Map](assets/session_03_04_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_03_04_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_03_04_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 12 (115/11/29 - 115/12/05) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **1. Session Purpose:**
  * A card issuer's fraud model gives every transaction a risk score from 1 to 100; above a cut-off score (the threshold), the transaction is held and the customer gets a verification text
  * The two possible mistakes have very different costs: a false alarm costs about $5 of checking, while a missed fraud costs the stolen money
  * The fraud operations manager asks: at what risk score should we stop a transaction, and what does each kind of mistake cost us? Today, you judge a model by business cost, not by a single accuracy number

* **2. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "Which kind of learning fits this problem?": tell supervised learning (learning from examples with known answers) from unsupervised learning (finding groups without answers), and group transactions with K-Means
  2. "How good is the risk score, really?": read a confusion matrix (a 2x2 count of right and wrong decisions) and calculate precision, recall, and ROC-AUC
  3. "Where should we set the threshold?": choose the threshold with the lowest business cost, and fix the `ValueError` raised when scores are passed in place of 0/1 decisions

* **3. Business Context: Not All Mistakes Cost the Same:**
  * Every decision on a transaction lands in one of four boxes:

    | | Model says: fraud (hold it) | Model says: legitimate (let it pass) |
    |---|---|---|
    | **Really fraud** | TP (True Positive): fraud caught | FN (False Negative): fraud missed, the money is lost |
    | **Really legitimate** | FP (False Positive): false alarm, about $5 to check | TN (True Negative): normal purchase, no cost |

  * In our cost model, a missed fraud costs `$1,500` and a false alarm costs `$5`, so one miss costs as much as 300 false alarms.
  * The accuracy trap: if only 1% of transactions are fraud, a model that says "never fraud" is 99% accurate and catches nothing. Accuracy hides the costly mistakes.

* **4. Data Basics: Reading a Classifier's Report Card:**
  * Four measures, each answering one question:

    | Measure | Formula | Question it answers |
    |---|---|---|
    | Accuracy | (TP + TN) / all | What share of all decisions was right? |
    | Precision | TP / (TP + FP) | Of the transactions we held, what share was really fraud? |
    | Recall | TP / (TP + FN) | Of all the fraud, what share did we catch? |
    | ROC-AUC | area under the ROC curve, from 0.5 to 1.0 | How well does the score rank fraud above legitimate transactions, at every threshold? |

  * A higher threshold holds fewer transactions: fewer false alarms (higher precision) but more missed fraud (lower recall). The threshold moves this trade-off; the business cost decides where it should sit.
  * ROC-AUC (Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic Curve): `0.5` means the score ranks no better than a coin flip; `1.0` means every fraud scores above every legitimate transaction.
  * Supervised vs unsupervised:
    * **Supervised:** past examples come with the answer (`Actual_Fraud_Binary`), and the model learns to predict it. Today's risk score is a supervised model's output.
    * **Unsupervised:** no answer column; the method finds groups of similar rows by itself. K-Means is the most common example.

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * File Link: [data/session_03_04_dataset.csv](data/session_03_04_dataset.csv)
  * Audited 16-row card transaction dataset (fictional teaching data), one row per transaction, checked afterwards by fraud investigators:
    * `Transaction_ID`: Transaction code (e.g., `F-TXN-8001`).
    * `Transaction_Amount_USD`: Purchase amount in U.S. Dollars.
    * `Foreign_IP_Flag`: `1` if the purchase came from a foreign IP (Internet Protocol) address, the network address of the device; `0` if domestic.
    * `Failed_Login_Attempts_Count`: Failed login attempts before the purchase.
    * `Cardholder_Risk_Score`: The fraud model's risk score, from 1 (safe) to 100 (very risky).
    * `Actual_Fraud_Binary`: The investigators' answer: `1` = fraud, `0` = legitimate. 7 of the 16 transactions were fraud.

* **6. Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `Supervised vs Unsupervised Learning`:
    * What: learning with known answers vs finding groups without answers
    * Why: the first question to ask about any ML project
  * `Threshold`:
    * What: the cut-off score above which the model's answer becomes "yes"
    * Why: the business sets it, not the model
  * `Confusion Matrix`:
    * What: the 2x2 count of TP, FP, FN, and TN
    * Why: shows each kind of mistake separately, so each can be priced
  * `Precision` and `Recall`:
    * What: the share of holds that were fraud, and the share of fraud that was held
    * Why: the two sides of the trade-off a threshold controls
  * `ROC-AUC`:
    * What: one number for how well a score ranks positives above negatives
    * Why: compares scoring models before any threshold is chosen
  * `K-Means Clustering`:
    * What: an unsupervised method that splits rows into K groups of similar values
    * Why: finds customer or transaction segments when no labels exist

* **7. Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * **7.1 From a score to a decision: the threshold**
    * *The idea:* A fraud model does not say "fraud" or "not fraud". It gives each transaction a score, here from 1 to 100. The business then chooses a **threshold**: every transaction scoring at or above it is held for checking. The model ranks; the business decides where to draw the line.
    * *See it on real data:* the 9 highest-scoring transactions, sorted by score.

      | Transaction | Score | Really fraud? | Held at 70? | Held at 75? |
      |---|---|---|---|---|
      | `F-TXN-8006` | `22` | no | no | no |
      | `F-TXN-8009` | `71` | no | **yes** (false alarm) | no |
      | `F-TXN-8005` | `74` | yes | yes | **no** (missed fraud) |
      | `F-TXN-8015` | `76` | yes | yes | yes |
      | `F-TXN-8001` | `78` | yes | yes | yes |
      | `F-TXN-8011` to `F-TXN-8007` | `85` to `92` | yes | yes | yes |

      Moving the line from 70 to 75 removes one false alarm but lets one fraud through.
    * *In code:* `(df["Cardholder_Risk_Score"] >= 70).astype(int)` turns every score into `1` (hold) or `0` (pass).
    * *More examples:*

      | Threshold | Transactions held | Who they are |
      |---|---|---|
      | `50` | `8` | all 7 frauds + 1 false alarm |
      | `70` | `8` | the same 8 |
      | `72` | `7` | exactly the 7 frauds |
      | `80` | `4` | 3 frauds slip through |
      | `90` | `1` | only the riskiest one |

    * *Remember:* the model gives scores; the threshold turns them into decisions.
  * **7.2 The confusion matrix: four kinds of outcome**
    * *The idea:* Every decision lands in one of four boxes. Counting them separately shows not only how often the model is right, but **which** mistakes it makes, and those mistakes have different costs.

      | | Held (`1`) | Passed (`0`) |
      |---|---|---|
      | **Really fraud** | TP: fraud caught | FN: fraud missed |
      | **Really legitimate** | FP: false alarm | TN: normal purchase passed |

    * *See it on real data:* at threshold 70, `confusion_matrix(y_true, y_pred)` returns `[[8 1] [0 7]]`. Scikit-Learn puts the "really legitimate" row first:

      | | Passed | Held |
      |---|---|---|
      | Really legitimate | TN `8` | FP `1` |
      | Really fraud | FN `0` | TP `7` |

    * *Common mistake:* reading the matrix in the wrong order. `.ravel()` flattens it as `tn, fp, fn, tp`, always in that order.
    * *More examples:* the four counts at other thresholds.

      | Threshold | TP | FP | FN | TN |
      |---|---|---|---|---|
      | `50` | `7` | `1` | `0` | `8` |
      | `72` | `7` | `0` | `0` | `9` |
      | `80` | `4` | `0` | `3` | `9` |
      | `90` | `1` | `0` | `6` | `9` |

    * *Remember:* TP and TN are right; FP and FN are the two different mistakes.
  * **7.3 Accuracy, precision, and recall**
    * *The idea:* Three measures answer three different questions:
      * **Accuracy** = (TP + TN) / all: what share of all decisions was right?
      * **Precision** = TP / (TP + FP): of the transactions we held, what share was really fraud?
      * **Recall** = TP / (TP + FN): of all the fraud, what share did we catch?
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Threshold | Accuracy | Precision | Recall |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | `70` | `0.938` | `0.875` | `1.000` |
      | `75` | `0.938` | `1.000` | `0.857` |
      | `80` | `0.812` | `1.000` | `0.571` |
      | `90` | `0.625` | `1.000` | `0.143` |

      Raising the threshold makes every hold more certain (precision up) but lets more fraud through (recall down).
    * *The accuracy trap:* a "model" that never holds anything is right on every legitimate transaction: `9` of `16`, an accuracy of `0.562`, while catching no fraud at all. In real card data, where fraud is often under 1% of transactions, such a useless model would be over 99% accurate.
    * *More examples:* the F1-score combines precision and recall into one number (their harmonic mean); it is high only when both are high.

      | Threshold | F1-score |
      |---|---|
      | `50` | `0.933` |
      | `72` | `1.000` |
      | `75` | `0.923` |
      | `80` | `0.727` |
      | `90` | `0.250` |

    * *Remember:* precision asks "were the holds right?"; recall asks "did we catch the fraud?"
  * **7.4 ROC-AUC: ranking quality at every threshold**
    * *The idea:* ROC-AUC (Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic Curve) judges a score before any threshold is chosen. It answers: if you pick one fraud and one legitimate transaction at random, how often does the fraud get the higher score? `0.5` is a coin flip; `1.0` means every fraud scores above every legitimate transaction.
    * *See it on real data:* `roc_auc_score(y_true, scores)` takes the scores themselves, not 0/1 decisions.

      | Score used | ROC-AUC | Meaning |
      |---|---|---|
      | `Cardholder_Risk_Score` | `1.000` | every fraud (`74`-`92`) above every legitimate one (`12`-`71`) |
      | `Transaction_Amount_USD` | `0.984` | amount alone ranks almost as well |
      | `Failed_Login_Attempts_Count` | `0.984` | so do failed logins |
      | `Foreign_IP_Flag` | `0.944` | a yes/no flag ranks less finely |

    * *Common mistake:* passing 0/1 decisions to `roc_auc_score()`, or scores to `confusion_matrix()`. The first loses information; the second raises `ValueError: Classification metrics can't handle a mix of binary and continuous targets`.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `roc_auc_score(y_true, 100 - scores)` | `0.0` | a score that is perfectly backwards |
      | `roc_auc_score(y_true, [50] * 16)` | `0.5` | the same score for everyone: a coin flip |
      | `roc_auc_score(y_true, scores / 100)` | `1.0` | rescaling does not change the ranking |

    * *Remember:* AUC compares scores; precision and recall judge decisions.
  * **7.5 Unsupervised learning: groups without answers**
    * *The idea:* Supervised learning learns from examples **with** answers, such as investigated fraud cases. Unsupervised learning has no answer column: it looks for groups of similar rows by itself. K-Means, the most common method, places K group centers and assigns every row to its nearest center, moving the centers until the groups stop changing.
    * *See it on real data:* K-Means on amount and risk score only (the fraud column is not used).

      | K (number of groups) | Group sizes | Inertia (spread inside the groups; lower is tighter) |
      |---|---|---|
      | `2` | `8`, `8` | `3.93` |
      | `3` | `4`, `8`, `4` | `0.70` |
      | `4` | `4`, `8`, `2`, `2` | `0.38` |

      Inertia always falls as K grows, but the big drop is from 2 to 3 groups; after that, extra groups only split small groups further. This "elbow" suggests K = 3.
    * *Common mistake:* expecting K-Means to name the groups. It only numbers them `0`, `1`, `2`; a person reads each group's profile and gives it a business name, such as "small, safe purchases".
    * *More examples:* `kmeans.predict()` places new rows (scaled the same way) into the existing groups.

      | Code or input | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | a new purchase: `$5,000`, score `95` | group `2` | large, high-risk purchases |
      | a new purchase: `$40`, score `10` | group `1` | small, safe purchases |
      | a new purchase: `$1,900`, score `73` | group `0` | mid-size, higher-risk purchases |
      | `kmeans.cluster_centers_.shape` | `(3, 2)` | 3 group centers, 2 features each |

    * *Remember:* supervised = learn the answer; unsupervised = find the groups, then name them.

* **8. Development Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE:**
  * `[1]` File Explorer: create your script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S34_demo_1.py`), and check the dataset is in `data/`.
  * `[2]` Code Editor: write a few lines, run, repeat. Small steps keep errors small.
  * `[3]` AI Agent Chat: on an error, capture the traceback (Python's error report) with `Win + Shift + S` (macOS: `Cmd + Shift + 4`), paste it with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why it failed.
  * `[4]` Terminal: run `python` followed by your script name. Return code `0` means the script finished with no errors. First time only: `pip install scikit-learn`.

* **9. Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * Every example has three parts: **1. Input**, **2. Process**, and **3. Output**. Name each file with your own name and the session code, for example `Reacher_S34_demo_1.py` (pattern: `YourName_S34_demo_1.py`).
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Confusion Matrix at Threshold 70):* `YourName_S34_demo_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the investigators' answers and the model's scores
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.metrics import confusion_matrix, precision_score, recall_score
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_04_dataset.csv")
    y_true = df["Actual_Fraud_Binary"]

    # 2. Process: hold every transaction scoring 70 or more
    y_pred = (df["Cardholder_Risk_Score"] >= 70).astype(int)
    cm = confusion_matrix(y_true, y_pred)
    precision = precision_score(y_true, y_pred)
    recall = recall_score(y_true, y_pred)

    # 3. Output: the 2x2 table, then the two key measures
    print("Rows: really legitimate, really fraud | Columns: passed, held\n", cm)
    print(f"Precision: {precision:.3f} | Recall: {recall:.3f}")
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `y_true` holds the investigators' answers: `1` for fraud, `0` for legitimate.
      * `(df["Cardholder_Risk_Score"] >= 70).astype(int)` turns each score into a decision at threshold 70 (section 7.1).
      * `confusion_matrix(y_true, y_pred)` counts the four outcomes (section 7.2).
      * `precision_score()` and `recall_score()` compute the two key measures (section 7.3).
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Rows: really legitimate, really fraud | Columns: passed, held
       [[8 1]
       [0 7]]
      Precision: 0.875 | Recall: 1.000
      ```

      * Top row: 8 legitimate transactions passed, 1 held by mistake (`F-TXN-8009`, score `71`).
      * Bottom row: 0 frauds missed, all 7 held.
      * Recall `1.000`: every fraud was caught. Precision `0.875`: 7 of the 8 holds were real fraud.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`classification_report()`):*
    * New tool: `classification_report(y_true, y_pred)` prints precision, recall, and the F1-score (one number that balances precision and recall) for both classes, plus accuracy. Why: the full report card in one call.
    * Task: add `classification_report` to the import line, then add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(_____________________(y_true, y_pred))`
    * Check: the `accuracy` line shows `0.94`, and the row for class `1` shows precision `0.88` and recall `1.00`.
  * *Micro-Example 2 (ROC-AUC: Ranking Quality at Every Threshold):* `YourName_S34_demo_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the answers and the scores
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_04_dataset.csv")
    y_true = df["Actual_Fraud_Binary"]

    # 2. Process: AUC uses the scores directly, not 0/1 decisions
    auc = roc_auc_score(y_true, df["Cardholder_Risk_Score"])

    # 3. Output: compare the score ranges of the two groups
    print(f"ROC-AUC: {auc:.3f}")
    print(df.groupby("Actual_Fraud_Binary")["Cardholder_Risk_Score"].agg(["min", "max"]))
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `roc_auc_score(y_true, df["Cardholder_Risk_Score"])` uses the raw scores, because it checks every possible threshold at once (section 7.4).
      * `df.groupby("Actual_Fraud_Binary")["Cardholder_Risk_Score"].agg(["min", "max"])` shows the lowest and highest score in each group, to see why the AUC is what it is.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      ROC-AUC: 1.000
                           min  max
      Actual_Fraud_Binary
      0                     12   71
      1                     74   92
      ```

      * Legitimate transactions score from `12` to `71`; fraud from `74` to `92`. The two ranges do not overlap, so the ranking is perfect: AUC `1.000`.
      * Any threshold from `72` to `74` would separate them exactly. With only 16 transactions, treat this as encouraging, not as proof.
  * *Quick Check 2 (`accuracy_score()`):*
    * New tool: `accuracy_score(y_true, y_pred)` returns the share of correct decisions. Why: shows how a single accuracy number can look good while hiding the costly mistakes.
    * Task: add two lines to part **3. Output**: `from sklearn.metrics import accuracy_score` and `print(______________(y_true, (df["Cardholder_Risk_Score"] >= 90).astype(int)))`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `0.625`. At threshold 90, 62.5% of the decisions are right, yet 6 of the 7 frauds pass through.
  * *Micro-Example 3 (K-Means: Groups Without Answers):* `YourName_S34_demo_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: two features only; the fraud column is NOT used
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.cluster import KMeans
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_04_dataset.csv")
    features = df[["Transaction_Amount_USD", "Cardholder_Risk_Score"]]

    # 2. Process: scale, then form 3 groups of similar transactions
    scaled = StandardScaler().fit_transform(features)
    kmeans = KMeans(n_clusters=3, random_state=42, n_init=10)
    df["Cluster_ID"] = kmeans.fit_predict(scaled)

    # 3. Output: the average profile of each group, and its fraud share
    profile = df.groupby("Cluster_ID")[
        ["Transaction_Amount_USD", "Cardholder_Risk_Score", "Actual_Fraud_Binary"]
    ].mean().round(1)
    print(profile)
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * Only two features go in: amount and risk score. The fraud column is deliberately left out; this is unsupervised learning (section 7.5).
      * `StandardScaler().fit_transform(features)` puts both on one scale (Session 3.2), so the dollar amounts do not outweigh the scores.
      * `KMeans(n_clusters=3, random_state=42, n_init=10)` asks for 3 groups; `n_init=10` tries 10 starting points and keeps the best; `random_state=42` makes the result repeatable.
      * `.fit_predict(scaled)` finds the groups and returns each row's group number, stored as `Cluster_ID`.
      * The `groupby()` table averages each group's columns, including the fraud column, which K-Means never saw.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
                  Transaction_Amount_USD  Cardholder_Risk_Score  Actual_Fraud_Binary
      Cluster_ID
      0                           1795.0                   74.8                  0.8
      1                             52.2                   16.1                  0.0
      2                           3587.5                   88.5                  1.0
      ```

      * Group `1`: small, low-risk purchases, no fraud. Group `0`: mid-size, higher-risk purchases, 80% fraud. Group `2`: large, high-risk purchases, all fraud.
      * The group numbers have no meaning in themselves; a person names the groups from these profiles.
      * Without seeing the answers, K-Means found groups that line up with fraud: a sign that amount and score carry the fraud pattern.
  * *Quick Check 3 (`.size()`):*
    * New tool: `df.groupby("Column").size()` counts the rows in each group. Why: a segment's size matters as much as its profile.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(df.groupby("Cluster_ID").____())`
    * Check: group sizes `4`, `8`, and `4`.

  * [Scikit-Learn: Clustering (K-Means Algorithm Overview)](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/clustering.html#k-means).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Fraud Model Cost Report:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S34_evaluation.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S34_evaluation.py`).
  * *Step 2: Count the Outcomes and Price the Mistakes:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the answers and the scores
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.metrics import confusion_matrix, precision_score, recall_score
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_04_dataset.csv")
    y_true = df["Actual_Fraud_Binary"]

    # 2. Process: decisions at threshold 70, then the cost of the mistakes
    y_pred = (df["Cardholder_Risk_Score"] >= 70).astype(int)
    tn, fp, fn, tp = confusion_matrix(y_true, y_pred).ravel()
    false_alarm_cost = 5.0
    missed_fraud_cost = 1500.0
    total_cost = fp * false_alarm_cost + fn * missed_fraud_cost

    # 3. Output: counts, measures, and the cost
    print(f"TP={tp}, FP={fp}, FN={fn}, TN={tn}")
    print(f"Precision={precision_score(y_true, y_pred):.2f}, "
          f"Recall={recall_score(y_true, y_pred):.2f}")
    print(f"Cost of mistakes: ${total_cost:,.2f}")
    ```
    * Two strings written next to each other inside `print()` are joined into one; this keeps long lines readable.
  * *Step 3: Interpret:* In two comment lines, explain why one missed fraud costs as much as 300 false alarms, and what that means for where the threshold should sit.
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:* Run `python YourName_S34_evaluation.py` in Panel `[4]` and confirm return code `0`.
  * *Success Check:* `TP=7, FP=1, FN=0, TN=8`; `Precision=0.88, Recall=1.00`; `Cost of mistakes: $5.00`.

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Find the Cheapest Threshold:**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In `YourName_S34_practice.py`, test thresholds from 50 to 90 in steps of 5 and find the one with the lowest cost.
  * *Task 1 (Threshold Loop):* Loop with `for threshold in range(50, 95, 5):`. For each threshold, compute the decisions, `tn, fp, fn, tp`, and the cost (false alarm `$5`, missed fraud `$1,500`). Append one dictionary per threshold to a list, and turn the list into a DataFrame with `Threshold`, `TP`, `FP`, `FN`, `TN`, and `Cost_USD`. `range(50, 95, 5)` gives 50, 55, ..., 90; the stop value 95 itself is not included.
  * *Task 2 (Read the ValueError & AI Diagnosis):* Inside the loop, try `confusion_matrix(y_true, df["Cardholder_Risk_Score"] / 100)`, passing the scores instead of the decisions. The script stops with `ValueError: Classification metrics can't handle a mix of binary and continuous targets`. Capture the traceback with `Win + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and ask how to turn scores into decisions.
  * *Task 3 (Choose and Save):* Fix the call with `(score >= threshold).astype(int)`. Choose the threshold with the lowest cost; if several tie, choose the highest of them, because it sends the fewest customers to verification on future transactions. Save the table to `data/fraud_threshold_costs.csv` with `index=False`.
  * *Success Check:* thresholds 50 to 70 cost `$5` (1 false alarm); 75 costs `$1,500`; 80 and 85 cost `$4,500`; 90 costs `$9,000`. Chosen threshold: `70`.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - Price the Misses with Real Amounts:**
  * *Goal:* The flat `$1,500` per missed fraud is a guess. Replace it with each missed transaction's real amount, test every possible threshold, and show the manager how much the guess was off.
  * *What you need:*
    * File: `data/session_03_04_dataset.csv` (described in **5. Lab Practice Dataset**).
    * Script name: `YourName_S34_challenge.py`.
    * Tools you already know: Boolean masks with `&` and `~` (Session 1.4), `.sum()`, `for` loops, `range()`, lists of dictionaries, and `pd.DataFrame()`.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. For every whole-number threshold from 1 to 100, mark the held transactions: `held = df["Cardholder_Risk_Score"] >= threshold`.
    2. Compute two costs for each threshold:
       * `Flat_Cost_USD`: false alarms x `$5` + missed frauds x `$1,500`
       * `Real_Cost_USD`: false alarms x `$5` + the **sum of `Transaction_Amount_USD`** of the missed frauds
    3. Put all 100 results in one DataFrame with `Threshold`, `False_Alarms`, `Missed_Frauds`, `Flat_Cost_USD`, and `Real_Cost_USD`.
    4. Print every threshold with the lowest `Real_Cost_USD`.
    5. Print the rows for thresholds `70`, `75`, `80`, and `90`, and add `Gap_USD` = `Real_Cost_USD` - `Flat_Cost_USD`.
    6. In two comment lines, recommend a threshold and explain why the flat guess is risky.
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * A false alarm is a held legitimate transaction: `held & (df["Actual_Fraud_Binary"] == 0)`. A missed fraud is a passed fraud: `~held & (df["Actual_Fraud_Binary"] == 1)`. `~` flips `True` and `False`.
    * Summing money over a mask: `df.loc[missed, "Transaction_Amount_USD"].sum()` adds up the amounts of the missed frauds only.
    * Selecting several rows by value: `results[results["Threshold"].isin([70, 75, 80, 90])]`.
  * *Hints:*
    * Inside the loop, compute the two masks first, then both costs, then append one dictionary.
    * `range(1, 101)` gives 1, 2, ..., 100.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Lowest real cost: `$0` at thresholds `72`, `73`, and `74`: every fraud held and no false alarm.
    * Gaps: threshold 70: `$0` (`$5` both ways); 75: `$600` (`$1,500` flat vs `$2,100` real); 80: `$830` (`$4,500` vs `$5,330`); 90: `$6,480` (`$9,000` vs `$15,480`).
    * Business meaning: the missed frauds at high thresholds are the large ones, so the flat guess understates the loss more and more as the threshold rises. On these 16 past transactions the safest choice is `72` to `74`, but a perfect result on so few rows will not last; re-check the threshold every month on new investigated cases, and keep using real amounts in the cost.

## Module 4: Strategic AI Integration, Enterprise Decision-Making, and AI Governance

Module 4 focuses on executive deployment, decision integration, and risk management. Students examine how machine learning predictions translate into operational policies, explore dynamic pricing and churn mitigation algorithms, and evaluate ethical considerations including algorithmic bias, data privacy, and artificial intelligence governance.

<a id="session-04-01"></a>
### Session 4.1: Integration of Business Analytics and Enterprise AI Architecture: End-to-End Decision Systems

![Session 4.1 Map](assets/session_04_01_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_04_01_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_04_01_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 13 (115/12/06 - 115/12/12) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **1. Session Purpose:**
  * A model that lives in an analyst's notebook creates no value; value comes only when its predictions trigger real actions, every day, without a person running the code
  * The card issuer from Session 3.4 wants its fraud model to score each night's transactions automatically and hold the risky ones
  * The head of operations asks: how do we put the fraud model to work every night, and how will we know when it starts to fail? Today, you save a trained model, reuse it on new data, and build the checks that warn you when the world changes

* **2. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "How does a model get from training to daily use?": map an end-to-end decision system (data in, checks, model, action), and save and reload a model with `joblib`
  2. "Is new data still like the training data?": detect data drift (a change in the incoming data that makes a model less reliable) by comparing averages
  3. "What if something breaks at night?": monitor latency and accuracy from a run log, and handle a missing model file (`FileNotFoundError`) with a safe fallback

* **3. Business Context: From a Model to a Decision System:**
  * A decision system has four stages, run every night as one batch (a group of records processed together):

    | Stage | What happens | Example |
    |---|---|---|
    | Ingestion | collect the day's data | 120,000 card transactions |
    | Validation | check it before use | no missing values, all columns present |
    | Scoring | the model gives each record a probability | fraud probability from 0 to 1 |
    | Action | a business rule turns scores into actions | hold every transaction at 0.5 or above |

  * Batch vs real-time:
    * **Batch** scoring runs on a schedule, such as every night, for many records at once. It is cheaper, and fine when decisions can wait a few hours.
    * **Real-time** scoring answers one request within milliseconds, for example while a customer waits at the checkout. It is faster but costs more to build and run.
  * Models get worse over time, because customers and markets change. Two warning signs to watch:
    * **Data drift:** the new data no longer looks like the training data (for example, purchases are much larger in the holiday season)
    * **Performance decay:** accuracy on recent, checked cases falls

* **4. Data Basics: Saving a Model and Watching It:**
  * Training is slow and done rarely; scoring is fast and done often. So the trained model is **saved to a file** once, then **loaded** by the nightly job:
    * `joblib.dump(model, "models/fraud_model.joblib")` saves it
    * `joblib.load("models/fraud_model.joblib")` loads it back, ready to predict, with no retraining
  * A simple drift check compares each feature's average in the new batch with its average in the training data:
    * change in percent = (new average - training average) / training average x 100
    * Our rule: flag a feature when the change is more than 20% in either direction
  * A run log records every night's stages with their record counts and latency (how long a stage took, in milliseconds; 1,000 ms = 1 second). Rising latency or falling accuracy is an early warning.

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * Three files (all fictional teaching data):
    * Training data: [data/session_03_04_dataset.csv](data/session_03_04_dataset.csv), the 16 investigated transactions from Session 3.4. The model uses `Transaction_Amount_USD`, `Foreign_IP_Flag`, and `Failed_Login_Attempts_Count` to predict `Actual_Fraud_Binary`.
    * New batch: [data/session_04_01_new_batch.csv](data/session_04_01_new_batch.csv), 8 new transactions from a holiday-season night, with the same three feature columns and no answer column.
    * Run log: [data/session_04_01_dataset.csv](data/session_04_01_dataset.csv), 20 rows: 5 nights x 4 stages.
  * Run log columns:
    * `Run_Date`: The night of the run, `2024-12-01` to `2024-12-05`.
    * `Pipeline_Stage`: `Ingestion`, `Validation`, `Scoring`, or `Action`.
    * `Records_Count`: Records processed by the stage. For `Action`, the number of held transactions.
    * `Latency_Ms`: Time the stage took, in milliseconds.
    * `Model_Accuracy`: Accuracy on that night's investigated cases. Filled only for the `Scoring` stage; empty (`NaN`) for the others.

* **6. Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `End-to-End Decision System`:
    * What: ingestion, validation, scoring, and action joined into one automatic run
    * Why: turns predictions into business actions without manual steps
  * `Batch vs Real-Time Scoring`:
    * What: many records on a schedule vs one record on request
    * Why: the business deadline decides which one is worth its cost
  * `Model Persistence` (`joblib`):
    * What: saving a trained model to a file and loading it later
    * Why: train once, score every night
  * `Data Drift`:
    * What: new data that no longer looks like the training data
    * Why: a model is only reliable on data like the data it learned from
  * `Monitoring`:
    * What: tracking counts, latency, and accuracy for every run
    * Why: catches a failing system before customers do
  * `Fallback`:
    * What: a simple, safe rule used when the model is not available
    * Why: the business keeps running when something breaks

* **7. Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * **7.1 Train once, use many times: saving a model**
    * *The idea:* Training a model is slow and done rarely; using it is fast and done every night. So the trained model is saved to a file once, and the nightly job loads that file instead of training again. `joblib.dump(model, path)` saves it; `joblib.load(path)` loads it back, exactly as it was, ready to predict.
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Step | Code | Result |
      |---|---|---|
      | Save | `joblib.dump(model, "models/fraud_model.joblib")` | a small file, about 1.7 KB |
      | Load | `loaded = joblib.load("models/fraud_model.joblib")` | the same trained model |
      | Check | `(model.predict_proba(X) == loaded.predict_proba(X)).all()` | `True`: identical predictions |

    * *Why save the whole `Pipeline`:* the scaler and the model are saved together, so the nightly job scales new data with the training averages (`1371.72`, `0.5`, `1.56`), exactly as during training. Saving only the model and re-fitting a scaler on tonight's data would be data leakage (Session 3.2).
    * *Common mistake:* a wrong path. `joblib.load("models/fraud_modle.joblib")` raises `FileNotFoundError: [Errno 2] No such file or directory`. Check first with `os.path.exists(path)`.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `os.makedirs("models", exist_ok=True)` | creates the folder | `exist_ok=True`: no error if it is already there |
      | `os.path.exists("models/fraud_model.joblib")` | `True` | the file is in place |
      | `os.path.exists("models/nope.joblib")` | `False` | check before loading |
      | `list(loaded.named_steps)` | `['scale', 'classify']` | the loaded pipeline still has both steps |

    * *Remember:* save the whole pipeline once; load it every night.
  * **7.2 Probabilities, not just yes or no**
    * *The idea:* `model.predict(X)` returns a decision, `1` or `0`, using a cut-off of 0.5. `model.predict_proba(X)` returns the probabilities behind it: two numbers per row, the probability of class `0` and of class `1`, which always add up to 1. With probabilities, the business can choose its own cut-off and rank cases by risk.
    * *See it on real data:* the first two new transactions.

      | Transaction | `predict_proba` | `[:, 1]` (fraud probability) | `predict` |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | `N-TXN-9001` | `[0.138, 0.862]` | `0.862` | `1` |
      | `N-TXN-9002` | `[0.930, 0.070]` | `0.070` | `0` |

      `[:, 1]` means "all rows (`:`), second column (`1`)".
    * *More examples:*

      | Code (`proba = model.predict_proba(X)`) | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `proba.sum(axis=1)` | `1.0` for every row | the two probabilities always add up to 1 |
      | `(proba[:, 1] >= 0.9).sum()` | `2` | a strict cut-off holds only the 2 clearest cases |
      | `(proba[:, 1] >= 0.5).sum()` | `4` | the default cut-off |
      | `(proba[:, 1] >= 0.1).sum()` | `5` | a cautious cut-off also holds `N-TXN-9006` (`0.163`) |
      | `proba[:, 1].mean()` | `0.499` | the expected share of fraud in tonight's batch |

    * *Remember:* use probabilities when the cut-off is a business decision.
  * **7.3 Data drift: is tonight's data like the training data?**
    * *The idea:* A model learned its patterns from past data. If new data looks very different, for example much larger purchases in the holiday season, the model is being asked about situations it rarely saw, and its scores become less reliable. A simple check compares each feature's average in the new batch with its average in the training data.
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Feature | Training average | Tonight's average | Change |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | `Transaction_Amount_USD` | `1371.72` | `2555.00` | `+86.3`% (drift) |
      | `Foreign_IP_Flag` | `0.50` | `0.50` | `0.0`% |
      | `Failed_Login_Attempts_Count` | `1.56` | `1.75` | `+12.2`% |

      2 of tonight's 8 purchases are larger than the largest training purchase (`4200.0`): the biggest is `5200.0`.
    * *Common mistake:* checking only for increases. A feature that falls by 30% has drifted too; use the absolute change, `.abs()`.
    * *More examples:*

      | Measure | Training | Tonight | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | median amount | `684.7` | `2150.0` | `+214.0`%: the median shows the shift even more clearly |
      | largest amount | `4200.0` | `5200.0` | tonight goes beyond anything seen in training |
      | purchases above the training maximum |  | `2` of `8` | scores for these two need extra caution |

    * *Remember:* compare new averages with training averages, in both directions.
  * **7.4 Monitoring: reading the run log**
    * *The idea:* A system that runs every night must report on itself. A run log records each stage of each night: how many records, how long it took (latency), and, where checked cases exist, how accurate the model was. Trends in the log warn you before customers notice a problem.
    * *See it on real data:* the Scoring stage over 5 nights.

      | Night | Latency (ms) | Accuracy | Held as share of incoming |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | 2024-12-01 | `820` | `0.945` | `4.02`% |
      | 2024-12-02 | `840` | `0.942` | `4.04`% |
      | 2024-12-03 | `860` | `0.938` | `4.08`% |
      | 2024-12-04 | `910` | `0.921` | `4.48`% |
      | 2024-12-05 | `980` | `0.904` | `4.85`% |

      Scoring is slower, less accurate, and holds more transactions every night: three signals pointing the same way.
    * *Common mistake:* treating an empty value as a problem. `Model_Accuracy` is empty (`NaN`) for 15 of 20 rows by design, because only the Scoring stage has an accuracy.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `log.groupby("Pipeline_Stage")["Latency_Ms"].mean()` | Scoring `882.0`, Ingestion `365.0`, Validation `193.0`, Action `126.0` | scoring is the slowest stage |
      | `log["Model_Accuracy"].isnull().sum()` | `15` | empty by design outside Scoring |
      | `log[log["Pipeline_Stage"] == "Scoring"]["Model_Accuracy"].min()` | `0.904` | the worst night |

    * *Remember:* log every run, then watch the trends, not just single nights.
  * **7.5 When something breaks: a safe fallback**
    * *The idea:* A nightly system must keep the business safe even when a part fails. A **fallback** is a simple rule used when the model is not available, for example "hold foreign purchases with 2 or more failed logins". It is weaker than the model, but much better than holding nothing or stopping everything.

      ```text
      if os.path.exists(model_path):
          use the model
      else:
          use the fallback rule, and report that it was used
      ```

    * *See it on real data:* the fallback holds 3 of tonight's transactions; the model holds 4. It misses `N-TXN-9005`, a foreign purchase of $3,900 with only 1 failed login.
    * *More examples:*

      | Transaction | Facts | Model | Fallback rule |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | `N-TXN-9001` | foreign, 3 failed logins | Hold | Hold |
      | `N-TXN-9003` | foreign, 5 failed logins | Hold | Hold |
      | `N-TXN-9005` | foreign, 1 failed login, $3,900 | Hold | Pass |
      | `N-TXN-9007` | foreign, 4 failed logins | Hold | Hold |
      | the 4 domestic purchases | 0 or 1 failed logins | Pass | Pass |

    * *Remember:* plan for failure, and always report which method made the decisions.

* **8. Development Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE:**
  * `[1]` File Explorer: create your script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S41_demo_1.py`), and check the three files are in `data/`. Micro-Example 1 creates a new `models/` folder; it appears here.
  * `[2]` Code Editor: write a few lines, run, repeat. Small steps keep errors small.
  * `[3]` AI Agent Chat: on an error, capture the traceback (Python's error report) with `Win + Shift + S` (macOS: `Cmd + Shift + 4`), paste it with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why it failed.
  * `[4]` Terminal: run `python` followed by your script name. Return code `0` means the script finished with no errors. First time only: `pip install scikit-learn joblib`.

* **9. Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * Every example has three parts: **1. Input**, **2. Process**, and **3. Output**. Name each file with your own name and the session code, for example `Reacher_S41_demo_1.py` (pattern: `YourName_S41_demo_1.py`). Run Micro-Example 1 first: the other examples load the model it saves.
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Train Once, Save to a File):* `YourName_S41_demo_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the investigated transactions from Session 3.4
    import os
    import joblib
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
    train = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_04_dataset.csv")
    features = ["Transaction_Amount_USD", "Foreign_IP_Flag",
                "Failed_Login_Attempts_Count"]

    # 2. Process: scale + logistic regression, trained in one Pipeline
    model = Pipeline([
        ("scale", StandardScaler()),
        ("classify", LogisticRegression()),
    ])
    model.fit(train[features], train["Actual_Fraud_Binary"])

    # 3. Output: save the trained model as a file
    os.makedirs("models", exist_ok=True)
    joblib.dump(model, "models/fraud_model.joblib")
    print("Saved: models/fraud_model.joblib")
    print("Training accuracy:", model.score(train[features], train["Actual_Fraud_Binary"]))
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * The training data is the 16 investigated transactions from Session 3.4; the model uses 3 features.
      * `Pipeline([("scale", StandardScaler()), ("classify", LogisticRegression())])` joins two steps into one object: first standardize, then classify. Each step has a name and a tool.
      * `model.fit(...)` fits both steps in order.
      * `os.makedirs("models", exist_ok=True)` creates the `models` folder if needed; `joblib.dump()` writes the trained pipeline into it (section 7.1).
      * `model.score(...)` measures accuracy on the training rows.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Saved: models/fraud_model.joblib
      Training accuracy: 0.9375
      ```

      * The file now exists in the `models` folder, visible in Panel `[1]`.
      * `0.9375` = 15 of 16 correct. It is measured on the rows the model learned from, so it is a ceiling, not an honest test.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`os.path.exists()`):*
    * New tool: `os.path.exists(path)` returns `True` if the file exists. Why: the nightly job should confirm the model is there before it starts.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(os.path.______("models/fraud_model.joblib"))`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `True`.
  * *Micro-Example 2 (The Nightly Job: Load, Score, Decide):* `YourName_S41_demo_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the saved model and tonight's new transactions
    import joblib
    import pandas as pd
    model = joblib.load("models/fraud_model.joblib")
    batch = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_01_new_batch.csv")
    features = ["Transaction_Amount_USD", "Foreign_IP_Flag",
                "Failed_Login_Attempts_Count"]

    # 2. Process: fraud probability, then the business rule (hold at 0.5 or above)
    batch["Fraud_Probability"] = model.predict_proba(batch[features])[:, 1].round(3)
    batch["Decision"] = "Pass"
    batch.loc[batch["Fraud_Probability"] >= 0.5, "Decision"] = "Hold"

    # 3. Output: print and save tonight's decisions
    print(batch[["Transaction_ID", "Fraud_Probability", "Decision"]])
    batch.to_csv("data/nightly_decisions.csv", index=False)
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `joblib.load(...)` brings back the trained pipeline: no training happens here, so this script runs in a fraction of a second.
      * `model.predict_proba(batch[features])[:, 1]` gives each new transaction its fraud probability (section 7.2). The feature columns must have the same names as in training.
      * `batch["Decision"] = "Pass"` sets every row to "Pass"; `batch.loc[batch["Fraud_Probability"] >= 0.5, "Decision"] = "Hold"` changes only the risky rows.
      * `batch.to_csv(...)` saves tonight's decisions for the operations team.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
        Transaction_ID  Fraud_Probability Decision
      0     N-TXN-9001              0.862     Hold
      1     N-TXN-9002              0.070     Pass
      2     N-TXN-9003              0.984     Hold
      3     N-TXN-9004              0.083     Pass
      4     N-TXN-9005              0.789     Hold
      5     N-TXN-9006              0.163     Pass
      6     N-TXN-9007              0.965     Hold
      7     N-TXN-9008              0.073     Pass
      ```

      * 4 of 8 transactions are held, all foreign purchases above $2,800.
      * The probabilities fall into two clear groups: above `0.78` or below `0.17`. No case sits near the 0.5 cut-off, so the decisions are not borderline.
  * *Quick Check 2 (Run date stamp):*
    * New tool: `from datetime import date` and `date.today()` give today's date. Why: every decision file must show when the decisions were made, for audits.
    * Task: add `from datetime import date` to part **1. Input**, and one line at the end of part **2. Process**: `batch["Run_Date"] = str(date._____())`
    * Check: the saved `data/nightly_decisions.csv` has a `Run_Date` column with today's date, written as `YYYY-MM-DD`.
  * *Micro-Example 3 (Drift Check: Does Tonight Look Like Training?):* `YourName_S41_demo_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: training data and tonight's batch
    import pandas as pd
    train = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_04_dataset.csv")
    batch = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_01_new_batch.csv")
    features = ["Transaction_Amount_USD", "Foreign_IP_Flag",
                "Failed_Login_Attempts_Count"]

    # 2. Process: compare the averages, feature by feature
    drift = pd.DataFrame({
        "Train_Avg": train[features].mean().round(2),
        "Batch_Avg": batch[features].mean().round(2),
    })
    drift["Change_Pct"] = (
        (drift["Batch_Avg"] - drift["Train_Avg"]) / drift["Train_Avg"] * 100
    ).round(1)
    drift["Drift_Flag"] = drift["Change_Pct"] > 20

    # 3. Output: the drift report
    print(drift)
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `train[features].mean()` and `batch[features].mean()` give each feature's average in the two files.
      * Putting both into `pd.DataFrame({...})` lines them up by feature name, one row per feature.
      * `Change_Pct` is the change in percent from the training average (section 7.3).
      * `Drift_Flag` marks features that changed by more than 20%.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
                                   Train_Avg  Batch_Avg  Change_Pct  Drift_Flag
      Transaction_Amount_USD         1371.72    2555.00        86.3        True
      Foreign_IP_Flag                   0.50       0.50         0.0       False
      Failed_Login_Attempts_Count       1.56       1.75        12.2       False
      ```

      * Tonight's purchases are on average 86% larger than the training purchases: amount has drifted.
      * The share of foreign purchases is unchanged, and failed logins rose only slightly.
      * The model still runs, but its scores for large purchases deserve a person's review tonight.
  * *Quick Check 3 (`.abs()`):*
    * New tool: `column.abs()` removes the minus sign. Why: a feature that **falls** by 30% has drifted too; `> 20` alone misses it.
    * Task: in part **2. Process**, change the flag line to `drift["Drift_Flag"] = drift["Change_Pct"].___() > 20`
    * Check: the flags do not change for tonight's batch, but a change of `-30.0` would now be flagged as well.

* **10. Official Python Documentation & References:**
  * [Scikit-Learn Model Persistence Guide (Joblib Documentation)](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/model_persistence.html).
  * [Python Library: `os.path` (Checking Files and Paths)](https://docs.python.org/3/library/os.path.html).
  * [MLflow Official Documentation: Open Source Platform for the Machine Learning Lifecycle](https://mlflow.org/docs/latest/index.html).
  * [Sculley et al.: Hidden Technical Debt in Machine Learning Systems (NeurIPS Standards)](https://papers.nips.cc/paper/5656-hidden-technical-debt-in-machine-learning-systems.pdf).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Read the Run Log:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S41_monitoring.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S41_monitoring.py`).
  * *Step 2: Find the Nights That Need Attention:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the run log, 5 nights x 4 stages
    import pandas as pd
    log = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_01_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: health rules for the Scoring stage, then totals per night
    scoring = log[log["Pipeline_Stage"] == "Scoring"].copy()
    scoring["Alert"] = (
        (scoring["Latency_Ms"] > 900) | (scoring["Model_Accuracy"] < 0.93)
    )
    nightly = log.groupby("Run_Date").agg(
        Total_Latency_Ms=("Latency_Ms", "sum"),
        Transactions_In=("Records_Count", "first"),
    )

    # 3. Output: the Scoring stage per night, then the nightly totals
    print(scoring[["Run_Date", "Latency_Ms", "Model_Accuracy", "Alert"]])
    print(nightly)
    ```
    * `.copy()` makes `scoring` an independent table, so adding the `Alert` column does not touch `log`.
    * `|` means "or": a night is flagged if either rule is broken.
    * `"first"` in `.agg()` takes the first value of each group; here, the `Ingestion` row's count.
  * *Step 3: Interpret:* In two comment lines, describe the trend in latency and accuracy across the 5 nights, and what the team should do next.
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:* Run `python YourName_S41_monitoring.py` in Panel `[4]` and confirm return code `0`.
  * *Success Check:* alerts on `2024-12-04` (latency `910`, accuracy `0.921`) and `2024-12-05` (`980`, `0.904`); total latency is `1470` ms on the first two nights, then rises every night to `1730` ms, while incoming transactions grow from `120000` to `138900`. Growing volume and falling accuracy together suggest drift: the model should be reviewed and possibly retrained.

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Survive a Missing Model File:**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In `YourName_S41_practice.py`, make the nightly job keep working when the model file cannot be found.
  * *Task 1 (Read the FileNotFoundError & AI Diagnosis):* Starting from Micro-Example 2, change the path to `"models/fraud_modle.joblib"` (a typo). The script stops with `FileNotFoundError: [Errno 2] No such file or directory: 'models/fraud_modle.joblib'`. Capture the traceback with `Win + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and ask how to check for a file before loading it.
  * *Task 2 (Add a Fallback Rule):* Store the path in a variable `model_path`. If `os.path.exists(model_path)`, load the model and decide as in Micro-Example 2, and set `method = "model"`. Otherwise, use the fallback rule: hold when `Foreign_IP_Flag == 1` **and** `Failed_Login_Attempts_Count >= 2`, and set `method = "fallback rule"`. Print which method was used.
  * *Task 3 (Compare):* Run the script once with the typo and once with the correct path. Print the held transaction IDs each time.
  * *Success Check:* with the typo, the fallback rule holds 3 transactions (`N-TXN-9001`, `N-TXN-9003`, `N-TXN-9007`); with the correct path, the model holds 4 (it also holds `N-TXN-9005`, a foreign purchase of `$3,900` with only 1 failed login). The script finishes with return code `0` both times: the fallback keeps the business safe, but it is weaker than the model, so the missing file must still be fixed quickly.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - One Function for the Whole Night:**
  * *Goal:* Combine everything into one function that the operations team could schedule to run every night: validate, check drift, score (or fall back), act, and report.
  * *What you need:*
    * Files: `data/session_03_04_dataset.csv` (training), `data/session_04_01_new_batch.csv` (tonight), and `models/fraud_model.joblib` (from Micro-Example 1).
    * Script name: `YourName_S41_challenge.py`.
    * Tools you already know: functions (Session 2.4), `assert` (Session 2.3), `os.path.exists()`, `joblib.load()`, `.predict_proba()`, the drift table from Micro-Example 3, and the fallback rule from Exercise 2.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. Write `run_nightly(batch_path, model_path, train_path)` that returns a dictionary summary.
    2. **Validation:** check with `assert` that the batch has the three feature columns and no missing values in them.
    3. **Drift:** compute the change in percent of each feature's average, as in Micro-Example 3, and collect the names of the features whose absolute change is above 20.
    4. **Scoring:** if the model file exists, hold transactions with a fraud probability of 0.5 or above; otherwise, use the fallback rule from Exercise 2.
    5. **Action:** save the batch with `Decision` and `Method` columns to `data/nightly_decisions.csv`.
    6. **Report:** return `{"records": ..., "held": ..., "method": ..., "drift_features": [...], "needs_review": ...}`, where `needs_review` is `True` if any feature drifted **or** the fallback was used.
    7. Call the function twice, once with the correct model path and once with a wrong path, and print both summaries.
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * Checking that columns exist: `set(features).issubset(batch.columns)` is `True` when every name in `features` is a column of `batch`.
    * A list of names from a Boolean column: `list(drift.index[drift["Drift_Flag"]])` keeps the row labels (feature names) where the flag is `True`.
    * Any `True` in a list: `len(drift_features) > 0`.
  * *Hints:*
    * Write and test each stage as a few lines first, then move them into the function.
    * Use `int(...)` around counts so the dictionary prints plain numbers.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Correct path: `{"records": 8, "held": 4, "method": "model", "drift_features": ["Transaction_Amount_USD"], "needs_review": True}`.
    * Wrong path: `{"records": 8, "held": 3, "method": "fallback rule", "drift_features": ["Transaction_Amount_USD"], "needs_review": True}`.
    * Business meaning: even when the model runs, tonight still needs a person's review, because the amounts are far outside the training data. A decision system should report its own doubts, not just its decisions.
