<a id="session-04-02"></a>
### Session 4.2: AI Applications in Business Decision-Making Part 1: Predictive Customer Analytics and Churn

![Session 4.2 Map](assets/session_04_02_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_04_02_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_04_02_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 14 (115/12/13 - 115/12/19) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **1. Session Purpose:**
  * A subscription telecom company loses customers every month to churn (customers cancelling their contract)
  * Its retention team can call customers or assign them an account manager, but every contact costs money, and calling customers who would stay anyway wastes it
  * The head of customer retention asks: which of our current customers should we contact first, and will the campaign pay for itself? Today, you turn churn predictions into revenue at risk and a campaign plan with a measurable return

* **2. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "Who is likely to leave?": train a churn model on past customers and score current customers with a churn probability
  2. "How much money is at stake?": calculate revenue at risk (churn probability x yearly customer value) and rank customers by it
  3. "Will the campaign pay off?": calculate a campaign's expected net benefit and ROI (Return on Investment), choose who to target, and fix a `ValueError` caused by missing feature columns

* **3. Business Context: Pricing a Retention Campaign:**
  * Four ideas, one example (current customer `ACT-101`):

    | Term | Meaning | Example |
    |---|---|---|
    | Churn probability | the model's estimate that this customer leaves within 60 days | `0.876` |
    | Annual value | monthly fee x 12: one year of revenue from this customer | `$2,399.88` |
    | Revenue at risk | churn probability x annual value: the revenue we expect to lose | `$2,102.29` |
    | Expected saved revenue | revenue at risk x success rate of the campaign | `$2,102.29 x 0.40 = $840.92` |

  * CLV (Customer Lifetime Value) is the total value of a customer over the whole relationship, often several years. Today we use one year of value: simpler, and a cautious basis for spending decisions.
  * A campaign is worth it for a customer when expected saved revenue is larger than the contact cost. Contacting a customer who would stay anyway, or whose value is small, wastes budget.
  * Net benefit vs ROI: net benefit = saved revenue - cost, in dollars; ROI = net benefit / cost x 100, in percent. The plan with the highest ROI is not always the plan that earns the most money.

* **4. Data Basics: Learn from the Past, Act on the Present:**
  * Two groups of customers, two files:

    | File | Customers | Has the answer (`Churned`)? | Used to |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | History | 14 past customers | yes | train the model |
    | Active | 10 current customers | no, not yet known | score and target |

  * A retention campaign only makes sense for customers who have not left yet. So the model learns from the history and predicts for the active customers.
  * What drives churn in our history: after scaling, the model's coefficients are `-0.718` for tenure (longer customers stay), `+0.897` for support calls, and `+0.878` for usage drop (more calls and falling usage signal leaving).

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * Two files (fictional teaching data):
    * History: [data/session_04_02_dataset.csv](data/session_04_02_dataset.csv), 14 past customers with their outcome; 5 of them churned.
    * Active: [data/session_04_02_active.csv](data/session_04_02_active.csv), 10 current customers with the same columns except the outcome.
  * Columns:
    * `Subscriber_ID`: Customer code (`SUB-...` in the history, `ACT-...` for active customers).
    * `Tenure_Months_Count`: Months as a customer.
    * `Monthly_Subscription_Fee_USD`: Monthly fee in U.S. Dollars.
    * `Customer_Support_Calls_Count`: Calls to customer support in the last 90 days.
    * `Usage_Drop_Last_30_Days_Pct`: How much the customer's use of the service fell in the last 30 days, in percent.
    * `Churned_Within_60_Days_Binary` (history only): `1` = left within 60 days, `0` = stayed.

* **6. Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `Churn`:
    * What: customers ending their relationship with the company
    * Why: in subscription businesses, keeping a customer is usually cheaper than winning a new one
  * `Churn Probability`:
    * What: the model's estimate, from 0 to 1, that a customer will leave
    * Why: ranks customers by risk instead of treating everyone the same
  * `Revenue at Risk`:
    * What: churn probability x annual value
    * Why: turns a probability into dollars a manager can budget against
  * `Customer Lifetime Value (CLV)`:
    * What: the total value of a customer over the whole relationship
    * Why: decides how much a customer is worth saving
  * `Net Benefit` and `ROI`:
    * What: saved revenue minus cost (dollars), and net benefit divided by cost (percent)
    * Why: the two numbers a finance team asks for before approving a campaign
  * `Targeting Threshold`:
    * What: the minimum churn probability for a customer to be contacted
    * Why: sets the size and cost of the campaign

* **7. Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * **7.1 Learn from the past, predict the present**
    * *The idea:* A churn model needs examples with known outcomes, so it learns from past customers whose story has ended: they either left or stayed. It then scores today's customers, whose outcome is still unknown. Two tables, one model: `fit` on the history, `predict_proba` on the active customers.
    * *See it on real data:* how the 14 past customers differ.

      | Average of past customers | Stayed (9) | Left (5) |
      |---|---|---|
      | Months as a customer | `36.9` | `6.8` |
      | Support calls (last 90 days) | `1.1` | `4.6` |
      | Usage drop (last 30 days) | `9.4`% | `46.0`% |

      Customers who left were newer, called support more, and had stopped using the service. The model turns these patterns into a probability for each current customer.
    * *Common mistake:* scoring the history instead of the active customers. The history has already happened; only current customers can still be saved.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `history["Churned_Within_60_Days_Binary"].mean()` | `0.357` | 5 of 14 past customers left |
      | `model.score(history[features], history["Churned_Within_60_Days_Binary"])` | `0.929` | 13 of 14 correct on the training rows |
      | `active.shape` | `(10, 5)` | 10 current customers, no answer column |
      | `model.named_steps["classify"].coef_` | tenure `-0.718`, calls `0.897`, usage drop `0.878` | the signals that push toward leaving |

    * *Remember:* fit on the past, score the present.
  * **7.2 Expected value: turning a probability into dollars**
    * *The idea:* A probability alone does not tell a manager how much is at stake. Multiply it by what the customer is worth, and you get the **expected** loss: what you would lose on average across many customers like this one. For one customer, the loss is either all or nothing; across a whole portfolio, the expected values add up reliably.

      | Customer | Churn probability | Annual value | Revenue at risk |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | `ACT-101` | `0.876` | `$2,399.88` | `$2,102.29` |
      | `ACT-105` | `0.941` | `$1,199.88` | `$1,129.09` |
      | `ACT-110` | `0.448` | `$2,399.88` | `$1,075.15` |
      | `ACT-102` | `0.026` | `$1,799.88` | `$46.80` |

    * *What the table shows:* `ACT-105` is the most likely to leave, but `ACT-101` has more money at risk because it pays twice as much. `ACT-110` has less than a 50% chance of leaving, yet almost as much at risk as `ACT-105`.
    * *Common mistake:* ranking by probability alone. It sends the retention team to the most likely leavers, not to the biggest losses.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `active["Revenue_At_Risk_USD"].sum()` | `$7,659.53` | total expected loss |
      | top 3 customers' share of it | `63.3`% | most of the risk sits with a few customers |
      | `active["Annual_Value_USD"].sum()` | `$16,918.80` | all revenue from the 10 customers |
      | revenue at risk / all revenue | `45.3`% | almost half of this group's revenue is in danger |
      | `active["Churn_Probability"].mean()` | `0.461` | the average customer here is close to a coin flip |

    * *Remember:* revenue at risk = probability x value; rank by dollars.
  * **7.3 The economics of a campaign: net benefit and ROI**
    * *The idea:* Contacting a customer costs money, and it does not always work. For each customer, the expected saved revenue is churn probability x success rate x annual value. The campaign pays off for that customer only if this is larger than the contact cost.
    * *See it on real data:* a call costs `$75` and keeps 40% of customers who would leave.

      | Customer | Expected saved revenue | Minus cost | Net |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | `ACT-101` | `0.876 x 0.40 x 2399.88 = 840.92` | `- 75` | `+765.92` |
      | `ACT-110` | `430.06` | `- 75` | `+355.06` |
      | `ACT-104` | `154.07` | `- 75` | `+79.07` |

    * *Two summary numbers:* net benefit (saved revenue - cost, in dollars) and ROI (net benefit / cost x 100, in percent). A small, targeted campaign often has the highest ROI; a broader one can earn more dollars. Exercise 2 shows both on our data.
    * *More examples:*

      | Customer | Call net (cost $75, 40% success) | VIP net (cost $250, 60% success) | Verdict |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | `ACT-101` | `+765.92` | `+1011.38` | VIP pays more |
      | `ACT-104` | `+79.07` | `-18.90` | only the call pays |
      | `ACT-107` | `+132.34` | `+61.01` | the call pays more |
      | `ACT-102` | `-56.28` | `-221.92` | no offer pays: contact costs more than it saves |

    * *Remember:* contact a customer only when expected savings exceed the contact cost.
  * **7.4 Matching the offer to the customer**
    * *The idea:* Not every customer deserves the same offer. A cheap offer (a call) suits customers with modest value or moderate risk; an expensive offer (a personal account manager) pays only for customers who are both valuable and very likely to leave. Rules applied in order, from cheapest to most valuable, express this.
    * *See it on real data:* Micro-Example 3's rules.

      | Rule (applied in this order) | Customers |
      |---|---|
      | everyone starts with "Newsletter" | all 10 |
      | probability `>= 0.4`: "Retention_Call" | overwrites 6 |
      | probability `>= 0.7` **and** value `>= 1500`: "VIP_Account_Manager" | overwrites 2: `ACT-101`, `ACT-108` |

      `ACT-105` has the highest risk (`0.941`) but stays with a call: its annual value, `$1,199.88`, is below the VIP limit.
    * *Common mistake:* applying the rules in the wrong order. If the VIP rule ran first, the call rule would then overwrite it, and nobody would get a VIP manager.
    * *More examples:*

      | Rule change | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | VIP limit lowered to `$1,000` | VIP: `ACT-101`, `ACT-105`, `ACT-108` | `ACT-105` now qualifies |
      | VIP probability raised to `0.8` | VIP: `ACT-101`, `ACT-108` | no change for this group |
      | call threshold lowered to `0.3` | `6` customers at or above it | the same 6 as at `0.4`: nobody sits between 0.3 and 0.4 |

    * *Remember:* set a default, then overwrite from the cheapest offer to the most valuable.

* **8. Development Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE:**
  * `[1]` File Explorer: create your script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S42_demo_1.py`), and check both files are in `data/`.
  * `[2]` Code Editor: write a few lines, run, repeat. Small steps keep errors small.
  * `[3]` AI Agent Chat: on an error, capture the traceback (Python's error report) with `Win + Shift + S` (macOS: `Cmd + Shift + 4`), paste it with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why it failed.
  * `[4]` Terminal: run `python` followed by your script name. Return code `0` means the script finished with no errors.

* **9. Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * Every example has three parts: **1. Input**, **2. Process**, and **3. Output**. Name each file with your own name and the session code, for example `Reacher_S42_demo_1.py` (pattern: `YourName_S42_demo_1.py`).
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Learn from the Past, Score the Present):* `YourName_S42_demo_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: past customers (with outcomes) and current customers
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
    history = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_02_dataset.csv")
    active = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_02_active.csv")
    features = ["Tenure_Months_Count", "Customer_Support_Calls_Count",
                "Usage_Drop_Last_30_Days_Pct"]

    # 2. Process: train on history, then score the active customers
    model = Pipeline([
        ("scale", StandardScaler()),
        ("classify", LogisticRegression()),
    ])
    model.fit(history[features], history["Churned_Within_60_Days_Binary"])
    active["Churn_Probability"] = model.predict_proba(active[features])[:, 1].round(3)

    # 3. Output: the active customers, most at risk first
    print(active.sort_values("Churn_Probability", ascending=False)[
        ["Subscriber_ID", "Tenure_Months_Count", "Churn_Probability"]
    ])
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * Two files are loaded: `history` (past customers, with the answer) and `active` (current customers, without it).
      * The `Pipeline` scales the 3 features and fits a logistic regression on the history (section 7.1).
      * `model.predict_proba(active[features])[:, 1]` scores the current customers: the second column is the probability of leaving.
      * `.sort_values(..., ascending=False)` lists the riskiest customers first.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
        Subscriber_ID  Tenure_Months_Count  Churn_Probability
      4       ACT-105                    7              0.941
      7       ACT-108                    3              0.900
      0       ACT-101                    5              0.876
      2       ACT-103                   10              0.675
      9       ACT-110                   18              0.448
      ...
      1       ACT-102                   40              0.026
      ```

      * The top 3 have all been customers for less than 8 months, like the past customers who left.
      * The longest-standing customers (`ACT-102`, 40 months; `ACT-106`, 50 months) are almost certain to stay: under 3%.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`named_steps` and `.coef_`):*
    * New tool: `model.named_steps["classify"].coef_` returns the learned coefficients of the logistic regression inside the pipeline. Why: shows which signals push a customer toward leaving.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(dict(zip(features, model.named_steps["classify"].____[0].round(3).tolist())))`
    * Check: tenure `-0.718`, support calls `0.897`, usage drop `0.878`. A negative coefficient lowers the churn probability; a positive one raises it.
  * *Micro-Example 2 (Revenue at Risk):* `YourName_S42_demo_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: train and score, as in Micro-Example 1
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
    history = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_02_dataset.csv")
    active = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_02_active.csv")
    features = ["Tenure_Months_Count", "Customer_Support_Calls_Count",
                "Usage_Drop_Last_30_Days_Pct"]
    model = Pipeline([("scale", StandardScaler()),
                      ("classify", LogisticRegression())])
    model.fit(history[features], history["Churned_Within_60_Days_Binary"])
    active["Churn_Probability"] = model.predict_proba(active[features])[:, 1].round(3)

    # 2. Process: one year of value, and the part of it at risk
    active["Annual_Value_USD"] = (active["Monthly_Subscription_Fee_USD"] * 12).round(2)
    active["Revenue_At_Risk_USD"] = (
        active["Churn_Probability"] * active["Annual_Value_USD"]
    ).round(2)
    ranked = active.sort_values("Revenue_At_Risk_USD", ascending=False)

    # 3. Output: the ranking and the total
    print(ranked[["Subscriber_ID", "Churn_Probability", "Annual_Value_USD",
                  "Revenue_At_Risk_USD"]])
    print(f"Total revenue at risk: ${active['Revenue_At_Risk_USD'].sum():,.2f}")
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * Part 1 repeats the training and scoring of Micro-Example 1.
      * `Monthly_Subscription_Fee_USD * 12` is one year of revenue from each customer.
      * Multiplying by the churn probability gives the revenue at risk (section 7.2).
      * The table is sorted by revenue at risk, and the total is printed with thousands separators.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
        Subscriber_ID  Churn_Probability  Annual_Value_USD  Revenue_At_Risk_USD
      0       ACT-101              0.876           2399.88              2102.29
      7       ACT-108              0.900           1799.88              1619.89
      4       ACT-105              0.941           1199.88              1129.09
      9       ACT-110              0.448           2399.88              1075.15
      ...
      Total revenue at risk: $7,659.53
      ```

      * The order differs from Micro-Example 1: `ACT-101` moves to the top because it pays the most, and `ACT-110` jumps to fourth despite its lower probability.
      * Across all 10 customers, about `$7,660` of yearly revenue is expected to be lost if nothing is done.
  * *Quick Check 2 (`.cumsum()`):*
    * New tool: `column.cumsum()` gives a running total down the rows. Why: shows how much of the total risk the first few customers carry.
    * Task: add one line at the end of part **2. Process**: `ranked["Running_Total_USD"] = ranked["Revenue_At_Risk_USD"].______().round(2)`, and add `"Running_Total_USD"` to the printed columns.
    * Check: the third row shows `4851.27`: the top 3 customers carry 63% of the total risk.
  * *Micro-Example 3 (Assign a Campaign to Each Customer):* `YourName_S42_demo_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: train and score, as in Micro-Example 2
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
    history = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_02_dataset.csv")
    active = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_02_active.csv")
    features = ["Tenure_Months_Count", "Customer_Support_Calls_Count",
                "Usage_Drop_Last_30_Days_Pct"]
    model = Pipeline([("scale", StandardScaler()),
                      ("classify", LogisticRegression())])
    model.fit(history[features], history["Churned_Within_60_Days_Binary"])
    active["Churn_Probability"] = model.predict_proba(active[features])[:, 1].round(3)
    active["Annual_Value_USD"] = (active["Monthly_Subscription_Fee_USD"] * 12).round(2)

    # 2. Process: rules from the cheapest to the most valuable campaign
    active["Campaign"] = "Newsletter"
    active.loc[active["Churn_Probability"] >= 0.4, "Campaign"] = "Retention_Call"
    active.loc[
        (active["Churn_Probability"] >= 0.7) & (active["Annual_Value_USD"] >= 1500),
        "Campaign",
    ] = "VIP_Account_Manager"

    # 3. Output: how many customers get each campaign
    print(active["Campaign"].value_counts())
    print(active[["Subscriber_ID", "Churn_Probability", "Campaign"]])
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * Part 1 scores the customers and computes their annual value, as before.
      * `active["Campaign"] = "Newsletter"` gives everyone the default.
      * The first `.loc[]` line overwrites the default with "Retention_Call" for customers at `0.4` or above.
      * The second `.loc[]` line overwrites again with "VIP_Account_Manager" for customers at `0.7` or above **and** worth `$1,500` or more a year (section 7.4).
      * `value_counts()` counts the customers per campaign.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Campaign
      Newsletter             4
      Retention_Call         4
      VIP_Account_Manager    2
      ...
      4       ACT-105              0.941       Retention_Call
      ...
      7       ACT-108              0.900  VIP_Account_Manager
      ```

      * 2 customers get a personal account manager, 4 get a call, and 4 only get the newsletter.
      * `ACT-105` gets a call, not a VIP manager, although it is the most likely to leave: it is not valuable enough for the expensive offer.
  * *Quick Check 3 (`pd.cut()`):*
    * New tool: `pd.cut(column, bins=[0, 0.4, 0.7, 1.0], labels=["Low", "Medium", "High"])` puts each value into a band. Why: managers think in risk bands, not in decimals.
    * Task: add two lines to part **3. Output**: `active["Risk_Band"] = pd.___(active["Churn_Probability"], bins=[0, 0.4, 0.7, 1.0], labels=["Low", "Medium", "High"])` and `print(active["Risk_Band"].value_counts())`
    * Check: `Low` `4`, `Medium` `3`, `High` `3`.

  * [Pandas API Reference: `pandas.cut`](https://pandas.pydata.org/docs/reference/api/pandas.cut.html).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Campaign Business Case:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S42_churn.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S42_churn.py`).
  * *Step 2: Price a Campaign for Customers at 0.5 or Above:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: train on history, score the active customers
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
    history = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_02_dataset.csv")
    active = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_02_active.csv")
    features = ["Tenure_Months_Count", "Customer_Support_Calls_Count",
                "Usage_Drop_Last_30_Days_Pct"]
    model = Pipeline([("scale", StandardScaler()),
                      ("classify", LogisticRegression())])
    model.fit(history[features], history["Churned_Within_60_Days_Binary"])
    active["Churn_Probability"] = model.predict_proba(active[features])[:, 1].round(3)
    active["Annual_Value_USD"] = (active["Monthly_Subscription_Fee_USD"] * 12).round(2)

    # 2. Process: target, then expected saved revenue, cost, net benefit, ROI
    success_rate = 0.40
    cost_per_contact = 75.0
    targeted = active[active["Churn_Probability"] >= 0.5]
    saved = (targeted["Churn_Probability"] * success_rate
             * targeted["Annual_Value_USD"]).sum()
    cost = len(targeted) * cost_per_contact
    net_benefit = saved - cost
    roi_pct = net_benefit / cost * 100

    # 3. Output: the business case
    print(f"Customers targeted: {len(targeted)}")
    print(f"Expected saved revenue: ${saved:,.2f}")
    print(f"Campaign cost: ${cost:,.2f}")
    print(f"Net benefit: ${net_benefit:,.2f} | ROI: {roi_pct:.1f}%")
    ```
    * Expected saved revenue multiplies three things per customer: the chance they would leave, the chance the call keeps them, and what they are worth.
  * *Step 3: Interpret:* In two comment lines, explain why we multiply by the churn probability instead of counting each targeted customer's full value.
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:* Run `python YourName_S42_churn.py` in Panel `[4]` and confirm return code `0`.
  * *Success Check:* `4` customers targeted; expected saved revenue `$2,199.68`; cost `$300.00`; net benefit `$1,899.68`; ROI `633.2`%.

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Highest ROI or Most Money?:**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In `YourName_S42_practice.py`, test targeting thresholds from 0.3 to 0.8 and compare the best ROI with the best net benefit.
  * *Task 1 (Threshold Loop):* For each threshold in `[0.3, 0.4, 0.5, 0.6, 0.7, 0.8]`, repeat the business case from Exercise 1 and collect `Threshold`, `Targeted`, `Saved_USD`, `Cost_USD`, `Net_Benefit_USD`, and `ROI_Pct` in one DataFrame.
  * *Task 2 (Read the ValueError & AI Diagnosis):* Score the active customers with only two features by mistake: `model.predict_proba(active[["Tenure_Months_Count", "Customer_Support_Calls_Count"]])`. The script stops with `ValueError: The feature names should match those that were passed during fit.` Capture the traceback with `Win + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why the model needs the same columns it was trained on.
  * *Task 3 (Two Recommendations):* Fix the scoring, then print two lines: the threshold with the highest `ROI_Pct`, and the threshold with the highest `Net_Benefit_USD`. If thresholds tie, report the higher threshold, since it contacts fewer customers.
  * *Success Check:* highest ROI: threshold `0.8` (`762.4`%, 3 customers, net `$1,715.51`); highest net benefit: threshold `0.4` (`$2,387.07`, 6 customers, ROI `530.5`%). The broader campaign earns `$671.56` more, at a lower ROI. If the budget allows, net benefit is what adds to profit.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - The Best Offer for Each Customer:**
  * *Goal:* Stop using one threshold for everyone. For each current customer, choose the offer with the highest expected net value, or no offer at all, and show the retention head how much more this earns than the best threshold plan from Exercise 2.
  * *What you need:*
    * Files: `data/session_04_02_dataset.csv` (history) and `data/session_04_02_active.csv` (active customers).
    * Script name: `YourName_S42_challenge.py`.
    * Tools you already know: the trained pipeline and churn probabilities (Micro-Example 1), annual value (Micro-Example 2), `.loc[]` with conditions, `.value_counts()`, and `.sum()`.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. Score the 10 active customers and add `Annual_Value_USD`, as in Micro-Example 2.
    2. Two possible offers:
       * **Call:** costs `$75`, keeps 40% of the customers who would otherwise leave
       * **VIP** (an account manager): costs `$250`, keeps 60%
    3. For each customer, compute `Call_Net` = churn probability x 0.40 x annual value - 75, and `VIP_Net` = churn probability x 0.60 x annual value - 250, both rounded to 2 decimals.
    4. Set `Best_Offer`:
       * `"VIP"` if `VIP_Net` is above 0 and above `Call_Net`
       * otherwise `"Call"` if `Call_Net` is above 0
       * otherwise `"None"`
    5. Add `Expected_Net_USD`: the net value of the chosen offer, or `0` for `"None"`.
    6. Print each customer's ID, churn probability, annual value, both net values, best offer, and expected net. Then print the count per offer, the total expected net, and the difference from Exercise 2's best net benefit (`$2,387.07`).
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * Ordered rules with `.loc[]`: set the default first (`active["Best_Offer"] = "None"`), then overwrite with `"Call"`, then with `"VIP"`. The conditions in step 4 must be written so that the last rule wins only when it should.
    * Copying values row by row from another column: `active.loc[mask, "Expected_Net_USD"] = active.loc[mask, "Call_Net"]`.
  * *Hints:*
    * Start `Expected_Net_USD` at `0.0` for every customer.
    * Check one customer by hand first, such as `ACT-101`: `0.876 x 0.60 x 2399.88 - 250 = 1011.38`.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Offers: VIP for `ACT-101`, `ACT-105`, `ACT-108`, and `ACT-110`; Call for `ACT-103`, `ACT-104`, and `ACT-107`; None for `ACT-102`, `ACT-106`, and `ACT-109`.
    * Total expected net value: `$2,951.44`, which is `$564.37` more than the best threshold plan.
    * Business meaning: matching the offer to the customer beats any single threshold. `ACT-104` has only a `0.214` churn probability but a high annual value, so even a call is worth it, while the expensive VIP offer pays only for valuable, high-risk customers such as `ACT-110`.

<a id="session-04-03"></a>
### Session 4.3: AI Applications in Business Decision-Making Part 2: Pricing Optimization and Revenue Management

![Session 4.3 Map](assets/session_04_03_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_04_03_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_04_03_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 15 (115/12/20 - 115/12/26) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **1. Session Purpose:**
  * An online retailer sells 12 seasonal products; the season ends in 12 days, and stock left after that must be sold off cheaply
  * Prices were set by gut feeling: some may be too low (leaving profit on the table), others too high (leaving stock unsold)
  * The pricing manager asks: which prices should we raise, which should we cut, and how do we clear the stock before the season ends? Today, you measure how customers react to price and simulate profit before changing a single price

* **2. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "How strongly do customers react to price?": estimate the price elasticity of demand (the % change in units sold for a 1% change in price) from a price test
  2. "Would a price change raise profit?": simulate units, revenue, and gross profit for several price changes before making them
  3. "How do we clear the stock in time?": calculate the price cut each product needs to sell out before the season ends, and fix a `SyntaxError` (an unclosed bracket)

* **3. Business Context: Price, Volume, and Profit:**
  * A lower price sells more units but earns less on each one; a higher price earns more per unit but sells fewer. Profit depends on both:

    | Term | Meaning | Example (`PRD-200`) |
    |---|---|---|
    | Unit margin | price - unit cost | `$49.99 - $22.50 = $27.49` |
    | Gross margin % | unit margin / price x 100 | `55.0`% |
    | Daily gross profit | unit margin x daily units | `$27.49 x 32 = $879.68` |
    | Days of stock | inventory / daily units | `450 / 32 = 14.1` days |

  * Price elasticity of demand (PED): if elasticity is `-1.5`, a 1% price increase loses about 1.5% of units. When elasticity is between `0` and `-1`, customers barely react; below `-1`, they react strongly.
  * The goal is profit, not revenue: a cut can raise revenue and still lower profit, because each extra unit brings in less margin.
  * Seasonal stock: units left at the season's end lose most of their value. Our retailer sells leftovers to a liquidator (a company that buys surplus stock) for 40% of their unit cost, a loss of 60% of the cost on every unsold unit.

* **4. Data Basics: Measuring Elasticity with a Price Test:**
  * To measure how customers react, the retailer changed the price of `PRD-202` once a week for 5 weeks and recorded daily units sold:

    | Week | Price | Daily units |
    |---|---|---|
    | 1 | `$59.99` | `27.9` |
    | 3 | `$69.99` | `22.0` |
    | 5 | `$79.99` | `18.1` |

  * Elasticity is the slope of a straight line through `log(units)` against `log(price)`. A logarithm (log) turns percentage changes into differences, so the slope reads directly as "% change in units for a 1% change in price".
  * `np.polyfit(x, y, 1)` fits that straight line and returns its slope and intercept. Result: elasticity `-1.51`.
  * Using elasticity for a small price change: new units = old units x (1 + elasticity x price change). For `+5%`: `1 + (-1.51 x 0.05) = 0.9245`, so about 7.6% fewer units. This straight-line rule is an approximation that works for changes of up to about 20%.

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * Two files (fictional teaching data):
    * Catalog: [data/session_04_03_dataset.csv](data/session_04_03_dataset.csv), 12 products, one row per product.
    * Price test: [data/session_04_03_price_test.csv](data/session_04_03_price_test.csv), 5 weeks of tested prices for `PRD-202`.
  * Catalog columns:
    * `Product_ID`: Product code (e.g., `PRD-201`).
    * `Current_Price_USD`: Our current price in U.S. Dollars.
    * `Competitor_Price_USD`: The main competitor's price for the same product.
    * `Inventory_Units_Count`: Units in stock.
    * `Historical_Daily_Sales_Units`: Average units sold per day at the current price.
    * `Unit_Cost_USD`: What one unit costs us, delivered to the warehouse.
  * Price test columns: `Test_Week` (1 to 5), `Product_ID`, `Test_Price_USD`, and `Avg_Daily_Units` (average units sold per day that week).

* **6. Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `Price Elasticity of Demand (PED)`:
    * What: the % change in units sold for a 1% change in price
    * Why: tells you whether a price rise loses a few customers or many
  * `Unit Margin` and `Gross Profit`:
    * What: price minus unit cost, and unit margin times units sold
    * Why: the measure a pricing decision should maximize
  * `Price Simulation`:
    * What: computing units and profit for several possible prices before choosing one
    * Why: test a decision on paper, not on real customers
  * `Days of Stock`:
    * What: inventory divided by daily sales
    * Why: shows which products will not sell out in time
  * `Markdown`:
    * What: a planned price cut to sell stock faster
    * Why: a smaller, earlier cut usually loses less than a fire sale at the end
  * `Salvage Value`:
    * What: what leftover stock is still worth after the season
    * Why: decides how costly it is to be left with unsold units

* **7. Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * **7.1 Price elasticity: how strongly customers react**
    * *The idea:* Price elasticity of demand (PED) is the percentage change in units sold for a 1% change in price. It is usually negative: a higher price sells fewer units. With an elasticity of `-1.51`, a 1% price rise loses about 1.51% of units. Values between `0` and `-1` mean customers barely react; values below `-1` mean they react strongly.
    * *See it on real data:* the price test for `PRD-202`, week by week.

      | Weeks | Price change | Units change | Elasticity of that step |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | 1 to 2 | `+8.3`% | `-12.5`% | `-1.51` |
      | 2 to 3 | `+7.7`% | `-9.8`% | `-1.28` |
      | 3 to 4 | `+7.1`% | `-10.9`% | `-1.53` |
      | 4 to 5 | `+6.7`% | `-7.7`% | `-1.15` |

      Each single step is noisy. Fitting one line through all 5 weeks gives one steadier estimate: `-1.51`.
    * *Common mistake:* trusting one week-to-week comparison. Sales also move for reasons other than price (weather, holidays, a competitor's promotion); use several test weeks.
    * *More examples:*

      | Elasticity | Units after a 10% price rise | Revenue effect (1.10 x units factor) |
      |---|---|---|
      | `-0.5` | `-5`% | `+4.5`%: revenue rises |
      | `-1.0` | `-10`% | about `-1`%: revenue almost flat |
      | `-1.51` (ours) | `-15.1`% | `-6.6`%: revenue falls |
      | `-2.0` | `-20`% | `-12`%: revenue falls sharply |

    * *Remember:* elasticity = % change in units / % change in price; estimate it from several observations.
  * **7.2 Why logs: percentages become straight lines**
    * *The idea:* Elasticity is about percentage changes, but a straight line measures fixed changes. The natural logarithm (`np.log`) turns one into the other: a 10% rise in price always adds the same amount, about `0.095`, to the log of the price, whatever the starting price. So on a log-log chart, constant elasticity becomes a straight line, and its slope is the elasticity.
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Price | `np.log(price)` |
      |---|---|
      | `59.99` | `4.094` |
      | `69.99` | `4.248` |
      | `79.99` | `4.382` |

    * *In code:* `slope, intercept = np.polyfit(np.log(prices), np.log(units), 1)` fits the line; the `1` means a straight line. The slope is the elasticity.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `np.log(1)` | `0.0` | the log of 1 is 0 |
      | `np.log(70) - np.log(60)` | `0.154` | close to the % change, `16.7`%: logs measure relative change |
      | `np.log(1.10)` | `0.0953` | a 10% rise, whatever the starting price |
      | `np.exp(0.0953)` | `1.1` | `np.exp()` undoes `np.log()` |

    * *Remember:* log price against log units: the slope is the elasticity.
  * **7.3 Simulating a price change**
    * *The idea:* Before changing a price, compute what would happen. For a small change, new units = old units x (1 + elasticity x price change). Then profit = (new price - unit cost) x new units. Writing this once as a function, `simulate(price_change)`, guarantees every scenario uses the same formula.
    * *See it on real data:* `PRD-200` (price `$49.99`, cost `$22.50`, 32 units a day).

      | Price change | New price | Daily units | Daily revenue | Daily gross profit |
      |---|---|---|---|---|
      | `-10`% | `$44.99` | `36.83` | `$1,657.11` | `$828.39` |
      | `0`% | `$49.99` | `32.00` | `$1,599.68` | `$879.68` |
      | `+5`% | `$52.49` | `29.58` | `$1,552.85` | `$887.21` |

      The cut brings the most revenue but the least profit; the rise loses units but earns the most.
    * *Common mistake:* maximizing revenue. Every extra unit sold at a lower price adds less margin, so revenue can rise while profit falls.
    * *More examples:* the straight-line rule vs the exact constant-elasticity formula, `(1 + change) ** elasticity`.

      | Price change | Straight-line units factor | Exact factor |
      |---|---|---|
      | `+5`% | `0.9245` | `0.9290` |
      | `+10`% | `0.8490` | `0.8660` |
      | `-10`% | `1.1510` | `1.1724` |
      | `+20`% | `0.6980` | `0.7593` |

      The two agree closely for small changes and drift apart for large ones: keep simulated changes within about 20%.
    * *Remember:* simulate profit, not revenue, and keep changes small.
  * **7.4 Stock and time: days of stock and markdowns**
    * *The idea:* Seasonal stock has a deadline. Days of stock = inventory / daily units shows how long the stock lasts at today's price. If that is longer than the days left in the season, the product needs a markdown (a planned price cut) to sell faster.
    * *See it on real data:* `PRD-202`, 12 days left in the season.

      | Step | Calculation | Result |
      |---|---|---|
      | Days of stock now | `290 / 22` | `13.2` days: too slow |
      | Units needed per day | `290 / 12` | `24.17` |
      | Units must rise by | `24.17 / 22 - 1` | `9.9`% |
      | Price change needed | `9.9% / -1.51` | `-6.5`% |

      The last step reverses the elasticity rule: if units must rise by a given percentage, the price must change by that percentage divided by the elasticity.
    * *More examples:*

      | Product | Days of stock | Units needed per day | Price change to sell out in 12 days |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | `PRD-200` | `14.1` | `37.50` | `-11.4`% |
      | `PRD-210` | `12.5` | `4.17` | `-2.8`%: almost on time |
      | `PRD-204` | `12.0` | `15.00` | `0`%: sells out exactly on time |
      | `PRD-203` | `11.1` | `17.50` | `+5.2`%: sells out early, so the price could rise |

    * *Remember:* compare days of stock with days left; cut just enough to sell out.
  * **7.5 Leftover stock has a cost: salvage value**
    * *The idea:* Units left after the season are sold to a liquidator for 40% of their cost, a loss of 60% of the cost on each one. So the true season profit is profit on units sold minus the loss on units left.
    * *See it on real data:* `PRD-200` over the 12-day season (450 units in stock).

      | Price | Units sold | Units left | Season profit |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | `$49.99` (no change) | `384` | `66` | `$9,665.16` |
      | `$44.99` (`-10`%) | `442` | `8` | `$9,832.00` |

      The cut earns less per unit but avoids most of the leftover loss: `$167` more profit for the season.
    * *More examples:*

      | Product and price | Sold | Left over | Season profit |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | `PRD-202` at `$69.99` | `264` | `26` | `$9,530.16` |
      | `PRD-202` at `$66.49` (`-5`%) | `284` | `6` | `$9,676.31`: the cut pays |
      | `PRD-210` at `$149.99` | `48` | `2` | `$3,706.32` |
      | `PRD-210` at `$142.49` (`-5`%) | `50` | `0` | `$3,574.50`: selling out is not worth the cut |
      | `PRD-204` at `$94.49` (`+5`%) | `166` | `14` | `$8,566.96`: a rise leaves stock behind |

    * *Remember:* count the cost of leftovers when you choose a price.

* **8. Development Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE:**
  * `[1]` File Explorer: create your script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S43_demo_1.py`), and check both files are in `data/`.
  * `[2]` Code Editor: write a few lines, run, repeat. Small steps keep errors small. The editor highlights matching brackets when you place the cursor next to one.
  * `[3]` AI Agent Chat: on an error, capture the traceback (Python's error report) with `Win + Shift + S` (macOS: `Cmd + Shift + 4`), paste it with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why it failed.
  * `[4]` Terminal: run `python` followed by your script name. Return code `0` means the script finished with no errors.

* **9. Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * Every example has three parts: **1. Input**, **2. Process**, and **3. Output**. Name each file with your own name and the session code, for example `Reacher_S43_demo_1.py` (pattern: `YourName_S43_demo_1.py`).
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Estimate Elasticity from the Price Test):* `YourName_S43_demo_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: 5 weeks of tested prices for PRD-202
    import numpy as np
    import pandas as pd
    test = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_03_price_test.csv")

    # 2. Process: a straight line through log(units) against log(price)
    slope, intercept = np.polyfit(np.log(test["Test_Price_USD"]),
                                  np.log(test["Avg_Daily_Units"]), 1)
    elasticity = round(slope, 2)

    # 3. Output: the elasticity and what it means
    print("Price elasticity of demand:", elasticity)
    print(f"A 1% price rise loses about {abs(elasticity)}% of units sold.")
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `import numpy as np` loads NumPy, the number library that pandas is built on.
      * `np.log(...)` takes the logarithm of every price and every unit count (section 7.2).
      * `np.polyfit(x, y, 1)` fits the best straight line through the 5 points and returns its slope and intercept. The slope is the elasticity.
      * `round(slope, 2)` keeps 2 decimals; `abs()` drops the minus sign for the sentence.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Price elasticity of demand: -1.51
      A 1% price rise loses about 1.51% of units sold.
      ```

      * `-1.51` is below `-1`: customers of this product react strongly to price.
      * A 10% rise would lose about 15% of units; whether that raises profit depends on the margin, which Micro-Example 2 checks.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`.pct_change()`):*
    * New tool: `column.pct_change()` gives the change from the previous row, as a share (`0.08` = 8%). Why: shows the week-to-week reactions behind the single slope.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print((test[["Test_Price_USD", "Avg_Daily_Units"]].__________() * 100).round(1))`
    * Check: the second row shows `8.3` (price up 8.3%) and `-12.5` (units down 12.5%). The first row is `NaN`, because week 1 has no previous week.
  * *Micro-Example 2 (Simulate Profit for Several Price Changes):* `YourName_S43_demo_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the catalog and the elasticity from Micro-Example 1
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_03_dataset.csv")
    elasticity = -1.51

    # 2. Process: one function, used for every price change
    def simulate(price_change):
        price = df["Current_Price_USD"] * (1 + price_change)
        units = df["Historical_Daily_Sales_Units"] * (1 + elasticity * price_change)
        return round(((price - df["Unit_Cost_USD"]) * units).sum(), 2)

    profit_curve = {}
    for change in [-0.10, -0.05, 0.0, 0.05, 0.10]:
        profit_curve[f"{change * 100:+.0f}%"] = simulate(change)

    # 3. Output: daily gross profit for the whole catalog at each change
    for label, profit in profit_curve.items():
        print(f"{label}: ${profit:,.2f}")
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `elasticity = -1.51` uses the value measured in Micro-Example 1 for every product. In a real business, each product group would get its own test.
      * `simulate(price_change)` computes the new price, the new units (section 7.3), and the total daily gross profit of the whole catalog.
      * The loop runs the function for 5 price changes and stores each result in a dictionary, labeled like `+5%`.
      * `f"{change * 100:+.0f}%"` writes the change with its sign: `+5%`, `-10%`.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      -10%: $6,881.85
      -5%: $7,163.83
      +0%: $7,342.85
      +5%: $7,418.90
      +10%: $7,391.98
      ```

      * Profit rises from `-10`% up to `+5`%, then falls again at `+10`%: there is a best point in between.
      * A 5% rise adds about `$76` a day across the catalog. The margins are high (above 52%), so the profit on the remaining units outweighs the units lost.
  * *Quick Check 2 (`max(..., key=...)`):*
    * New tool: `max(profit_curve, key=profit_curve.get)` returns the label with the highest profit. Why: finds the best scenario without reading the list by eye.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print("Best change:", ___(profit_curve, key=profit_curve.get))`
    * Check: the Terminal shows `Best change: +5%`.
  * *Micro-Example 3 (Which Products Will Not Sell Out in 12 Days?):* `YourName_S43_demo_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the catalog
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_03_dataset.csv")
    season_days = 12

    # 2. Process: days of stock and our price gap to the competitor
    df["Days_Of_Stock"] = (
        df["Inventory_Units_Count"] / df["Historical_Daily_Sales_Units"]
    ).round(1)
    df["Price_Gap_Pct"] = (
        (df["Current_Price_USD"] - df["Competitor_Price_USD"])
        / df["Competitor_Price_USD"] * 100
    ).round(1)
    at_risk = df[df["Days_Of_Stock"] > season_days]

    # 3. Output: the products that need action
    print(at_risk[["Product_ID", "Days_Of_Stock", "Price_Gap_Pct"]])
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `Days_Of_Stock` divides inventory by daily sales (section 7.4).
      * `Price_Gap_Pct` compares our price with the competitor's: negative means we are cheaper.
      * `df[df["Days_Of_Stock"] > season_days]` keeps the products that will not sell out in 12 days.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
        Product_ID  Days_Of_Stock  Price_Gap_Pct
      0     PRD-200           14.1           -3.9
      1     PRD-201           13.6            2.5
      2     PRD-202           13.2           -2.8
      9     PRD-209           13.0            3.7
      10    PRD-210           12.5           -1.3
      11    PRD-211           13.3            3.2
      ```

      * 6 of 12 products would still have stock at the end of the season.
      * `PRD-201`, `PRD-209`, and `PRD-211` are also more expensive than the competitor: a price cut there has two good reasons.
      * `PRD-200` is already cheaper than the competitor, yet still too slow: its stock is simply too large for the season.
  * *Quick Check 3 (`np.where()`):*
    * New tool: `np.where(condition, value_if_true, value_if_false)` fills a whole column with one of two values. Why: a one-line alternative to setting a default and overwriting it with `.loc[]`.
    * Task: add `import numpy as np` to part **1. Input**, and one line at the end of part **2. Process**: `df["Action"] = np._____(df["Days_Of_Stock"] > season_days, "Markdown", "Keep")`. Then print `df["Action"].value_counts()`.
    * Check: `Keep` `6`, `Markdown` `6`.

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Catalog Profit Curve:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S43_pricing.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S43_pricing.py`).
  * *Step 2: Simulate a Wider Range of Price Changes:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the catalog and the measured elasticity
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_03_dataset.csv")
    elasticity = -1.51

    # 2. Process: today's unit economics, then a profit curve from -15% to +15%
    df["Unit_Margin_USD"] = df["Current_Price_USD"] - df["Unit_Cost_USD"]
    df["Gross_Margin_Pct"] = (df["Unit_Margin_USD"] / df["Current_Price_USD"] * 100).round(1)
    rows = []
    for change in [-0.15, -0.10, -0.05, 0.0, 0.05, 0.10, 0.15]:
        price = df["Current_Price_USD"] * (1 + change)
        units = df["Historical_Daily_Sales_Units"] * (1 + elasticity * change)
        rows.append({
            "Price_Change_Pct": round(change * 100),
            "Daily_Units": round(units.sum(), 1),
            "Daily_Revenue_USD": round((price * units).sum(), 2),
            "Daily_Gross_Profit_USD": round(((price - df["Unit_Cost_USD"]) * units).sum(), 2),
        })
    curve = pd.DataFrame(rows)

    # 3. Output: margins today, then the curve
    print("Gross margin % by product:", df["Gross_Margin_Pct"].tolist())
    print(curve)
    ```
  * *Step 3: Interpret:* In two comment lines, explain why the price change with the highest revenue is not the one with the highest profit.
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:* Run `python YourName_S43_pricing.py` in Panel `[4]` and confirm return code `0`.
  * *Success Check:* gross margins range from `52.5`% to `55.0`%. Gross profit peaks at `+5` (`$7,418.90`), while revenue is highest at `-15` (`$14,218.32`): the change that brings in the most money is not the one that keeps the most. Cutting prices by 15% sells the most units but earns the least profit (`$6,496.89`).

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Markdowns That Clear the Stock:**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In `YourName_S43_practice.py`, calculate the price cut each at-risk product needs to sell out in 12 days.
  * *Task 1 (Required Price Change):* For the products with more than 12 days of stock (Micro-Example 3):
    * `Required_Daily_Units` = `Inventory_Units_Count` / 12
    * `Price_Change_Pct` = (`Required_Daily_Units` / `Historical_Daily_Sales_Units` - 1) / elasticity x 100, rounded to 1 decimal. It is negative: a cut.
    * This reverses the rule in section 4: if units must rise by a given share, the price must change by that share divided by the elasticity.
  * *Task 2 (Read the SyntaxError & AI Diagnosis):* Write the new price line with a missing bracket: `df["New_Price_USD"] = df["Current_Price_USD"] * (1 + df["Price_Change_Pct"] / 100`. Run the script; Python stops before running anything with `SyntaxError: '(' was never closed`. Capture the message with `Win + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and ask where the bracket is missing.
  * *Task 3 (Fix and Export):* Close the bracket, round `New_Price_USD` to 2 decimals, and save `Product_ID`, `Current_Price_USD`, `Price_Change_Pct`, and `New_Price_USD` for the at-risk products to `data/markdown_plan.csv` with `index=False`.
  * *Success Check:* price changes: `PRD-200` `-11.4`%, `PRD-201` `-8.7`%, `PRD-202` `-6.5`%, `PRD-209` `-5.5`%, `PRD-210` `-2.8`%, and `PRD-211` `-7.4`%. The deepest cut goes to the product with the most stock left relative to its sales, not to the most expensive one.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - The Best Season Price for Each Product:**
  * *Goal:* Find, for each product, the price that earns the most over the 12-day season, counting the loss on any stock left over. Some prices may go up and some down. Show the manager how much the plan adds compared with keeping today's prices.
  * *What you need:*
    * File: `data/session_04_03_dataset.csv` (described in **5. Lab Practice Dataset**).
    * Script name: `YourName_S43_challenge.py`.
    * Tools you already know: functions, `for` loops over rows and price changes, `min()`, `max()`, lists of dictionaries, and `pd.DataFrame()`.
  * *Rules of the season:*
    * Season length: `12` days; elasticity: `-1.51`; daily units at a new price: units x (1 + elasticity x change).
    * Units sold in the season: the smaller of (daily units x 12) and the inventory: you cannot sell stock you do not have.
    * Unsold units go to a liquidator at 40% of unit cost, a loss of `0.6 x unit cost` each.
    * Season profit = (price - unit cost) x units sold - 0.6 x unit cost x unsold units.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. Write `season_profit(price, unit_cost, daily_units, inventory)` that returns the season profit, following the rules above.
    2. For each product, try the price changes `-20%`, `-15%`, `-10%`, `-5%`, `0%`, `+5%`, `+10%`, `+15%`, and `+20%`. Round each new price to 2 decimals before you use it.
    3. Keep, for each product, the change with the highest season profit, and also record the season profit at `0%` (today's price).
    4. Build one DataFrame with `Product_ID`, `Best_Change_Pct`, `Best_Price_USD`, `Best_Season_Profit_USD`, and `Current_Season_Profit_USD`, profits rounded to 2 decimals.
    5. Print the table, the total of both profit columns, and the difference.
    6. Count how many products get a price cut, how many a price rise, and how many keep today's price.
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * Looping over rows with `for _, row in df.iterrows():`. Each `row` behaves like a small dictionary: `row["Unit_Cost_USD"]`. The `_` holds the row label, which we do not need.
    * `min(a, b)` returns the smaller of two numbers.
  * *Hints:*
    * Test `season_profit()` on `PRD-200` at `0%` first: it should return about `9665.16`, because 66 units are left over.
    * Inside the product loop, keep the best result so far in two variables and update them when a change earns more.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Best changes: cuts for `PRD-200` (`-10`%), `PRD-201`, `PRD-202`, `PRD-209`, and `PRD-211` (`-5`% each); rises of `+5`% for `PRD-203`, `PRD-205`, `PRD-206`, and `PRD-208`; no change for `PRD-204`, `PRD-207`, and `PRD-210`.
    * Total season profit: `$85,969.93` with the plan against `$83,590.57` at today's prices: `$2,379.36` more.
    * Business meaning: one rule for all products leaves money behind. Products with too much stock need a cut to avoid leftover losses; products that will sell out anyway can carry a higher price. Note that for `PRD-210`, a cut that clears all stock earns less than keeping the price and selling 2 leftover units to the liquidator.

<a id="session-04-04"></a>
### Session 4.4: Ethical Considerations in Artificial Intelligence: Algorithmic Bias, Regulatory Compliance, and AI Governance

![Session 4.4 Map](assets/session_04_04_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_04_04_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_04_04_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 16 (115/12/27 - 116/01/02) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)

* **1. Session Purpose:**
  * A consumer lender approves loan applications automatically when its model's approval score is `0.70` or higher
  * Before the model's annual review, the board's risk committee must confirm that it treats applicant groups fairly and that every rejection can be explained to the applicant
  * The chief risk officer asks: does our credit model treat applicant groups fairly, can we explain each rejection, and what should the board decide? Today, you audit the model for fairness, explain its decisions, and prepare the facts the board needs

* **2. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "Are the groups treated alike?": measure approval rates by group and the disparate impact ratio, and test it against the four-fifths rule
  2. "Why was this applicant rejected?": explain each decision with feature contributions (how much each feature pushed the score up or down)
  3. "What should the board decide?": compare one shared threshold's fairness and risk, write a governance record, and fix a `NameError` caused by a variable that only exists inside a function

* **3. Business Context: Fairness, Explanations, and Accountability:**
  * A model learns patterns from past data. If the past was unfair, for example because some groups had less access to credit, the model can repeat that unfairness at scale, without anyone intending it.
  * Two kinds of unfair treatment:
    * **Disparate treatment:** using a person's group directly in the decision, such as a different threshold for each group. In lending, this is prohibited in many countries, including under the US Equal Credit Opportunity Act (ECOA).
    * **Disparate impact:** a rule that treats everyone the same on paper, but approves one group far less often. It is a warning sign that must be investigated and justified.
  * The four-fifths rule: if one group's approval rate is less than 80% (four-fifths) of the most-approved group's rate, the gap needs review. It comes from US employment guidelines (1978) and is widely borrowed as a first screening test. Failing it is a reason to investigate, not proof of discrimination.
  * Explanations are required: US lenders must give rejected applicants the main reasons (an adverse action notice), and the EU AI Act (European Union Artificial Intelligence Act) treats credit scoring of people as high-risk, with duties for documentation, human oversight, and transparency.
  * Governance: the board stays accountable for automated decisions. It needs a written record of what was tested, what was found, and who decided what.

* **4. Data Basics: Three Fairness Numbers:**
  * Approval rate of a group = approved applicants in the group / all applicants in the group.
  * Disparate impact ratio = the lower group's approval rate / the higher group's approval rate. `1.0` means equal rates; below `0.80` fails the four-fifths rule.
  * Feature contribution (for a model that adds up features, like linear regression) = coefficient x (applicant's value - average value). A negative contribution pulled the score down. For such a model, this number is also the feature's SHAP value (SHapley Additive exPlanations, a widely used explanation method).
  * Our approval scores come from a model whose formula we were not given. We fit a linear regression that copies it from the scores (a surrogate model) and explain that copy. It reproduces the scores well (`R-squared` `0.975`), so its explanations are trustworthy for this model.

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * File Link: [data/session_04_04_dataset.csv](data/session_04_04_dataset.csv)
  * Audited 16-row loan application dataset (fictional teaching data), one row per applicant, 8 in each group:
    * `Applicant_ID`: Applicant code (e.g., `APP-6001`).
    * `Demographic_Group`: An anonymized protected group, `Group_A` or `Group_B`. A protected group is defined by a characteristic such as race, sex, or age that the law forbids using against an applicant. The model does not use this column; auditors use it only to test fairness.
    * `Credit_Score_Count`: Credit score from a credit bureau (a company that records how people repay debts); higher is better.
    * `Debt_To_Income_Pct`: Monthly debt payments as a share of monthly income (DTI); lower is safer.
    * `Algorithmic_Approval_Score`: The model's score from 0 to 1; approved at `0.70` or higher.
    * `Adverse_Action_Notice_Flag`: `1` if the applicant was rejected and must receive an adverse action notice, else `0`.

* **6. Keywords (Core Programming Concepts):**
  * `Disparate Impact`:
    * What: a rule that approves one group much less often than another, even though it never uses the group
    * Why: the most common way bias enters automated decisions
  * `Disparate Impact Ratio` and the `Four-Fifths Rule`:
    * What: the lower approval rate divided by the higher one, tested against `0.80`
    * Why: a simple, widely used first test of fairness
  * `Disparate Treatment`:
    * What: deciding differently because of a person's group
    * Why: prohibited in lending in many countries, so it can never be the fix
  * `Explainability` (feature contributions, SHAP):
    * What: showing how much each feature pushed a decision up or down
    * Why: applicants, regulators, and managers need reasons, not just scores
  * `Surrogate Model`:
    * What: a simple model fitted to copy a model whose formula is hidden
    * Why: makes a hidden model explainable, as long as the copy is accurate
  * `AI Governance`:
    * What: the rules, records, and people that keep AI decisions accountable
    * Why: the board, not the model, answers for the decisions

* **7. Core Programming & Computational Foundations:**
  * **7.1 Approval rates by group: the average of a 0/1 column**
    * *The idea:* Fairness checks start with a simple question: what share of each group was approved? If approved applicants are marked `1` and rejected ones `0`, the average of that column is the share of `1`s, so `df.groupby("Demographic_Group")["Approved"].mean()` gives each group's approval rate in one line.
    * *See it on real data:* threshold `0.70`.

      | Group | Applicants | Approved | Approval rate |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | `Group_A` | `8` | `7` | `0.875` |
      | `Group_B` | `8` | `2` | `0.250` |

    * *Common mistake:* counting approvals instead of rates. If one group had 80 applicants and another 8, raw counts would hide the comparison; rates put groups of any size on the same scale.
    * *More examples:*

      | Code | Result | What it tells you |
      |---|---|---|
      | `df["Approved"].mean()` | `0.5625` | 9 of all 16 applicants approved |
      | `df.groupby("Demographic_Group")["Approved"].sum()` | A `7`, B `2` | approvals per group |
      | `df.groupby("Demographic_Group")["Approved"].count()` | A `8`, B `8` | applicants per group |
      | rate = sum / count | A `0.875`, B `0.250` | the same rates as `.mean()` |

    * *Remember:* the mean of a 0/1 column is a rate.
  * **7.2 The disparate impact ratio and the four-fifths rule**
    * *The idea:* Divide the lower approval rate by the higher one. `1.0` means equal treatment in outcomes; the lower the number, the bigger the gap. Under the four-fifths rule, a ratio below `0.80` is a warning that must be investigated.
    * *See it on real data:* `0.250 / 0.875 = 0.286`. To pass, Group B would need an approval rate of at least `0.80 x 0.875 = 0.70`: 6 of its 8 applicants instead of 2.
    * *Common mistake:* treating the rule as a verdict. Failing it does not prove discrimination, and passing it does not prove fairness. It tells you where to look.
    * *More examples:* the ratio at other shared thresholds (Exercise 3 computes the full table).

      | Threshold | Group A rate | Group B rate | Ratio | Passes? |
      |---|---|---|---|---|
      | `0.45` | `1.000` | `1.000` | `1.000` | yes |
      | `0.50` | `1.000` | `0.875` | `0.875` | yes |
      | `0.60` | `1.000` | `0.500` | `0.500` | no |
      | `0.70` | `0.875` | `0.250` | `0.286` | no |
      | `0.75` | `0.875` | `0.000` | `0.000` | no |

    * *Remember:* lower rate / higher rate; below `0.80`, investigate.
  * **7.3 Where a gap comes from: the features**
    * *The idea:* Our model never sees the group column. But if the groups differ on the features the model uses, its scores and approvals will differ too. This is how disparate impact appears without anyone intending it.
    * *See it on real data:*

      | Group average | Group A | Group B |
      |---|---|---|
      | Credit score | `730.62` | `670.00` |
      | Debt-to-income (DTI) | `26.58`% | `36.05`% |
      | Approval score | `0.86` | `0.60` |
      | Lowest and highest score | `0.66` to `0.94` | `0.49` to `0.72` |

    * *The real question:* do these feature differences reflect real repayment risk, or past unequal access to credit? A credit score depends partly on how long someone has had access to credit. Data alone cannot settle this; people must investigate.
    * *More examples:*

      | Measure | Group A | Group B |
      |---|---|---|
      | lowest and highest credit score | `690` to `760` | `640` to `705` |
      | share with DTI above 35% | `0.0` | `0.5` |
      | correlation of score with credit score (all 16) | `0.987` |  |
      | correlation of score with DTI (all 16) | `-0.982` |  |

    * *Remember:* a model can be unfair without using the group column; look at the features.
  * **7.4 Explaining one decision: feature contributions**
    * *The idea:* For a model that adds up its features, like linear regression, each feature's contribution is coefficient x (applicant's value - average value). The contributions show how much each feature pushed this applicant's score above or below the average score. They add up exactly: average score + all contributions = the applicant's score.
    * *See it on real data:* our surrogate model has coefficients `0.00292` per credit point and `-0.00734` per DTI point; the averages are a credit score of `700.31`, a DTI of `31.31`%, and a score of `0.7275`.

      | Applicant | Credit effect | DTI effect | Average + effects | Decision |
      |---|---|---|---|---|
      | `APP-6007` (640, 41.2%) | `-0.176` | `-0.073` | `0.7275 - 0.176 - 0.073 = 0.479` | rejected |
      | `APP-6012` (690, 34.0%) | `-0.030` | `-0.020` | `0.678` | rejected, just below `0.70` |
      | `APP-6006` (760, 22.0%) | `+0.174` | `+0.068` | `0.970` | approved |

      For `APP-6007`, the low credit score is the main reason (`-0.176`), more than twice the effect of the high debt ratio.
    * *Common mistake:* explaining a model you have not checked. A surrogate's explanations are only as good as its fit; ours reproduces the scores with an R-squared of `0.975`.
    * *More examples:*

      | Applicant | Credit effect | DTI effect | Decision |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | `APP-6000` (720, 28.5%) | `+0.058` | `+0.021` | approved |
      | `APP-6005` (705, 29.5%) | `+0.014` | `+0.013` | approved, close to average |
      | `APP-6013` (700, 30.5%) | `-0.001` | `+0.006` | approved, almost exactly average |
      | `APP-6001` (680, 34.2%) | `-0.059` | `-0.021` | rejected: credit score is the main reason |

    * *Remember:* contribution = coefficient x (value - average); the largest negative one is the main reason.
  * **7.5 Fair process: what a fix may and may not do**
    * *The idea:* When a model fails a fairness test, the fix itself must be lawful and responsible.

      | Possible fix | Allowed? | Why |
      |---|---|---|
      | A lower threshold for one group | no | deciding by group is disparate treatment, prohibited in lending in many countries |
      | One lower threshold for everyone | only with care | Exercise 3 shows it approves applicants above the risk limit |
      | Investigate whether the features measure risk equally well for both groups | yes | addresses the cause |
      | Add data such as rent and utility payment history | yes | gives applicants with thin credit records a fair hearing |
      | Human review of rejections near the threshold, documented | yes | a person checks the borderline cases |

    * *More examples:*

      | A governance record states | Example from this session |
      |---|---|
      | the fairness test and its result | ratio `0.286`, fails the four-fifths rule |
      | the threshold in use | `0.70`, the lender's current approval rule |
      | the explanation method and its fit | surrogate model, R-squared `0.975` |
      | the actions decided and their owners | feature investigation; human review near the threshold |

    * *Remember:* never decide by group; fix the cause, add oversight, and document every decision.

* **8. Development Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE:**
  * `[1]` File Explorer: create your script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S44_demo_1.py`), and check the dataset is in `data/`.
  * `[2]` Code Editor: write a few lines, run, repeat. Small steps keep errors small.
  * `[3]` AI Agent Chat: on an error, capture the traceback (Python's error report) with `Win + Shift + S` (macOS: `Cmd + Shift + 4`), paste it with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why it failed.
  * `[4]` Terminal: run `python` followed by your script name. Return code `0` means the script finished with no errors.

* **9. Hands-On Coding Micro-Examples:**
  * Every example has three parts: **1. Input**, **2. Process**, and **3. Output**. Name each file with your own name and the session code, for example `Reacher_S44_demo_1.py` (pattern: `YourName_S44_demo_1.py`).
  * *Micro-Example 1 (Approval Rates and the Four-Fifths Rule):* `YourName_S44_demo_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the 16 applications
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_04_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: decisions at 0.70, approval rate per group, and the ratio
    df["Approved"] = (df["Algorithmic_Approval_Score"] >= 0.70).astype(int)
    rates = df.groupby("Demographic_Group")["Approved"].mean()
    ratio = rates.min() / rates.max()

    # 3. Output: rates, ratio, and the four-fifths test
    print(rates)
    print(f"Disparate impact ratio: {ratio:.3f}")
    print("Passes the four-fifths rule:", ratio >= 0.80)
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `(df["Algorithmic_Approval_Score"] >= 0.70).astype(int)` turns each score into `1` (approved) or `0` (rejected).
      * `df.groupby("Demographic_Group")["Approved"].mean()` gives each group's approval rate (section 7.1).
      * `rates.min() / rates.max()` divides the lower rate by the higher, whichever group it is (section 7.2).
      * `ratio >= 0.80` gives `True` or `False`: the four-fifths test.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Demographic_Group
      Group_A    0.875
      Group_B    0.250
      Name: Approved, dtype: float64
      Disparate impact ratio: 0.286
      Passes the four-fifths rule: False
      ```

      * 7 of 8 Group A applicants are approved, but only 2 of 8 in Group B.
      * The ratio, `0.286`, is far below `0.80`: the gap must be investigated before the model's next review.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`.map()`):*
    * New tool: `column.map({1: "Approved", 0: "Rejected"})` replaces each value using a dictionary. Why: tables for the board should use words, not codes.
    * Task: add two lines to part **3. Output**: `df["Decision"] = df["Approved"].___({1: "Approved", 0: "Rejected"})` and `print(pd.crosstab(df["Demographic_Group"], df["Decision"]))`
    * Check: Group A: `7` approved, `1` rejected; Group B: `2` approved, `6` rejected.
  * *Micro-Example 2 (Where Does the Gap Come From?):* `YourName_S44_demo_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the 16 applications
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_04_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: average features and score by group
    profile = df.groupby("Demographic_Group")[
        ["Credit_Score_Count", "Debt_To_Income_Pct", "Algorithmic_Approval_Score"]
    ].mean().round(2)

    # 3. Output: the two group profiles
    print(profile)
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `df.groupby("Demographic_Group")[[...]].mean()` averages the two features and the score in each group (section 7.3).
      * The group column is used here only by the auditor, to compare groups; the model never sees it.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
                         Credit_Score_Count  ...  Algorithmic_Approval_Score
      Demographic_Group                      ...
      Group_A                        730.62  ...                        0.86
      Group_B                        670.00  ...                        0.60
      ```

      * The `...` hides the debt-to-income column: Group A `26.58`%, Group B `36.05`%. Quick Check 2 shows the full table.
      * Group B has lower credit scores and higher debt ratios on average, so it gets lower approval scores. The gap in approvals follows from the gap in features.
  * *Quick Check 2 (`.T`):*
    * New tool: `table.T` flips a table so that rows become columns. Why: with many measures and few groups, the flipped table is easier to read in a report.
    * Task: in part **3. Output**, change the line to `print(profile._)`
    * Check: the measures are now rows and `Group_A` and `Group_B` are the two columns.
  * *Micro-Example 3 (Explain Each Decision):* `YourName_S44_demo_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the 16 applications and the two features
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_04_dataset.csv")
    features = ["Credit_Score_Count", "Debt_To_Income_Pct"]

    # 2. Process: copy the score with a linear model, then contributions
    surrogate = LinearRegression().fit(df[features], df["Algorithmic_Approval_Score"])
    contributions = (df[features] - df[features].mean()) * surrogate.coef_
    contributions.columns = ["Credit_Score_Effect", "Debt_Ratio_Effect"]
    report = pd.concat([df[["Applicant_ID", "Algorithmic_Approval_Score"]],
                        contributions.round(3)], axis=1)

    # 3. Output: how well the copy fits, then the explanations
    print("Surrogate R-squared:", round(surrogate.score(df[features], df["Algorithmic_Approval_Score"]), 3))
    print(report[df["Algorithmic_Approval_Score"] < 0.70])
    ```
    * *How it works, line by line:*
      * `LinearRegression().fit(df[features], df["Algorithmic_Approval_Score"])` fits a simple model that copies the scores from the two features: the surrogate (section 7.4).
      * `(df[features] - df[features].mean()) * surrogate.coef_` computes every applicant's contribution for both features at once: each column's average is subtracted, and each column is multiplied by its own coefficient.
      * `contributions.columns = [...]` gives the two results readable names.
      * `pd.concat([...], axis=1)` puts the IDs, scores, and contributions side by side.
      * `report[df["Algorithmic_Approval_Score"] < 0.70]` keeps the rejected applicants only.
    * *Read the output:*

      ```text
      Surrogate R-squared: 0.975
         Applicant_ID  ...  Debt_Ratio_Effect
      1      APP-6001  ...             -0.021
      3      APP-6003  ...             -0.053
      7      APP-6007  ...             -0.073
      ...
      15     APP-6015  ...             -0.067
      ```

      * The surrogate explains `97.5`% of the score differences, so its explanations can be trusted.
      * All 7 rejected applicants have negative effects. The hidden `Credit_Score_Effect` column is larger in every case, so the credit score is the main reason in each adverse action notice (Quick Check 3).
  * *Quick Check 3 (`.idxmin(axis=1)`):*
    * New tool: `contributions.idxmin(axis=1)` returns, for each row, the column with the most negative value. Why: that column is the main reason to print on the adverse action notice.
    * Task: add one line at the end of part **2. Process**: `report["Main_Reason"] = contributions.______(axis=1)`
    * Check: the rejected applicants all show `Credit_Score_Effect` as the main reason.

  * [US Consumer Financial Protection Bureau: Equal Credit Opportunity Act (Regulation B)](https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1002/).
