  * [Barocas, Hardt, Narayanan: Fairness and Machine Learning: Limitations and Opportunities](https://fairmlbook.org/).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Fairness Audit Report:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S44_governance.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S44_governance.py`).
  * *Step 2: Audit the Current Threshold:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the 16 applications
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_04_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: decisions, rates, ratio, and a check of the notice flags
    df["Approved"] = (df["Algorithmic_Approval_Score"] >= 0.70).astype(int)
    rates = df.groupby("Demographic_Group")["Approved"].mean()
    ratio = rates.min() / rates.max()
    notices_match = ((1 - df["Approved"]) == df["Adverse_Action_Notice_Flag"]).all()

    # 3. Output: the audit findings
    print(f"Group A approval: {rates['Group_A'] * 100:.1f}% | "
          f"Group B approval: {rates['Group_B'] * 100:.1f}%")
    print(f"Disparate impact ratio: {ratio:.3f}")
    if ratio < 0.80:
        print("ALERT: fails the four-fifths rule; investigate before the next review.")
    else:
        print("Passes the four-fifths rule.")
    print("Every rejected applicant is flagged for a notice:", notices_match)
    ```
    * `1 - df["Approved"]` turns approved (`1`) into `0` and rejected (`0`) into `1`, matching the notice flag.
  * *Step 3: Interpret:* In two comment lines, explain why failing the four-fifths rule calls for an investigation, and why it does not by itself prove the model discriminates.
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:* Run `python YourName_S44_governance.py` in Panel `[4]` and confirm return code `0`.
  * *Success Check:* `Group A approval: 87.5% | Group B approval: 25.0%`; ratio `0.286`; the alert line prints; `Every rejected applicant is flagged for a notice: True`.

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - A Reusable Audit Function and a Governance Record:**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In `YourName_S44_practice.py`, turn the audit into a function and save a governance record for the board.
  * *Task 1 (Audit Function):* Write `approval_audit(df, threshold)` that computes the approval rates and `ratio` at any threshold, exactly as in Exercise 1.
  * *Task 2 (Read the NameError & AI Diagnosis):* Call `approval_audit(df, 0.70)`, then write `print(ratio)` on the next line, outside the function. The script stops with `NameError: name 'ratio' is not defined`. Capture the traceback with `Win + Shift + S`, paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat) with `Ctrl + V`, and ask why a variable made inside a function cannot be seen outside it.
  * *Task 3 (Return, Record, Save):* Make the function end with `return rates, ratio`, and call it as `rates, ratio = approval_audit(df, 0.70)`. Build a one-row governance record with `pd.DataFrame([{...}])` and the keys `Model`, `Threshold`, `Group_A_Rate`, `Group_B_Rate`, `Disparate_Impact_Ratio`, `Passes_Four_Fifths`, and `Status`, where `Model` is `"Credit_Approval_Model_v1"` and `Status` is `"Board review required"` if the test fails. Save it to `data/board_ai_governance_audit.csv` with `index=False`, and print a 3-bullet memo to the board risk committee: finding, evidence, and requested decision.
  * *Success Check:* the record shows `Group_A_Rate` `0.875`, `Group_B_Rate` `0.25`, `Disparate_Impact_Ratio` `0.286`, `Passes_Four_Fifths` `False`, and `Status` `Board review required`; the script ends with return code `0`.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - Can One Shared Threshold Fix the Gap?:**
  * *Goal:* A different threshold for each group would be disparate treatment, so it is not an option. Test whether a **single threshold for everyone** can pass the four-fifths rule, and what it would cost in credit risk. Prepare the trade-off for the board.
  * *What you need:*
    * File: `data/session_04_04_dataset.csv` (described in **5. Lab Practice Dataset**).
    * Script name: `YourName_S44_challenge.py`.
    * Tools you already know: your `approval_audit()` function from Exercise 2, `for` loops, lists of dictionaries, `pd.DataFrame()`, and Boolean masks.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. For each shared threshold in `[0.45, 0.50, 0.55, 0.60, 0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80, 0.85]`, compute: the number approved, the approval rate of each group, the disparate impact ratio (rounded to 3 decimals), and `Risky_Approvals`: approved applicants with a DTI above `35`%, the lender's usual risk limit.
    2. Collect the results in one DataFrame with the columns `Threshold`, `Approved`, `Group_A_Rate`, `Group_B_Rate`, `DI_Ratio`, and `Risky_Approvals`.
    3. Find the **highest** threshold that passes the four-fifths rule, and report its `Risky_Approvals`.
    4. Find the **highest** threshold that passes the four-fifths rule **and** has `0` risky approvals. If there is none, print that clearly.
    5. Write three comment lines for the board: what the threshold can and cannot fix, and two other actions the board could request instead.
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * Combining two masks: `approved & (df["Debt_To_Income_Pct"] > 35)` is `True` for applicants who are approved **and** above the risk limit; `.sum()` counts them.
    * Filtering a results table with two conditions: `results[(results["DI_Ratio"] >= 0.80) & (results["Risky_Approvals"] == 0)]`. An empty result has `len()` of `0`.
    * Guarding a division: if a group has no approvals at all, its rate is `0`, and the ratio is `0`; check `rates.max() > 0` before dividing.
  * *Hints:*
    * Reuse `approval_audit()` inside the loop, so every threshold is tested the same way.
    * Print the full table before answering requirements 3 and 4; the pattern is easier to see.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Ratios: `0.45` `1.000`, `0.50` `0.875`, `0.55` `0.625`, `0.60` `0.500`, `0.65` `0.250`, `0.70` `0.286`, and `0.000` from `0.75` upward.
    * Highest passing threshold: `0.50`, with 15 of 16 applicants approved and `3` risky approvals (DTI above 35%).
    * No threshold passes the rule with `0` risky approvals.
    * Business meaning: moving one threshold either fails the fairness test or approves applicants above the lender's risk limit. The threshold cannot fix the gap, because the gap comes from the features themselves. The board should instead ask for an investigation of the features (do credit scores and DTI measure real repayment risk equally well for both groups?), consider additional data such as rent and utility payment history, and require human review of rejected applications near the threshold, with every decision documented.
